Geometric optics approximation for the Einstein vacuum equations
Arthur Touati

Abstract
We show the stability of the geometric optics approximation in general relativity by constructing a family ( g λ ) λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] (g_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]} of high-frequency metrics solutions to the Einstein vacuum equations in 3+1 dimensions without any symmetry assumptions. In the limit λ → 0 \lambda\to 0 this family approaches a fixed background g 0 g_{0} solution of the Einstein-null dust system, illustrating the backreaction phenomenon. We introduce a precise second order high-frequency ansatz and identify a generalised wave gauge as well as polarization conditions at each order. The validity of our ansatz is ensured by a weak polarized null condition satisfied by the quadratic non-linearity in the Ricci tensor. The Einstein vacuum equations for g λ g_{\lambda} are recast as a hierarchy of transport and wave equations, and their coupling induces a loss of derivatives. In order to solve it, we take advantage of a null foliation associated to g 0 g_{0} as well as a Fourier cut-off adapted to our ansatz. The construction of high-frequency initial data solving the constraint equations is the content of our companion paper [ Tou23b ] .



1
Introduction

1.1
Presentation of the result

In the present article, we are interested in oscillating solutions of the Einstein vacuum equations

\displaystyle R_{\alpha\beta}(g)=0,

(1.1)

where
R_{\alpha\beta}(g)
is the Ricci tensor of the Lorentzian metric
g
defined on a manifold
\mathcal{M}
. Our main goal is to extend Choquet-Bruhat’s construction from
[
CB69
]
in order to fully justifies the geometric optics approximation for (
1.1
). Choquet-Bruhat constructs approximate oscillating vacuum spacetimes, i.e a family
(g_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
of metrics oscillating at frequency
\lambda^{-1}
satisfying
R_{\alpha\beta}(g_{\lambda})=O\left(\lambda\right)
. We improve this result by constructing a similar family satisfying
R_{\alpha\beta}(g_{\lambda})=0
. We give a rough version of our main result.

Theorem 1.1

(Rough version of Theorem
2.2
)
.

There exists a family of metrics
(g_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
of the form

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}=g_{0}+\lambda g^{(1)}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)+O\left(\lambda^{2}\right)

(1.2)

solving (
1.1
) in generalised wave gauge on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
where
g_{0}
is a given background metric close to Minkowski,
u_{0}
a solution of
g_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})=0
and
g^{(1)}
is a periodic tensor in the variable
\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}
.

In addition to a more refined high-frequency ansatz compared to the one of
[
CB69
]
, we need to choose a gauge in order to deal with the invariance by diffeomorphism of (
1.1
). We choose to work in a generalised wave gauge, i.e we rewrite (
1.1
) as a system of quasi-linear wave equations for the metric coefficients. The initial data for this system are given by our companion paper
[
Tou23b
]
which provides oscillating solutions of the constraint equations on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

As it can be seen on (
1.2
), the sequence
(g_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
constructed in this article illustrates the backreaction phenomenon, i.e it converges weakly to a non-vacuum spacetime in the high-frequency limit
\lambda\to 0
, denoted by
g_{0}
in Theorem
1.1
:

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\to g_{0},\quad\text{uniformly in $L^{\infty}$,}

(1.3)

\displaystyle\partial g_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\partial g_{0},\quad\text{weakly in $L^{2}_{loc}$.}

The background metric
g_{0}
is a generic solution of the Einstein-null dust system

\left\{\begin{aligned} R_{\alpha\beta}(g_{0})&=F_{0}^{2}\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0},\\
g_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})&=0,\\
2g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}u_{0}\partial_{\sigma}F_{0}+(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0})F_{0}&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Therefore, our result is in agreement with Burnett’s conjecture from
[
Bur89
]
, which states that the closure of the set of vacuum spacetimes for the topology associated with the convergence (
1.3
) identifies with the set of kinetic spacetimes solving the massless Einstein-Vlasov system.

From the rough ansatz (
1.2
), we can also deduce that Theorem
1.1
is a low-regularity existence result for the Einstein vacuum equations. Indeed, by differentiating (
1.2
) twice, we obtain
\left\|g_{\lambda}\right\|_{H^{2}}\sim\lambda^{-1}
. This implies that a direct application of the best existence result for (
1.1
), the celebrated bounded
L^{2}
curvature theorem from
[
KRS15
]
, to the oscillating data provided by
[
Tou23b
]
would only give a time of existence of order
\lambda
. Therefore, our result requires a specific construction exploiting the structure of the high-frequency ansatz (
1.2
). Such a construction allows us to show existence on a time interval uniform in
\lambda
.

The remainder of this introduction discusses further the main two aspects of our result.

•

In Section
1.2
, we present the standard strategy of geometric optics and describes briefly the new challenges posed by the semi-linear terms and the gauge freedom of (
1.1
). We also discuss in depth the construction of
[
CB69
]
.

•

In Section
1.3
, we compare our result with the state of the art on Burnett’s conjecture.

1.2
Geometric optics

From a physical point of view, the strategy of geometric optics aims at describing the appearance of rays from wave propagation. The word ”optics” reminds us of the very first motivations of physicists to describe the propagation of light from the Maxwell equations. From a mathematical point of view, geometric optics aims at constructing oscillating solutions to non-linear hyperbolic system of equations. We refer to
[
Rau12
]
and
[
Mé09
]
for two very rich presentations of the field of geometric optics.

1.2.1
Towards weakly non-linear geometric optics and transparency

Consider a generic first order non-linear hyperbolic system

\displaystyle A_{0}(u)\partial_{t}u+\sum_{i=1}^{d}A_{i}(u)\partial_{i}u

\displaystyle=F(u),

(1.4)

where
u:\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
is the unknown,
A_{0}
is a positive definite
N\times N
matrix, the
A_{i}
’s are symmetric
N\times N
matrices, and
F
is a
\mathbb{R}^{N}
-valued function. One can wonder what happens to oscillating initial data of the form

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}(0,x)=\exp\left(i\frac{\varphi_{0}(x)}{\lambda}\right)f_{0}(x),

where
f_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
is the initial amplitude,
\varphi_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a scalar function and
\lambda>0
is a small wavelength. In the case where (
1.4
) is linear, i.e when
A_{0}
and the
A_{i}
’s don’t depend on
u
and
F
is a linear function of
u
, this question has been settled in
[
Lax57
]
. There, it is shown that the solution
u_{\lambda}
can be written as a so called WKB ansatz

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}=\exp\left(i\frac{\varphi}{\lambda}\right)\left(f^{(0)}+\lambda f^{(1)}+\lambda^{2}f^{(2)}+\cdots\right),

(1.5)

where the phase
\varphi
satisfies the eikonal equation
\det\left(A_{0}\partial_{t}\varphi+\sum_{i=1}^{d}A_{i}\partial_{i}\varphi\right)=0
, and each
f^{(i)}
satisfies a polarization condition and is transported along the rays, i.e along the level surfaces of
\varphi
. The whole point of geometric optics is to understand what survives when one goes to the non-linear system. In this case, one expects the waves to interact and to create harmonics. Therefore, a more refined ansatz than (
1.5
) is required

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}\sim\lambda^{p}\sum_{n\geq 0}\lambda^{n}U_{n}\left(t,x,\frac{\varphi(t,x)}{\lambda}\right),

where the profiles
U_{n}(t,x,\theta)
are periodic in
\theta
. The prefactor
\lambda^{p}
measures the amplitude of the oscillations, and the value of
p
dictates the role played by the non-linear interaction. If
p
is large, the first profile is still linearly propagated. However if one decreases
p
, we reach the regime of weakly non-linear geometric optics when non-linear terms enter the transport equation for the first profile. We refer to
[
HK83
,
JR92
,
JMR93
]
for standard results on this regime. For quadratic interactions as in the Einstein vacuum equations, the threshold for this regime is
p=1
. Looking at (
1.2
), this article deals with the weakly non-linear regime for the Einstein vacuum equations.

For some systems, the interaction between two waves vanishes due to the particular structure of the non-linear terms: the transport equations are then linear, even in the weakly non-linear regime. This situation is known as transparency in the geometric optics literature, see
[
JMR00
]
. As discussed in depth in
[
Lan13
]
, transparency is closely related to the famous null condition for quadratic interaction introduced in
[
Chr86
,
Kla86
]
. As it is well known, the Einstein vacuum equations in wave gauge don’t satisfy the null condition but rather a weak form of it, which still plays a major role in both Choquet-Bruhat’s approximate construction and the present article’s exact construction.

1.2.2
Approximate geometric optics in general relativity

The application of geometric optics to general relativity and the Einstein vacuum equations is motivated by a potential non-linear description of gravitational waves. The standard description of gravitational waves is based on the linearized gravity setting, where one considers a metric of the form
g=m+h
and linearizes (
1.1
) around the Minkowski metric
m
. Assuming the Lorentz gauge and the TT-gauge, one can consider plane wave solutions to the linearized equations with two degrees of freedom corresponding to two polarizations. See Chapter 35 of
[
MTW73
]
for more details.

Though being historically crucial, linearized gravity fails to describe gravitational waves in a more physical context. First, the restriction to small perturbations
h
can’t, by definition, describe strong gravitational fields. Moreover, one cannot describe the energy of a gravitational wave since any quadratic expression in the perturbation
h
is neglected. This has the following consequence: the background spacetime on which the gravitational wave propagates doesn’t see the wave. As first shown in
[
CB69
]
, geometric optics provides a mathematical framework that goes beyond linearized gravity. Note that other averaging schemes have been considered in
[
BH64
,
Isa68a
,
Isa68b
]
to describe the energy of a gravitational wave.

We now describe the construction of
[
CB69
]
(see also Chapter 11 of
[
CB09
]
). The author considers WKB ansatz for the metric of the spacetime, i.e introduces a small parameter
\lambda>0
and construct
g_{\lambda}
of the form

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=g_{0}+\lambda g^{(1)}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)+\lambda^{2}g^{(2)}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)

(1.6)

satisfying
R_{\mu\nu}(g_{\lambda})=O\left(\lambda\right)
. As in the present article, the notation
g^{(i)}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
means that
g^{(i)}
are symmetric 2-tensor depending on an extra phase argument
\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}
. Since the Ricci tensor involves at most two derivatives of the metric, we have formally
R_{\mu\nu}(g_{\lambda})=\frac{1}{\lambda}R_{\mu\nu}^{(-1)}+R_{\mu\nu}^{(0)}+O\left(\lambda\right)
. The goal of Choquet-Bruhat’s construction is to identify conditions on
g_{0}
,
g^{(1)}
and
g^{(2)}
so that
R_{\mu\nu}^{(-1)}=R_{\mu\nu}^{(0)}=0
. These conditions are the following. To ensure
R_{\mu\nu}^{(-1)}=0
, one must have
g_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})=0
and the polarization condition

\displaystyle g_{0}^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}u_{0}g^{(1)}_{\alpha\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}g^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}\right)=0.

(1.7)

To ensure
R_{\mu\nu}^{(0)}=0
, one must have the following transport equation for
g^{(1)}

\displaystyle 2\partial^{\alpha}u_{0}\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}g^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}+(\mathbf{D}^{\alpha}\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}u_{0})g^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0.

(1.8)

Moreover, one needs
R_{\mu\nu}(g_{0})=\tau\partial_{\mu}u_{0}\partial_{\nu}u_{0}
with
\tau>0
depending quadratically on
g^{(1)}
and
g^{(2)}
to satisfy a polarization condition with a RHS also depending quadratically on
g^{(1)}
. Note that the polarization condition (
1.7
) is propagated by the transport equation (
1.8
) and corresponds to the two degrees of freedom of the TT-gauge from linearized gravity. Moreover, the equation for
g_{0}
shows how this strategy successfully describes the impact of the wave on the background spacetime. Finally, as announced above, the special structure of the Einstein vacuum equations implies transparency, i.e that gravitational waves do not suffer self-distortion, which translates as the linearity of the transport equation (
1.8
).

Before we describe the exact construction of this article, let us mention that WKB ansatz have also been considered in the linearized gravity setting in
[
AJOR21
]
in order to describe new geometric effect such as the spin Hall effect for gravitational waves, by analogy with the spin Hall effect for light (see
[
OJD+20
]
from the same authors).

1.2.3
Exact geometric optics in general relativity

The article
[
CB69
]
constructs first order approximate high-frequency vacuum spacetimes. Two questions arise in the spirit of the geometric optics: first, can one construct approximate vacuum spacetimes of any order? That is, for a given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, can one construct
(g_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in(0,1]}
such that
R_{\mu\nu}(g_{\lambda})=O\left(\lambda^{N}\right)
?
A priori
, an iterative procedure similar to Choquet-Bruhat’s construction or the author’s might give a positive answer, though a very technical one. Of greater importance is the second question: given Choquet-Bruhat’s
g_{\lambda}
, can one show stability of the geometric optics approximation? That is, can one show that
g_{\lambda}
stays close to an exact vacuum spacetime for a fixed time interval? This question is for instance raised in
[
Mé09
]
, and Theorem
1.1
and its precise version Theorem
2.2
provide a positive answer, thus fully justifying the geometric optics approximation for the Einstein vacuum equations. Note that this is far from automatic, and in many cases approximate solutions of arbitrary order are strongly unstable, see for example
[
Leb01
]
.

Let us now describe the new challenges posed by the construction of exact high-frequency vacuum spacetimes compared with Choquet-Bruhat’s construction or with standard geometric optics results. The most striking difference is the need for a more refined high-frequency ansatz, since satisfying
R^{(-1)}_{\mu\nu}=R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}=0
is not enough anymore. The ansatz (
1.6
) is strictly included in the ansatz (
1.2
), and the
O\left(\lambda^{2}\right)
in (
1.2
) hides some key terms such as a non-oscillating remainder, which absorbs every terms in the Ricci tensor of order
\lambda^{2}
or higher. Compared with
[
CB69
]
, we also provide the oscillating behaviour of
g^{(1)}
and
g^{(2)}
, thus describing the creation of harmonics. In order to control the creation of harmonics, we heavily rely on the exact structure of the quadratic non-linearity in (
1.1
). This is already present in
[
CB69
]
for second harmonic but we extend it to the third harmonic in
R^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}
.

The second difference is the need of a gauge choice, not needed if one only constructs approximate solutions to (
1.1
). We choose to work in a generalised wave gauge, which is part of our geometric optics construction. Concretely, the standard wave gauge
\Box_{g}x^{\alpha}=0
condition is replaced by
\Box_{g_{\lambda}}x^{\alpha}=O\left(\lambda\right)
where the
O\left(\lambda\right)
contains oscillating terms determined during the construction. This is the major difference with standard geometric optics results such as
[
JMR93
]
, where the systems of equations at stake are by nature already hyperbolic, whereas for (
1.1
) hyperbolicity precisely comes at a price of a gauge choice. Note that geometric optics for semi-linear gauge invariant systems such as the Yang-Mills equations have been studied in
[
Jea02
]
. The situation here is different since (
1.1
) are quasi-linear. Dealing with the oscillations in the gauge and using them to absorb some non-linear interactions is the main formal challenge of this article.

The third difference is linked to well-posedness, and as for the second, is totally absent from
[
CB69
]
. As explained at the end of Section
1.1
, the scaling of weakly non-linear geometric optics implies that Theorem
1.1
corresponds to a regime for which well-posedness is not proved, since the
H^{2}
norm of
g_{\lambda}
diverges in the high-frequency limit, while only the
H^{1}
norm is bounded. This loss of one derivative manifests itself in the coupled wave-transport system between the non-oscillating remainder and
g^{(2)}
. Regaining the missing derivative is the main analytical challenge of this article. A similar issue already occurs in
[
HL18b
]
, where the elliptic gauge is of great help. Here, we introduce Fourier multipliers adapted to the high-frequency expansion of the metric and rely on the background null foliation.

1.3
Burnett’s conjecture

We now turn to the second motivation of our result, the so-called Burnett conjecture, introduced by Burnett in
[
Bur89
]
. In this article, he consideres a family of metrics
(h_{\lambda})_{\lambda}
converging to a metric
h_{0}
as in (
1.3
), with the
h_{\lambda}
’s all satisfying (
1.1
). Burnett then asks: what is
R_{\mu\nu}(h_{0})-\frac{1}{2}R(h_{0})(h_{0})_{\mu\nu}
? In other words, what effective stress-energy tensor can be produced via weak limits of vacuum spacetimes? He proposes the following double conjecture:

•

the metric
h_{0}
solves the massless Einstein-Vlasov system

\left\{\begin{aligned} R_{\alpha\beta}(h_{0})&=\int_{h_{0}^{-1}(p,p)=0}f(x,p)p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\mathrm{d}\mu_{h_{0}},\\
p^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}f-p^{\alpha}p^{\beta}\Gamma(g_{0})^{\rho}_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{p^{\rho}}f&=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.9)

where
\mathrm{d}\mu_{h_{0}}
is the measure on the tangent bundle,

•

conversely, any solution of (
1.9
) is the limit in the sense (
1.3
) of a sequence of vacuum spacetimes
(h_{\lambda})_{\lambda}
.

Before we review the literature, let us explain how the direct and indirect parts of the conjecture interact. The direct conjecture deals with given sequences of solutions to (
1.1
) and tries to characterize their limits’ Ricci tensor. The singular nature of these vacuum solutions implies that their own existence is not trivial. Therefore, by answering the indirect conjecture one actually constructs concrete examples of sequences exhibiting the singular behaviour required by the direct conjecture.

The direct conjecture has first been adressed in
[
GW11
]
, where it is proved that the effective stress-energy tensor is traceless and satisfies the weak energy condition. This work has led to strong debates among cosmologists, see
[
BCE+15
,
GW15
]
, on the role played by backreaction in cosmology.

In a series of papers, Huneau and Luk prove Burnett’s conjecture in
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry, a symmetry under which the 3+1 Einstein vacuum equations reduce to the 2+1 Einstein-wave map system (see
[
Mon86
]
). In this setting, the direct part of the conjecture is proved by the means of microlocal defect measure in
[
HL19
]
(see also
[
GdC21
]
), while the indirect conjecture is proved for
N
null dusts (a discretized multiphase version of (
1.9
)) in
[
HL18b
]
and will be fully adressed in the forthcoming
[
HL23
]
. The two articles
[
HL18b
,
HL23
]
are both based on high-frequency ansatz in the spirit of geometric optics, but highly benefit from the
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry which allows the construction of an elliptic gauge.

In
[
LR20
]
, Luk and Rodnianski provide the first result without any symmetry assumption. Based on their work on the low-regularity characteristic Cauchy problem in double null gauge (see
[
Luk12
,
LR17
]
), they prove both sides of Burnett’s conjecture. Their proof is not based on a high-frequency ansatz, and thus can handle loss of strong convergence not only by oscillation but also by concentration. It also sheds new light on the Einstein-null dusts system and on the formation of trapped surfaces. They impose extra regularity along the angular directions compared with the null directions associated to the double null gauge. This has the following consequence:
[
LR20
]
only proves Burnett’s conjecture for two null dusts.

Our present geometric optics construction proves Burnett’s indirect conjecture without any symmetry assumption but only for one null dust. Therefore, it is somehow an intermediate result between Huneau and Luk’s approach and Luk and Rodnianski’s. However, thanks to
[
HL18b
]
and multiphase geometric optics results such as
[
JMR93
]
, there is hope that the present proof can be extended to an arbitrary number of null dusts, since the generalised wave gauge does not single out any null direction.

1.4
Acknowledgments

The author would like to thank his advisor Cécile Huneau for all the helpful suggestions and encouragement. The author is also very grateful to the anonymous referees for their constructive inputs that considerably improve the initial manuscript.

1.5
Outline of the article

The remainder of this article is structured as follows.

•

In Section
2
, we introduce the usual analytical material, define our class of initial data and state the main result on the existence of the sequence
(g_{\lambda})_{\lambda}
, i.e Theorem
2.2
.

•

In Section
3
, we present the strategy of the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

•

Sections
4
to
8
are devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

2
Statement of the results

In this section, we first introduce some material, define the background spacetime and finally state our main result.

2.1
Preliminaries

2.1.1
Notations and function spaces

We will work on the manifold
\mathcal{M}\vcentcolon=[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For
t\in[0,1]
, we denote by
\Sigma_{t}
the hypersurface
\{t\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We introduce the following standard conventions and notations.

•

The greek indices go from 0 to 3 and will always refer to the usual global coordinates system
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
on
\mathcal{M}
, while latin lower case indices go from 1 to 3 and correspond to the three spatial directions.

•

Let
T
and
S
be two symmetric 2-tensors and
g
a Lorentzian metric on
\mathcal{M}
. We define a scalar product by

\displaystyle|T\cdot S|_{g}=g^{\alpha\beta}g^{\mu\nu}T_{\alpha\mu}S_{\beta\nu},

with associated norm
|T|^{2}_{g}=|T\cdot T|_{g}
. The trace of
T
with respect to
g
is defined by
\mathrm{tr}_{g}T=g^{\alpha\beta}T_{\alpha\beta}
.

•

If
f
is a scalar function on
\mathcal{M}
, we denote by
\nabla f
a spatial derivative of
f
and by
\partial f
a time derivative
\partial_{t}f
or
\nabla f
. The wave operator associated to a Lorentzian metric is defined by

\displaystyle\Box_{g}f

\displaystyle=g^{\alpha\beta}\left(\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}f-\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\mu}f\right),

where
\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
are the Christoffel symbols. The principal part of this operator is denoted
\tilde{\Box}_{g}
, i.e
\tilde{\Box}_{g}f=g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}f
.

On each slice
\Sigma_{t}
we consider the usual function spaces
L^{p}
and
W^{k,p}
, by which we always mean
L^{p}(\Sigma_{t})
and
W^{k,p}(\Sigma_{t})
for some
t
depending on the context. We will also use the weighted Sobolev spaces defined below.

Definition 2.1

(Weighted Sobolev spaces)
.

For
1\leq p<+\infty
,
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
we define the space
W^{k,p}_{\delta}
as the completion of
C^{\infty}_{c}
for the norm

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{W^{k,p}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq|\alpha|\leq k}\left\|\langle x\rangle^{\delta+|\alpha|}\nabla^{\alpha}u\right\|_{L^{p}},

where the
L^{p}
norm is defined with the Euclidean volume element. We extend this definition to tensors of any type by summing over all components in coordinates. Some special cases are
H^{k}_{\delta}\vcentcolon=W^{k,2}_{\delta}
and
L^{p}_{\delta}\vcentcolon=W^{0,p}_{\delta}
.

We also define the following
L^{\infty}
-based spaces.

Definition 2.2
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
we define
C^{k}_{\delta}
as the completion of
C^{\infty}_{c}
for the norm

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{C^{k}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq|\alpha|\leq k}\left\|\langle x\rangle^{\delta+|\alpha|}\nabla^{\alpha}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}},

Let us recall some usual facts about these spaces (see
[
CB09
]
for the proofs).

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
s,s^{\prime},s_{1},s_{2},m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta,\delta^{\prime},\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq p<+\infty
.

(i)

If
s\leq\min(s_{1},s_{2})
,
s<s_{1}+s_{2}-\frac{3}{p}
and
\delta<\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\frac{3}{p}
we have the continuous embedding

\displaystyle W^{s_{1},p}_{\delta_{1}}\times W^{s_{2},p}_{\delta_{2}}\subset W^{s,p}_{\delta}.

(ii)

If
m<s-\frac{3}{p}
and
\beta\leq\delta+\frac{3}{p}
we have the continuous embedding

\displaystyle W^{s,p}_{\delta}\subset C^{m}_{\beta}.

There won’t be any issue of regularity in the time variable so if
f
is a scalar function defined on
\mathcal{M}
, then
\left\|f\right\|_{X}\leq C
without any precision on
t
will always mean

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\left\|f\right\|_{X(\Sigma_{t})}\leq C,

where
X
is one of the function spaces defined above.

2.1.2
High-frequency notations

In this section we introduce some notations regarding the oscillating behaviour of all the quantities involved, such as scalar functions, metrics, and more generally tensor fields defined on
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Such a quantity is said to be high-frequency or oscillating if it admits an expansion in powers of the small parameter
\lambda>0
with coefficients of the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)f,

(2.1)

where
f
is defined on
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathrm{T}
is a linear combination of trigonometric functions, i.e elements of

\displaystyle\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\sin(k\theta)~\middle|~k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\cup\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\cos(k\theta)~\middle|~k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

(2.2)

The phase
u_{0}
is defined in the next section, as a solution of the eikonal equation for the background metric
g_{0}
. The phase
\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}
will be considered as the fundamental harmonic or first harmonic, while the phase
\frac{ku_{0}}{\lambda}
is the
k
-th harmonic.

•

When considering a high-frequency quantity such as a tensor
S
, we denote by
S^{(i)}
the coefficients of
\lambda^{i}
in the expansion defining
S
, which thus expands formally as

\displaystyle S=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda^{i}S^{(i)}.

Note that
S^{(i)}
is a tensor of the same type as
S
. Moreover, if
j\in\mathbb{Z}
we define
S^{(\geq j)}
by

S^{(\geq j)}=\sum_{k\geq j}\lambda^{k-j}S^{(k)}.

This allows us to clearly truncate high-frequency expansions at a fixed order as in

\displaystyle S=\sum_{k\leq j-1}\lambda^{k}S^{(k)}+\lambda^{j}S^{(\geq j)}.

•

In terms of derivation, if
Q(t,x)=\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}(t,x)}{\lambda}\right)q(t,x)
is an oscillating quantity, then we introduce the two following notations

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}Q=\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)q,\qquad\tilde{\partial}_{\alpha}Q=\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{\alpha}q.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}Q=\frac{\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}}{\lambda}\partial_{\theta}Q+\tilde{\partial}_{\alpha}Q.

2.2
The background spacetime

The background quantities are defined on
\mathcal{M}
and are composed of a metric
g_{0}
, an optical function
u_{0}
and a density
F_{0}
. They solve the Einstein-null dust system on
\mathcal{M}
:

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}(g_{0})

\displaystyle=F_{0}^{2}\partial_{\mu}u_{0}\partial_{\nu}u_{0},

(2.3)

\displaystyle g_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})

\displaystyle=0,

(2.4)

\displaystyle-2L_{0}F_{0}+(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0})F_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

(2.5)

where
L_{0}
is the spacetime gradient of
u_{0}
, i.e

\displaystyle L_{0}=-g_{0}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}.

(2.6)

By (
2.4
),
L_{0}
is null and geodesic, i.e

\displaystyle g_{0}(L_{0},L_{0})=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}L_{0}=0,

where
\mathbf{D}
denotes throughout this article the covariant derivative associated to
g_{0}
. We also assume that
g_{0}
satisfies in the coordinates
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
the standard wave condition

\displaystyle g_{0}^{\mu\nu}\Gamma(g_{0})^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}=0.

(2.7)

Note that this condition implies that the wave operator associated to
g_{0}
reduces to its principal part, i.e
\Box_{g_{0}}=\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}
. A standard computation shows that under the wave condition (
2.7
) the Einstein type equation (
2.3
) rewrites as

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}(g_{0})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=P_{\alpha\beta}(g_{0})(\partial g_{0},\partial g_{0})-2F^{2}_{0}\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0},

(2.8)

where
P_{\alpha\beta}(g_{0})(\partial g_{0},\partial g_{0})
is a quadratic non-linearity (see (
3.2
) for its exact expression). We make the following assumptions on the background quantities
(g_{0},u_{0},F_{0})
. In what follows,
N\geq 10
,
\delta>-\frac{3}{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
is our smallness threshold.

•

Assumptions on the metric

g_{0}
. There exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that for all
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\left\|g_{0}-m\right\|_{H^{N+1}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}g_{0}\right\|_{H^{N}_{\delta+1}}+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}g_{0}\right\|_{H^{N-1}_{\delta+2}}\leq\varepsilon

(2.9)

where
m
is the Minkowski metric on
\mathcal{M}
. For convenience, we also assume that
\partial_{t}
is the unit normal to
\Sigma_{0}
of
g_{0}
, and that the second fundamental form of
\Sigma_{0}
is traceless. The former assumption simplifies the construction of the initial data for the high-frequency metric (see Section
5.5
) and the latter is only used in
[
Tou23b
]
to simplify the construction of high-frequency solutions to the constraint equations. The estimates (
2.9
) corresponds to the asymptotically flat setting.

•

Assumptions on the optical function

u_{0}
. We assume that there exists a constant non-zero vector field
\mathfrak{z}=(\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2},\mathfrak{z}_{3})
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u_{0}-\mathfrak{z}\right\|_{H^{N}_{\delta+1}}\leq\varepsilon,

(2.10)

where
\nabla u_{0}=(\partial_{1}u_{0},\partial_{2}u_{0},\partial_{3}u_{0})
is the spatial Euclidean gradient of
u_{0}
. This implies that the level sets of
u_{0}
restricted to any
\Sigma_{t}
are asymptotically planes in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and that if
\varepsilon
is small enough there exists
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\nabla u_{0}|(x)>c.

(2.11)

This in turn implies that
u_{0}
has no critical point. Finally, we assume that
L_{0}
defined by (
2.6
) is future-directed and since
\partial_{t}
is assumed to be the unit normal of
g_{0}
to
\Sigma_{0}
and
u_{0}
solves (
2.4
), this implies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{0}=|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}

(2.12)

on
\Sigma_{0}
, where
\bar{g}_{0}
is the induced metric on
\Sigma_{0}
. Finally, we mention another consequence of (
2.11
): with a stationary phase argument we can show that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{K}\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\psi\right|\lesssim\lambda\left\|\psi\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}(K)},

where
K
is any compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\mathrm{T}
is any trigonometric functions and
\psi
any test function defined on
K
. This in turn implies that the family
\left(\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\right)_{\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{0}]}
converges weakly to 0 in
L^{2}(K)
when
\lambda
tends to 0.

•

Assumptions on the density

F_{0}
. We assume that
F_{0}
is initially compactly supported, i.e there exists
R>0
such that
F_{0}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}
is supported in
\{|x|\leq R\}
. Since
L_{0}
is null and geodesic, (
2.5
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}\left(F_{0}\right)\subset J_{0}^{+}\left(\{|x|\leq R\}\right),

where
J_{0}^{+}
denotes the causal future associated to
g_{0}
. We can assume for simplicity that

\displaystyle J_{0}^{+}\left(\{|x|\leq R\}\right)\subset\left\{(t,x)\in\mathcal{M}~\middle|~|x|\leq C_{\mathrm{supp}}R\right\},

for some constant
C_{\mathrm{supp}}>0
. Finally we assume the following estimate:

\displaystyle\left\|F_{0}\right\|_{H^{N}}\leq\varepsilon.

(2.13)

In this article we don’t prove the existence of the background solution
(g_{0},F_{0},u_{0})
on
\mathcal{M}
, it follows from
[
CBF06
]
adapted to the null dust case. We will denote by
C_{0}
any numerical constant depending on the background estimates stated here, that is depending on
\delta
,
N
,
R
or
\mathfrak{z}
.

In addition to the usual coordinates on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
we will use a null frame attached to the optical function
u_{0}
, which we define as follows. We denote by
\mathcal{H}_{u}
the level sets of
u_{0}
, i.e

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{u}

\displaystyle=\left\{(t,x)\in\mathcal{M}~\middle|~u_{0}(t,x)=u\right\}.

Since
u_{0}
is a solution of the eikonal equation (
2.4
), each
\mathcal{H}_{u}
is a null hypersurface generated by the geodesic vector field
L_{0}
. Thanks to (
2.11
) they induce a foliation of the spacetime. For
t\in[0,1]
and
u
in the image of
u_{0}
we define the following 2-surfaces

\displaystyle P_{t,u}=\Sigma_{t}\cap\mathcal{H}_{u}.

Thanks to (
2.10
), each
P_{t,u}
have the topology of a plane in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We denote by
\mathring{g}_{0}
the induced metric on
P_{t,u}
. We consider a null vector field
\underline{L}_{0}
such that
g_{0}(\underline{L}_{0},L_{0})=-2
and an orthonormal frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
of
TP_{t,u}
for
\mathring{g}_{0}
. This defines the
background null frame

(L_{0},\underline{L}_{0},e_{1},e_{2})
, and such a choice is always possible, at least locally. We use the latin upper case letters as indices for the frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
on
TP_{t,u}
. We consider the subset

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{0}=\{L_{0},e_{1},e_{2}\}

(2.14)

of the background null frame. The use of such a frame originates in the seminal work of Christodoulou and Klainerman on the stability of Minkowski spacetime (see
[
CK93
]
) and a concise presentation can be found in
[
Sze18
]
.

Further geometric objects related to the background null frame will be needed for the proof of Theorem
2.2
, linked to the commutator
[L_{0},\Box_{g_{0}}]
. We refer to Lemma
3.1
for the main estimate on this commutator and to Appendix
B
for its proof.

2.3
Initial data

We reproduce here the main result of
[
Tou23b
]
where we construct high-frequency solutions
(\bar{g}_{\lambda},K_{\lambda})
of the constraint equations on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, that is

\displaystyle R(\bar{g}_{\lambda})+(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}_{\lambda}}K_{\lambda})^{2}-|K_{\lambda}|^{2}_{\bar{g}_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=0,

(2.15)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{g}_{\lambda}}K_{\lambda}+\mathrm{d}\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}_{\lambda}}K_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=0.

(2.16)

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
(g_{0},u_{0},F_{0})
be the solution of the Einstein-null dust system described in Section
2.2
, and let
\varepsilon>0
be the smallness threshold. There exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\delta,R)>0
such that if
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, there exists for all
\lambda\in(0,1]
a solution
(\bar{g}_{\lambda},K_{\lambda})
solution of the constraint equations (
2.15
)-(
2.16
) on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of the form

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\bar{g}_{0}+\lambda\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\bar{F}^{(1)}+\lambda^{2}\left(\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\bar{F}^{(2,1)}+\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\bar{F}^{(2,2)}\right)+\lambda^{2}\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda},

(2.17)

\displaystyle K_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=K^{(0)}_{\lambda}+\lambda K^{(1)}_{\lambda}+\lambda^{2}K^{(\geq 2)}_{\lambda},

(2.18)

with

\displaystyle K^{(0)}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=K_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}\bar{F}^{(1)},

(2.19)

\displaystyle\left(K^{(1)}_{\lambda}\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-N_{0}\bar{F}^{(1)}_{ij}+(\partial_{t}+N_{0})^{\rho}\Gamma(g_{0})_{\rho(i}^{k}\bar{F}^{(1)}_{j)k}+\frac{1}{2|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}}(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0})\bar{F}^{(1)}_{ij}\right)

(2.20)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla u_{0}\right|_{\bar{g}_{0}}\left(\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\bar{F}^{(2,1)}_{ij}-2\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\bar{F}^{(2,2)}_{ij}\right),

where
K_{0}
is the second fundamental form of
\Sigma_{0}
for
g_{0}
and
N_{0}=-\frac{\bar{g}_{0}^{ij}\partial_{i}u_{0}\partial_{j}}{|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}}
.
Moreover:

(i)

the tensors
\bar{F}^{(1)}
,
\bar{F}^{(2,1)}
and
\bar{F}^{(2,2)}
are supported in
\{|x|\leq R\}
and there exists
C_{\mathrm{cons}}=C_{\mathrm{cons}}(\delta,R)>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{F}^{(1)}\right\|_{H^{N}}+\left\|\bar{F}^{(2,1)}\right\|_{H^{N-1}}+\left\|\bar{F}^{(2,2)}\right\|_{H^{N-1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{cons}}\varepsilon,

(2.21)

(ii)

the tensor
\bar{F}^{(1)}
is
\bar{g}_{0}
-traceless, tangential to
P_{0,u}
and satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\bar{F}^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{\bar{g}_{0}}=8F_{0}^{2},

(2.22)

(iii)

the tensors
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}
and
K^{(\geq 2)}_{\lambda}
belong to the spaces
H^{5}_{\delta}
and
H^{4}_{\delta+1}
respectively and satisfy

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\nabla^{r+1}\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{cons}}\varepsilon,

(2.23)

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\nabla^{r}K_{\lambda}^{(\geq 2)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{cons}}\varepsilon.

(2.24)

We make two comments about the formulation of Theorem
2.1
, compared to the corresponding statement in
[
Tou23b
]
.

•

The main result of
[
Tou23b
]
is a purely elliptic result, it only requires a given solution of the maximal null dust constraint equations and not a spacetime solution of the Einstein-null dust system, as it may seem from the above formulation. Obviously, by projecting the background spacetime of Section
2.2
on
\Sigma_{0}
, one recovers the assumptions of
[
Tou23b
]
.

•

Similarly, the expressions of
K^{(0)}_{\lambda}
and
K^{(1)}_{\lambda}
given by (
2.19
) and (
2.20
) are equal to the ones given in
[
Tou23b
]
, thanks to some of the assumptions made on the background spacetime in the previous section, namely the fact that
\partial_{t}
is the unit normal to
\Sigma_{0}
for
g_{0}
and the wave condition.

2.4
Local existence in generalised wave gauge

The following theorem is our main result.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
(g_{0},u_{0},F_{0})
be the solution of the Einstein-null dust system described in Section
2.2
, and let
\varepsilon>0
be the smallness threshold. There exists
\lambda_{0}>0
and
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\delta,R)>0
such that if
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, there exists for all
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{0}]
a solution
g_{\lambda}
of the form

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}=g_{0}+\lambda g^{(1)}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)+\lambda^{2}g^{(2)}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)+\lambda^{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda},

(2.25)

to (
1.1
) on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
in generalised wave gauge. Moreover:

(i)

the tensors
g^{(i)}
for
i=1,2
are supported in
J_{0}^{+}\left(\{|x|\leq R\}\right)
and are periodic and smooth functions of the argument
\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}
,

(ii)

there exists
C=C(\delta,R)>0
such that the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,10\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\nabla^{r}g^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,6\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\nabla^{r}g^{(2)}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\max_{r\in\llbracket 1,5\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\nabla^{r+1}\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r}}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon,

(2.26)

(iii)

the tensor
g^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}=\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}
satisfies the following polarization condition, energy condition and transport equation,

\displaystyle g_{0}^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

(2.27)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}-\frac{1}{16}\left(\mathrm{tr}_{g_{0}}F^{(1)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=F_{0}^{2},

(2.28)

\displaystyle 2\partial^{\alpha}u_{0}\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}F^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}+(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0})F^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0.

(2.29)

Before we present the strategy behind the proof of Theorem
2.2
in Section
3
, let us make some general remarks.

•

The notation
g^{(i)}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
is used in (
2.25
) to emphasize the oscillating character of
g^{(i)}
, but these tensors also depends on the spacetime variables and this notation actually stands for the following functions on the spacetime

\displaystyle g^{(i)}_{\alpha\beta}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)(t,x)=g^{(i)}_{\alpha\beta}\left(t,x,\frac{u_{0}(t,x)}{\lambda}\right),

where
(t,x)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

•

The exact expression of the ansatz for
g_{\lambda}
is given in Section
4.1
. Compared to (
2.25
), we will then describe precisely the oscillating behaviour of each
g^{(i)}
for
i=1,2
, i.e give a finite frequency decomposition. Note that in Theorem
2.2
we already give the frequency decomposition of
g^{(1)}
, that is
g^{(1)}=\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(1)}
.

•

As explained in depth in the introduction, our main goal is to consider the high-frequency limit, that is
\lambda\to 0
. From this perspective, the restriction
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{0}]
with
\lambda_{0}
potentially very small is meaningless and only plays a role in the improvement of one of the bootstrap assumptions (see Proposition
7.1
).

•

The generalised wave condition defining our gauge has a complicated expression and we choose not to present it in Theorem
2.2
. In particular, it depends on the exact ansatz for
g_{\lambda}
and the coupling between the transport equations for the waves and the wave equation for the remainder in (
2.25
). See Section
5.3
for its exact expression.

•

The tensor
g^{(2)}
also satisfies polarization conditions similar to (
2.27
) but with RHS depending on
g^{(1)}
. See Section
5.3
for their exact expressions.

•

The oscillating terms in (
2.25
) depend on the phase
\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}
, where
u_{0}
is the solution of the background eikonal equation, i.e (
2.4
). We emphasize the fact that we don’t solve the eikonal equation for the metric
g_{\lambda}
, as in
[
HL18a
]
but as opposed to
[
LR20
]
. This would require the construction of a null foliation in 3+1 dimension and hence would complicate our proof.

3
Strategy of proof

In this section, we sketch the proof of Theorem
2.2
, the challenges of which we already introduced briefly in Section
1.2.3
.

3.1
The high-frequency ansatz

In any coordinates system, the Ricci tensor of a Lorentzian metric
g
reads

\displaystyle 2R_{\alpha\beta}(g)=-\tilde{\Box}_{g}g_{\alpha\beta}+H^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\beta}+g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}H^{\rho}+P_{\alpha\beta}(g)(\partial g,\partial g).

(3.1)

We encounter three types of terms in (
3.1
).

•

\tilde{\Box}_{g}
stands for the principal part of the wave operator. Because of this term, the Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
) are quasi-linear. Moreover, if we think of (
1.1
) as a system of second order partial differential equations for the metric coefficients, the
-\tilde{\Box}_{g}g_{\alpha\beta}
term represents the diagonal part of the Ricci operator.

•

The terms involving
H^{\rho}=g^{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}
are the gauge terms of the Ricci operator and their derivatives in (
3.1
) involve second order derivatives of the metric coefficients. They represent the non-diagonal part of the Ricci operator and working in generalised wave gauge precisely means that we prescribe the value of
H^{\rho}
in order to solve the loss of hyperbolicity it causes.

•

The quadratic non-linearity
P_{\alpha\beta}(g)(\partial g,\partial g)
is given in coordinates by

\displaystyle P_{\alpha\beta}(g)(\partial g,\partial g)

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\rho}g^{\nu\sigma}\left(\partial_{(\alpha}g_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta)\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\nu}-\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\nu}\partial_{\sigma}g_{\beta\mu}+\partial_{\rho}g_{\sigma\alpha}\partial_{\mu}g_{\nu\beta}\right).

(3.2)

It satisfies what we call the weak polarized null condition, see Section
3.1.2
.

While being highly non-linear, the Ricci tensor and the three parts defining it all enjoy some special structures. The goal of this section is to show how we benefit from these various structures to construct
g_{\lambda}
, whose precise ansatz is given by

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}=g_{0}+\lambda g^{(1)}+\lambda^{2}\left(g^{(2)}+\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}\right)+\lambda^{3}g^{(3)}.

(3.3)

The remainder
\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}
is a symmetric 2-tensor and each
g^{(i)}
are oscillating symmetric 2-tensor as defined in Section
2.1.2
. In the rest of this section and for the sake of clarity we drop the index
\lambda
and only write
g
and
\mathfrak{h}
instead of
g_{\lambda}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}
.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
g^{(1)}
has the form
g^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}=\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
, as stated in Theorem
2.2
. The Ricci tensor being non-linear, we must incorporate the creation of harmonics into
g
. Even though terms like
P_{\alpha\beta}(g)(\partial g,\partial g)
are of the form
g^{-1}g^{-1}\partial g\partial g
, thus corresponding to the interaction of four oscillating terms, the derivatives lose
\lambda
powers so at leading order in
\lambda
the terms
g^{-1}g^{-1}\partial g\partial g
corresponds actually to an interaction of only two oscillating terms. Therefore, we expect
g^{(2)}
to oscillate with frequencies
\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}
and
\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}
, i.e to contain the first and second harmonic. More precisely, by analogy with our toy model in
[
Tou23a
]
,
g^{(2)}
oscillates as
\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
and
\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
.

Since the Ricci tensor involves at most second order derivatives of the metric, it admits
a priori
a formal expansion of the form

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}(g_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}R^{(-1)}_{\mu\nu}+R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}+\lambda R^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}+\cdots.

(3.4)

As every geometric optics construction, it all comes down to plugging the high-frequency ansatz (
3.3
) in (
1.1
), obtaining the expression of the different orders in (
3.4
), equating them to zero and deduce equations and conditions for each terms in (
3.3
).

In order to understand the structures at stake, we introduce two key structural notions: admissible harmonics and non-tangential tensors.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
k=0,1
. The
admissible harmonics
at the
\lambda^{k}
level in the Ricci tensor (i.e in
R^{(k)}_{\mu\nu}
) are
\frac{\ell u_{0}}{\lambda}
for
\ell\in\llbracket 1,k+1\rrbracket
. A non-admissible harmonic at a certain level is said to be forbidden at this level.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
S
be a symmetric 2-tensor and recall (
2.14
).

(i)

We say that
S
is
non-tangential
if
S_{XY}=0
for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
. In coordinates this is equivalent to the existence of a 1-form
Q
such that
S_{\alpha\beta}=\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}Q_{\beta)}
.

(iii)

We say that
S
is
fully non-tangential
if the only non-zero null components of
S
is
S_{\underline{L}_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}
. In coordinates this is equivalent to the existence of a scalar function
f
such that
S_{\alpha\beta}=f\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0}
.

We also define the polarization tensor of a symmetric 2-tensor, already introduced in
[
CB69
]
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
S
be a symmetric 2-tensor, we define its polarization tensor by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}(S)=g_{0}^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}u_{0}S_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}S_{\mu\nu}\right).

(3.5)

Its components in the background null frame (see Section
2.2
) are

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}(S)

\displaystyle=-S_{L_{0}L_{0}},

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}_{A}(S)

\displaystyle=-S_{AL_{0}},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}_{\underline{L}_{0}}(S)

\displaystyle=-\delta^{AB}S_{AB}.

(3.8)

3.1.1
Quasi-linear forbidden harmonics

We start by looking at the wave operator in the Ricci tensor, also called the wave part in the sequel, that is
-\tilde{\Box}_{g}g_{\alpha\beta}
. Let us see how it acts on an oscillating scalar function, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\left(\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)f\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{g}f+\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(2g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}u_{0}\partial_{\nu}f+(\tilde{\Box}_{g}u_{0})f\right)

(3.9)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)g^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})f,

for any scalar function
f
and
\mathrm{T}
a trigonometric function. Thanks to (
2.4
) and (
3.6
), we have schematically

\displaystyle g^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})=\lambda\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(1)}\right)+\lambda^{2}\left(\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(2)}\right)+\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\right)+O\left(\lambda^{3}\right),

(3.10)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\left(\lambda g^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle=L_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}+\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(1)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}+\lambda\left(\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(2)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}+\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}\right)+O\left(\lambda^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\left(\lambda^{2}g^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda\left(L_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(2)}+\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(1)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(2)}\right)+O\left(\lambda^{2}\right),

where we neglected lower order terms. Regrouping terms with respect to their
\lambda
power, we obtain the following idealized equations by equating
\tilde{\Box}_{g}g
to 0:

\displaystyle L_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}+\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(1)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}

\displaystyle=0,

(3.11)

\displaystyle L_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(2)}+\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(1)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(2)}+\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(2)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}+\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}

\displaystyle=0.

(3.12)

We recover here the main feature of geometric optics: the waves
g^{(i)}
solve transport equations along the rays of the optical function
u_{0}
. These equations will absorb all the terms oscillating as
g^{(i)}
, i.e containing only admissible harmonics (recall Definition
3.1
). The terms
\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(i)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(k+2-i)}
in (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) for
k=0,1
contains necessarily forbidden harmonics that can’t be absorbed by the transport equations. They are precisely the terms expressing the quasi-linear nature of
\tilde{\Box}_{g}g
, i.e the ones coming from (
3.10
). We refer to them as quasi-linear forbidden harmonics in the rest of this section. We will use the structures of the gauge part and the quadratic part of the Ricci tensor to remove them.

The remainder
\mathfrak{h}
in (
3.3
) being non-oscillating, its contribution to the Ricci tensor from the wave part is simply
-\lambda^{2}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}
. Therefore, the condition
R^{(\geq 2)}_{\mu\nu}=0
will require
\mathfrak{h}
to solve a wave equation, absorbing any harmonics appearing at the
\lambda^{2}
level. For instance, one of the
\lambda^{2}
contribution of
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\left(\lambda^{2}g^{(2)}\right)
is of the form
\lambda^{2}\tilde{\partial}^{2}g^{(2)}
, this oscillating term will appear in the RHS of the wave equation for
\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}=\tilde{\partial}^{2}g^{(2)}+\text{other terms}.

(3.13)

We choose to highlight the term
\tilde{\partial}^{2}g^{(2)}
since it is the source of the loss of one derivative in the coupling with (
3.12
) (where
\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}
appears), see Section
3.2.2
below.

The role played by
\mathfrak{h}
, i.e absorbing every terms in
R^{(\geq 2)}_{\mu\nu}
, explains why
g^{(3)}
is different than
g^{(1)}
and
g^{(2)}
. Indeed the transport term
\lambda^{2}L_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(3)}
(coming from
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\left(\lambda^{3}g^{(3)}\right)
) will simply appear in the RHS of (
3.13
). Therefore,
g^{(3)}
won’t be defined as the solution of a transport equation, unlike
g^{(1)}
and
g^{(2)}
. See Section
3.1.3
below.

3.1.2
Semi-linear forbidden harmonics

We now turn our attention to the semi-linear terms, i.e the quadratic non-linearity
P_{\alpha\beta}(g)(\partial g,\partial g)
in the Ricci tensor given by (
3.2
). This term plays a key role in our construction, since it is the source of backreaction and the cause of new forbidden harmonics. To understand this, let us first look at its
\lambda^{0}
contribution to the Ricci tensor. Schematically we have

\displaystyle P_{\alpha\beta}(g)(\partial g,\partial g)

\displaystyle=P_{\alpha\beta}(g_{0})(\partial g_{0},\partial g_{0})+g_{0}^{-2}\partial g_{0}\partial u_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}+g_{0}^{-2}\left(\partial u_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}\right)^{2}+O\left(\lambda\right).

(3.14)

The first term in (
3.14
) will simply be identified with the corresponding term in the background equation (
2.8
). The second term is linear in
\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}
, and thus can be added to the RHS of (
3.11
) since it corresponds to an admissible harmonic. The most crucial term in (
3.14
) is the third one. Since
g^{(1)}=\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(1)}
, it schematically expands as

\displaystyle g_{0}^{-2}\left(\partial u_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{-2}\left(\partial u_{0}F^{(1)}\right)^{2}+\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)g_{0}^{-2}\left(\partial u_{0}F^{(1)}\right)^{2}.

(3.15)

The first term (
3.15
) is non-oscillating and thus must be absorbed by the background equation (
2.8
). Looking at this equation, the only possibility is to identify it with
-2F_{0}^{2}\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0}
, which explains why
P_{\alpha\beta}(g)(\partial g,\partial g)
is the source of backreaction. The oscillating term in (
3.15
) is a forbidden harmonic and for this reason cannot be absorbed by the transport equation (
3.11
). We refer to this term as the semi-linear forbidden harmonic, and as for the quasi-linear forbidden harmonic
\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(1)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}
in (
3.11
), it needs to be remove by the gauge terms.

Similarly, the
\lambda^{1}
contribution to the Ricci tensor of
P_{\alpha\beta}(g)(\partial g,\partial g)
contains among others the term
g_{0}^{-2}\partial u_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}\partial u_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(2)}
. It is a forbidden harmonic and cannot be absorbed by the transport equation (
3.12
). It is also a semi-linear forbidden harmonic and must be removed by gauge terms as the quasi-linear forbidden harmonics
\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(1)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(2)}
and
\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(2)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}
in (
3.12
).

Let us understand the structure of the semi-linear forbidden harmonics

\displaystyle g_{0}^{-2}\left(\partial u_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}\right)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{0}^{-2}\partial u_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}\partial u_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(2)}.

(3.16)

For this, we introduce the following quadratic form acting on symmetric 2 tensors

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(T,S)

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{\mu\rho}g_{0}^{\nu\sigma}\left(\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}T_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}u_{0}S_{\beta)\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}T_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\beta}u_{0}S_{\mu\nu}-\partial_{\rho}u_{0}T_{\alpha\nu}\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}S_{\beta\mu}\right).

This expression is obtained from (
3.2
) by replacing the contractions with
g
by contractions with
g_{0}
and replacing the
\partial g\partial g
by
\partial u_{0}T\partial u_{0}S
(the fourth term in (
3.2
) does not contribute because of (
2.4
)). It is such that the problematic terms in (
3.16
) are precisely given by
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}\left(\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)},\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}\right)
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}\left(\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)},\partial_{\theta}g^{(2)}\right)+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}\left(\partial_{\theta}g^{(2)},\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}\right)
respectively. A cumbersome computation leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(T,T)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(T,T)\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0}+g_{0}^{\nu\sigma}\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}(T)T_{\beta)\nu}-\mathrm{Pol}_{\alpha}(T)\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}(T),

(3.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(T,S)+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(S,T)

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{E}(T,S)\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0}+g_{0}^{\nu\sigma}\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\left(\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}(T)S_{\beta)\nu}+\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}(S)T_{\beta)\nu}\right)

(3.18)

\displaystyle\quad-\mathrm{Pol}_{(\alpha}(T)\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta)}(S),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(T,S)=\frac{1}{4}(\mathrm{tr}_{g_{0}}T)(\mathrm{tr}_{g_{0}}S)-\frac{1}{2}|T\cdot S|_{g_{0}}.

(3.19)

Since
L_{0}
is a null vector field for
g_{0}
, the nature of the quadratic form
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}
can be understood in the context of null conditions. The standard null condition has been introduced in
[
Kla86
,
Chr86
]
as a criterion for global existence in the small data regime for solutions to quadratic wave systems in 3+1 dimensions. In our context, it would translate to
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(T,T)=0
. In
[
CB00
]
, Choquet-Bruhat introduces the polarized null condition, which here reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}(T)=0\Longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(T,T)=0.

As it can be seen on (
3.17
), the quadratic form
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}
does not satisfy neither the standard null condition nor the polarized null condition. However, it does satisfy what we call the weak polarized null condition:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}(T)=0\Longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(T,T)\;\text{is fully non-tangential},

where we recall Definition
3.2
. For
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(T,S)+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(S,T)
, the weak polarized null condition reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}(T)=0\Longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(T,S)+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(S,T)\;\text{is non-tangential}.

More precisely, if
\mathrm{Pol}(T)=0
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(T,T)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(T,T)\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0},

(3.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(T,S)+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(S,T)

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{E}(T,S)\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0}+g_{0}^{\nu\sigma}\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}(S)T_{\beta)\nu}.

(3.21)

Remark 3.1
.

The expression for
\mathcal{E}\left(T,T\right)
does not seem to have a sign in the general case, but it does if
T
satisfies
\mathrm{Pol}(T)=0
. Indeed, in this case, we can show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\left(T,T\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\delta^{AB}\delta^{CD}T_{AC}T_{BD},

where the letters
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
refer to vectors of the orthonormal basis
(e_{1},e_{2})
of
TP_{t,u}
. In this article, the energy
\mathcal{E}(T,T)
is only used for tensors satisfying
\mathrm{Pol}(T)=0
, see in particular Section
8.1
.

Remark 3.2
.

The weak polarized null condition is linked to the weak null condition identified by Lindblad and Rodnianski in their proof of the stability of Minkowski in wave coordinates. We postpone the discussion of this aspect to Section
3.1.6
.

3.1.3
The gauge term leads to polarization conditions

In the last two sections, we showed how the quasi-linear aspects of the wave operator and the absence of the null condition for the quadratic non-linearity in the Ricci tensor cause forbidden harmonics to appear in
R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
and
R^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}
. In this section, we show how the gauge terms in the Ricci tensor can deal with these forbidden harmonics.

The gauge term
H^{\rho}
rewrites

\displaystyle H^{\rho}=g^{\mu\nu}g^{\rho\sigma}\left(\partial_{\mu}g_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}\right).

Its main contribution to the Ricci tensor of the oscillating metric
g
is then given by

\displaystyle g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}H^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta)}\left(\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}\right)+\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta)}\left(\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(2)}\right)+\lambda\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta)}\left(\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(3)}\right)

(3.22)

\displaystyle\quad+O\left(\lambda^{2}\right),

where we neglected any lower order terms (recall also Definition
3.3
). The fact that the gauge term manifests itself as polarization tensors in the Ricci tensor is the main reason why we introduced them in the first place, and why we expressed both the quasi-linear and semi-linear forbidden harmonics in terms of the polarization tensors of the different waves.

If we collect the expansions of the wave part and quadratic part of the Ricci tensor from the previous sections we obtain schematically an expression for the oscillating part of the Ricci tensor (we neglect the non-oscillating terms in
R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
and the
\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}
in
R^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}
):

\displaystyle R^{(-1)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{(\mu}u_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{\nu)}\left(\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}\right),

\displaystyle R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=L_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}+\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(1)}\right)\partial^{2}_{\theta}g^{(1)}+\text{admissible harmonic}

\displaystyle\quad+\partial_{(\mu}u_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{\nu)}\left(\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(2)}\right)+\text{semi-linear forbidden harmonic},

\displaystyle R^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=L_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(2)}+\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(1)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(2)}+\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(g^{(2)}\right)\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(1)}+\text{admissible harmonic}

\displaystyle\quad+\partial_{(\mu}u_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{\nu)}\left(\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(3)}\right)+\text{semi-linear forbidden harmonic}.

From the requirement
R^{(-1)}_{\mu\nu}=0
, we deduce that
\mathrm{Pol}\left(g^{(1)}\right)=0
. This polarization condition was already identified in
[
CB69
]
, and it implies that the physically significant part of
g^{(1)}
satisfies the TT-gauge assumptions. For our construction, it has three major consequences. First, the quasi-linear forbidden harmonics in
R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
vanishes. Second, the semi-linear forbidden harmonic in
R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
are now fully non-tangential (recall (
3.20
)). This implies that we can absorb them by imposing a polarization condition on
g^{(2)}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}\left(g^{(2)}\right)=\mathrm{const.}\times\mathcal{E}(T,T)\mathrm{d}u_{0}.

This condition implies in turn that the quasi-linear forbidden harmonic in
R^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}
vanishes (this would not hold if
Q(T,T)
were only non-tangential). Finally,
\mathrm{Pol}\left(g^{(1)}\right)=0
implies that the semi-linear forbidden harmonic in
R^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}
are now non-tangential (recall (
3.21
)). We can thus absorb them by imposing a polarization condition on
g^{(3)}
. Here, we used twice the following simple fact: the equation
\partial_{(\mu}u_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{\nu)}(T)=U_{\mu\nu}
is solvable if and only if
U_{\mu\nu}
is non-tangential.

Assuming that these various polarization conditions hold, we now have

\displaystyle R^{(-1)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=L_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(1)}+\text{admissible harmonic},

\displaystyle R^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=L_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(2)}+\text{admissible harmonic}.

We then obtain
R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}=R^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}=0
by defining
g^{(1)}
and
g^{(2)}
as the solutions of transport equations along the rays with appropriate RHS. In particular, since the admissible harmonic in
R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
are linear terms in
g^{(1)}
(see (
3.14
)), the transport equation for
g^{(1)}
is linear. As explained in depth in the introduction, this is known as transparency in the geometric optics literature. As shown here, transparency is made possible by the particular structure of the quadratic non-linearity and the presence of gauge terms. The link between transparency and the standard null condition has already been discussed in
[
Lan13
]
. Here we see how it survives with a weaker form of the standard null condition with the help of gauge invariance.

3.1.4
The gauge term leads to a generalised wave condition

The gauge term
H^{\rho}
and its main contribution
g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}H^{\rho}
to the Ricci tensor are also responsible for a more standard loss of hyperbolicity for the Einstein vacuum equations. In our case, this loss of hyperbolicity affects the wave equation for the remainder
\mathfrak{h}
. Very schematically, it reads

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}=\partial\mathfrak{h}\partial\mathfrak{h}+\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h},

(3.23)

where we neglected all contributions from the oscillating parts of (
3.3
), and singled out the second derivatives of
\mathfrak{h}
coming from
g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}H^{\rho}
. To remove
\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}
from (
3.23
) and get a well-posed equation, we need to set to zero the corresponding parts of
H^{\rho}
, i.e the
\partial\mathfrak{h}
in
H^{\rho}
. Moreover, as the discussion of Section
3.2.2
will show, some terms in
g^{(2)}
are actually coupled to
\mathfrak{h}
and we also need to set their contribution to
H^{\rho}
to zero. The precise definition of the parts of
H^{\rho}
we want to cancel is postponed to Section
4.2.3
.

3.1.5
Propagation of the polarization conditions

As explained in Section
3.1.3
, we need the waves
g^{(1)}
and
g^{(2)}
to satisfy both transport equations along the rays and polarization conditions. In
[
CB69
]
, Choquet-Bruhat proves that the polarization condition for
g^{(1)}
, that is
\mathrm{Pol}\left(g^{(1)}\right)=0
, is propagated by the transport equation
g^{(1)}
satisfies, which schematically reads
L_{0}g^{(1)}=g^{(1)}
. By commuting
L_{0}
and
\mathrm{Pol}
, she deduced a transport equation for
\mathrm{Pol}\left(g^{(1)}\right)
, allowing the propagation of
\mathrm{Pol}\left(g^{(1)}\right)=0
from the data. Unfortunately, the transport equations and polarization conditions satisfied by
g^{(2)}
(see Sections
5.1
and
5.3
) are much more complicated than the one
g^{(1)}
satisfies and the previous strategy seems unfeasible. Instead of deriving the equations for
\mathrm{Pol}\left(g^{(2)}\right)
directly from the equations that
g^{(2)}
satisfies, we treat the polarization conditions for
g^{(2)}
as gauge conditions. This means that we first solve the hierarchy of equations as if they were satisfied, and then derive the desired transport equations from the contracted Bianchi identities, which states that the Einstein tensor of a Lorentzian metric is divergence free. This shows how we recover the polarization conditions on the whole spacetime, since our choice of initial data will be such that they hold initially (see Corollary
5.1
).

3.1.6
A remark on null conditions

In this cultural section, we intend to motivate the designation ”weak polarized null condition” and to make the link with the weak null condition of Lindblad and Rodnianski. This condition is introduced in
[
LR03
]
for general non-linear wave systems with quadratic non-linearity. It is also proved there that the Einstein vacuum equations in wave coordinates do satisfy this condition. It plays a crucial role in the proof of the stability of Minkowski spacetime in wave coordinates, see
[
LR10
]
.

Since we consider the same system of equations as Lindblad and Rodnianski, namely (
1.1
) in a similar gauge, their weak null condition is satisfied here. Actually, the term
\mathcal{E}\left(T,T\right)\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0}
in (
3.17
) precisely corresponds to the problematic term
P(\partial_{\mu}h,\partial_{\nu}h)
from
[
LR10
]
(see in particular Proposition 3.1 there). However, the phenomena observed in the present article, that is a non-linear effect (backreaction) together with linear propagation (transparency), seem to single out the Einstein vacuum equations in (generalised or not) wave coordinates among systems satisfying Lindblad and Rodnianski’s weak null condition. Indeed, consider the following system

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Box\varphi&=(\partial_{t}\psi)^{2},\\
\Box\psi&=Q(\partial\varphi,\partial\varphi),\end{aligned}\right.

(3.24)

where
\varphi
and
\psi
are scalar functions defined on
\mathbb{R}^{3+1}
,
\Box
is the flat wave operator and
Q
satisfies the standard null condition. Let us perform a geometric optics expansion for
\varphi
and
\psi
, in the spirit of the present article. Consider functions of the form

\displaystyle\varphi=\varphi_{0}+\lambda\varphi^{(1)}\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\psi=\psi_{0}+\lambda\psi^{(1)}\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right),

(3.25)

where
v
solves the flat eikonal equation (say
v=t-x
or
v=t-r
), and
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\psi^{(1)}
also depends on the variable
(t,x)
. The ansatz (
3.25
) defines approximate solutions of the system (
3.24
) up to terms of order
\lambda
if and only if the following hierarchy of equations hold

\displaystyle\Box\varphi_{0}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{t}\psi_{0})^{2}+(\partial_{t}v)^{2}\pi\left(\partial_{\theta}\psi^{(1)}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle\Box\psi_{0}

\displaystyle=Q(\partial\varphi_{0},\partial\varphi_{0}),

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{v}\partial_{\theta}\varphi^{(1)}

\displaystyle=2\partial_{t}\psi_{0}\partial_{t}v\partial_{\theta}\psi^{(1)}+(\partial_{t}v)^{2}\left(\mathrm{Id}-\pi\right)\left(\partial_{\theta}\psi^{(1)}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{v}\partial_{\theta}\psi^{(1)}

\displaystyle=2Q(\partial\varphi_{0},\partial v)\partial_{\theta}\varphi^{(1)},

where
\pi
denotes the mean operator with respect to the oscillating variable and
\mathscr{L}_{v}=2\partial^{\mu}v\partial_{\mu}+\Box v
is the transport operator along the rays. Since
(\varphi_{0},\psi_{0})
does not satisfy (
3.24
), we observe backreaction (which is of no surprise since (
3.24
) doesn’t satisfy the standard null condition). We don’t observe transparency since the transport system for
\left(\varphi^{(1)},\psi^{(1)}\right)
is non-linear. However, it is well known that the system (
3.24
) does satisfy the weak null condition. This simple example explains in particular the need in our work for a new designation ”weak polarized null condition”, where the adjective ”polarized” refers to the fact that transparency is obtained through polarization conditions for the waves.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that the specificity of the Einstein vacuum equations is even more evident in other gauges than wave gauges. For instance, in double null gauges or in Coulomb type gauges, (
1.1
) satisfy equivalents of the null condition. This is crucially used in seminal works such as
[
CK93
]
or
[
KRS15
]
.

3.2
Solving the hierarchy of equations

In this section, we explain how we prove well-posedness for the hierarchy of equations derived from putting the ansatz (
3.3
) into the Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
). As explained above, the hierarchy of equations is made of transport equations for
g^{(1)}
and
g^{(2)}
and a wave equation for
\mathfrak{h}
. As we will see, well-posedness would be trivial if not for the quasi-linear nature of the wave operator in the Ricci tensor.

3.2.1
The background transport equations

As explained above, the transport equation for
g^{(1)}
is linear and reads schematically

\displaystyle L_{0}g^{(1)}

\displaystyle=g^{(1)}.

(3.26)

The transport equation for
g^{(2)}
reads schematically

\displaystyle L_{0}g^{(2)}

\displaystyle=g^{(2)}+\tilde{\partial}^{2}g^{(1)},

(3.27)

where in the RHS of (
3.27
) we only consider the worst term from the regularity point of view. In particular, we neglected the term
\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}
from (
3.12
). The system (
3.26
)-(
3.27
) is triangular and solving it causes no problem. In the sequel, the exact form of this system will be called the
background system
because the regularity of its solution only depends on the background spacetime and does not require any bootstrap argument.

Remark 3.4
.

The threshold for the background regularity, i.e
N\geq 10
(see Section
2.2
), is chosen so that all these background quantities and their derivatives can be ultimately bounded in
L^{\infty}
. Let us explain briefly why the value
10
fills this requirement. If the background metric coefficients are in
H^{N+1}
(we neglect the weights at spacelike infinity in this discussion), then (
3.26
) and (
3.27
) imply that
g^{(1)}\in H^{N}
and
g^{(2)}\in H^{N-2}
. Second derivatives of
g^{(2)}
will appear as a source term in the wave equation for the remainder
\mathfrak{h}
. In order to prove local well-posedness for this equation we will differentiate it 4 times (see (
3.36
) below) and our worse term is thus
\tilde{\partial}^{6}g^{(2)}
. We want to bound it in
L^{\infty}
so the usual Sobolev embedding
H^{2}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
implies that we need
N-2\geq 8
.

3.2.2
The quasi-linear coupling and the loss of derivatives

In this section, we discuss the major impact of the term
\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}
in (
3.12
), which has been neglected so far since it causes no formal issues and thus does not enter the discussion on forbidden harmonics. Recall that the presence of this term as source term in the transport equation for
g^{(2)}
is directly linked to the quasi-linear aspect of
\tilde{\Box}_{g}g
.

To make things clearer, we assume that
\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}
is the only source term in the transport equation for
g^{(2)}
, which we can thus write as
g^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}=\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}
. Similarly, we assume that
\Box_{g}\mathfrak{F}
is the only source term in the wave equation for
\mathfrak{h}
(this term was already singled out in (
3.13
)). The coupled system between
\mathfrak{F}
and
\mathfrak{h}
then reads

\displaystyle L_{0}\mathfrak{F}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h},

(3.28)

\displaystyle\Box_{g}\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\Box_{g}\mathfrak{F},

(3.29)

where we simply write
\mathfrak{h}
instead of
\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\partial^{2}_{\theta}g^{(1)}
. Performing energy estimates on (
3.28
) implies schematically that
\partial\mathfrak{F}\sim\partial\mathfrak{h}
while the energy estimate for (
3.29
) gives
\partial\mathfrak{h}\sim\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}
. Therefore, there is an
a priori
loss of one derivative in the coupling between (
3.28
) and (
3.29
). Regaining this derivative is the main analytical challenge of our work.

3.2.3
Regaining the missing derivative

The main idea is to show that
\Box_{g}\mathfrak{F}
is better than
\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}
, i.e better than any second order derivatives of
\mathfrak{F}
. To show this, we would like to derive a transport operator for
\Box_{g}\mathfrak{F}
from (
3.28
). However we can’t hope for any structural property of
[L_{0},\Box_{g}]
since
L_{0}
is a null vector for
g_{0}
and not
g
. The idea is to first benefit from the expansion defining
g
and write schematically

\displaystyle\Box_{g}\mathfrak{F}

\displaystyle=\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}+\lambda\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}.

(3.30)

We improve the first term in (
3.30
) by considering the commutator
[L_{0},\Box_{g_{0}}]
for which we can now hope for structural properties. Formally we have from (
3.28
)

\displaystyle L_{0}\left(\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right)

\displaystyle=[L_{0},\Box_{g_{0}}]\mathfrak{F}+\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{h}.

(3.31)

We need to know what are the second derivatives appearing in the commutator
[L_{0},\Box_{g_{0}}]
. This is the content of the following key lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
f
be compactly supported, we have

\displaystyle\left\|[L_{0},\Box_{g_{0}}]f\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(C_{0})\left(\left\|\partial L_{0}f\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\Box_{g_{0}}f\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial f\right\|_{L^{2}}\right).

(3.32)

Moreover, if
r\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}[L_{0},\Box_{g_{0}}]f\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(C_{0})\left(\left\|\nabla^{r}\partial L_{0}f\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}f\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial f\right\|_{H^{r}}+\left\|\partial^{2}f\right\|_{H^{r-1}}\right).

(3.33)

The proof of this lemma is postponed to Appendix
B
and is based on the background null foliation induced by the optical function
u_{0}
. In the case of equation (
3.31
), the term
\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}
from (
3.32
) is treated with Gronwall’s inequality and the term
\partial L_{0}\mathfrak{F}
rewrites as
\partial\mathfrak{h}
thanks to (
3.28
). By using again the expansion for
g
, we can use equation (
3.29
) for the term
\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{h}
in (
3.31
). Therefore, we are able to show that
\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}
is at the level of
\partial\mathfrak{h}
, which is consistent with (
3.29
). This is done in detail in Section
7.4.1
.

It remains to improve the second term in (
3.30
). As our notation suggests it, we don’t use any structure of the second order derivatives involved but rather benefit from the small factor
\lambda
in front. The idea is to cancel one derivative with this factor, which rigorously involves a spatial Fourier projector
\Pi_{\leq}
, that is a Fourier multiplier supported in
\left\{|\xi|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\right\}
. If we also define
\Pi_{\geq}=\mathrm{Id}-\Pi_{\leq}
, we have indeed Bernstein-like estimates in
L^{2}
:

\displaystyle\lambda\partial\Pi_{\leq}(f)\sim f\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{\lambda}\Pi_{\geq}(f)\sim\partial f.

The proof of these estimates and the exact definition of the projectors
\Pi_{\leq}
and
\Pi_{\geq}
are postponed to Appendix
C.3
. In order to benefit from these projectors, we need to introduce them into the system (
3.28
)-(
3.29
). Instead of this system, we thus solve

\displaystyle L_{0}\mathfrak{F}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\leq}(\mathfrak{h}),

(3.34)

\displaystyle\Box_{g}\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\Box_{g}\mathfrak{F}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\Pi_{\geq}(\mathfrak{h}).

(3.35)

Using the two Bernstein-like estimates, equation (
3.34
) now implies that
\lambda\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\sim\partial\mathfrak{h}
while the extra term in (
3.35
) is absorbed by the energy estimate for the wave operator. This modification of the system is only possible because (
3.28
)-(
3.29
) are obtained as part of the high-frequency expansion of the Ricci tensor. This allows us to artificially remove
\Pi_{\geq}(\mathfrak{h})
from the transport equation for
\mathfrak{F}
by simply rewriting the
\lambda\mathfrak{h}
term as

\displaystyle\lambda\Pi_{\leq}(\mathfrak{h})+\lambda^{2}\times\frac{1}{\lambda}\Pi_{\geq}(\mathfrak{h}).

In particular, this modification leaves (
1.1
) invariant. This shows how one can use (
3.30
) to gain a derivative, and obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\partial\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda^{r}},

(3.36)

for
r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
(we omitted the weight at spacelike infinity for
\mathfrak{h}
). Note that since we modified the equation for
\mathfrak{F}
, we need the following commutator estimate to deal with the first term in (
3.30
):

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
u
and
v
be two tempered distribution. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\left[u,\Pi_{\leq}\right]\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left(\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\lambda\left\|u\right\|_{H^{\frac{7}{2}}}\right)\left\|v\right\|_{L^{2}}.
