(3.37)

This lemma will be used to estimate
\left[\Pi_{\leq},\Box_{g_{0}}\right]
. Its proof is postponed to Appendix
C.3
and relies on the Littlewood-Paley decomposition, and we introduce in Section
C.1
the material needed.

Remark 3.5
.

Strictly speaking, the introduction of the Fourier projectors
\Pi_{\leq}
and
\Pi_{\geq}
, going from (
3.28
)-(
3.29
) to (
3.34
)-(
3.35
), does not solve all of our issues. It actually replaces the loss of one derivative by the loss of one power of
\lambda
, since (
3.34
) now implies
\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\sim\frac{1}{\lambda}\partial h
. Plugging this into the energy estimate for (
3.35
), this translates as a time of existence of order
\lambda
, which is far from satisfactory. A careful bootstrap argument is still required to regain this power of
\lambda
, see Section
7
.

3.2.4
The order of precision of the ansatz

Finally, let us comment on the order of precision of the high-frequency ansatz defining
g
. As (
3.3
) shows, the order of precision is 2, since the non-oscillating remainder
\mathfrak{h}
appears first at the order
\lambda^{2}
in
g
. This is similar to the construction of
[
HL18b
]
in
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry, but different from the one of
[
Tou23a
]
, where we are able to construct high-frequency ansatz of arbitrary precision for a semi-linear toy model.

As in
[
Tou23a
]
, the value of this order of precision is linked to the estimates used to solve the equations at stake. To make this clearer, let us assume that this value, denoted by
K\in\mathbb{Z}
, is not fixed yet. The transport equations only involve the transport operator
L_{0}
which is part of the background and therefore their resolution is independent from
\lambda
. However, we use energy estimates associated to
g
to solve (
3.35
). As shown in
[
Sog95
]
(see also Lemma
5.5
in this article) this energy estimates reads schematically

\displaystyle\left\|\partial u\right\|_{L^{2}}(t)\lesssim\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\partial g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\left(\left\|\partial u\right\|_{L^{2}}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\tilde{\Box}_{g_{\lambda}}u\right\|_{L^{2}}\right).

(3.38)

Therefore, in order to construct a solution to (
3.35
) with a time of existence independent of
\lambda
, we need
\left\|\partial g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
to be bounded uniformly in the high-frequency limit
\lambda\to 0
, since it appears as coefficient in (
3.38
). If we focus on the term
\lambda^{K}\mathfrak{h}
in
g
, the usual Sobolev embedding
H^{2}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
gives

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda^{K}\partial\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\lambda^{K}\left\|\partial\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{K-2},

where we used the fact that one loses one power of
\lambda
for each derivative of
\partial\mathfrak{h}
(see (
3.36
)). This shows why
K\geq 2
is necessary. The present article then shows that
K=2
is sufficient to construct local high-frequency solutions to (
1.1
) on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Remark 3.6
.

As explained in the introduction, constructing similar solutions to (
1.1
) but of greater order of precision would be an interesting problem from the geometric optics point of view. Moreover, it could pave the way for the study of the long time dynamics of high-frequency gravitational waves. Indeed, the main result of
[
Tou23a
]
shows on a toy model how the vector field method and the Klainerman-Sobolev inequality can be applied to geometric optics approximation but require
K\geq 4
.

3.3
Outline of the proof

We give here an outline of the article’s remainder which is concerned with the proof of Theorem
2.2
and follows the strategy depicted in the previous sections.

•

In Section
4
, we give the final expression of the ansatz for
g
and compute its Ricci tensor.

•

In Section
5
, we give the hierarchy of equations as well as the polarization and generalised wave gauge conditions. The hierarchy is composed of the background system and the reduced system. We also define the initial data and provide basic estimates.

•

In Section
6
, we solve the background system.

•

In Section
7
, we solve the reduced system.

•

In Section
8
, we show that the metric
g
is actually a solution of (
1.1
) by propagating the polarization and generalised wave gauge conditions.

4
The high-frequency ansatz

4.1
High-frequency expansion of the metric

The metric we consider is given by

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}=g_{0}+\lambda g^{(1)}+\lambda^{2}\left(g^{(2)}+\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}\right)+\lambda^{3}g^{(3)},

(4.1)

where

\displaystyle g^{(1)}

\displaystyle=F^{(1)}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right),

(4.2)

\displaystyle g^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}_{\lambda}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)+F^{(2,1)}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)+F^{(2,2)}\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right).

(4.3)

The symmetric 2-tensors
F^{(1)}
and
F^{(2,i)}
for
i=1,2
are called the background perturbations. They solve the background system, defined in Section
5.1
. The symmetric 2-tensors
\mathfrak{F}_{\lambda}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}
solve the reduced system, defined in Section
5.2
. We use a different font to emphasize their special role. The exact definition of
g^{(3)}
will be given in Section
5.4
, let us just say for now that it is a polynomial in terms of the background perturbations and their derivatives and is independent from
\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}
but does depends on
\mathfrak{F}_{\lambda}
without derivatives. The background perturbations tensors don’t depend on
\lambda
, unlike the tensors
\mathfrak{F}_{\lambda}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}
and the metric
g_{\lambda}
itself. However, for the sake of clarity we drop from now on this notation and only write
\mathfrak{F}
,
\mathfrak{h}
and
g
.

Remark 4.1
.

The presence of
\mathfrak{F}
and
F^{(2,1)}
in
g^{(2)}
seems redundant and is not necessary. However it seems useful to distinguish between the part of the
\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}
oscillation in
g^{(2)}
coupled to
\mathfrak{h}
(see Sections
3.2.2
for a informal discussion) and the part whose only purpose is to absorb terms coming from
g^{(1)}
such as
\Box_{g_{0}}F^{(1)}
and which can thus be treated as a background quantity.

We can formally compute the high-frequency expansion of the inverse of
g
. In coordinates it is given by

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{\mu\nu}+\lambda(g^{\mu\nu})^{(1)}+\lambda^{2}(g^{\mu\nu})^{(2)}+\lambda^{3}H^{\mu\nu},

(4.4)

where

\displaystyle(g^{\mu\nu})^{(1)}

\displaystyle=-(F^{(1)})^{\mu\nu}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right),

(4.5)

\displaystyle(g^{\mu\nu})^{(2)}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{\mu\rho}(F^{(1)})^{\nu\sigma}F^{(1)}_{\rho\sigma}\cos^{2}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)-\mathfrak{F}^{\mu\nu}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)-\mathfrak{h}^{\mu\nu}

(4.6)

\displaystyle\quad-(F^{(2,1)})^{\mu\nu}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)-(F^{(2,2)})^{\mu\nu}\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right).

On the RHS of these expressions, all the inverses are with respect of the background metric
g_{0}
. For example we have
(F^{(1)})^{\mu\nu}=g_{0}^{\mu\rho}g_{0}^{\nu\sigma}F^{(1)}_{\rho\sigma}
. The quantity
H^{\mu\nu}
is defined so that (
4.4
) holds with
(g^{\mu\nu})^{(i)}
for
i=1,2
being defined by (
4.5
)-(
4.6
), and one can verify by hand that
H^{\mu\nu}=O\left(1\right)
.

Remark 4.2
.

We denote by
g^{-k}
any product of
k
coefficients of the inverse metric
g^{-1}
. This also applies to the background inverse metric
g_{0}^{-1}
.

We conclude this section by computing the Christoffel symbols of the metric
g
. They are given by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(g_{0})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}+(\tilde{\Gamma}^{(0)})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}+O\left(\lambda\right),

(4.7)

where

\displaystyle(\tilde{\Gamma}^{(0)})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\left(\partial_{\mu}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\nu\sigma}+\partial_{\nu}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\mu\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}\right).

(4.8)

4.2
High-frequency expansion of the Ricci tensor

In this section, we compute the Ricci tensor of the metric
g
given by (
4.1
). In order to lighten the computations, we assume that
F^{(1)}
satisfies

\displaystyle F^{(1)}_{L_{0}\alpha}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{tr}_{g_{0}}F^{(1)}=0.

(4.9)

These are very reasonable assumptions, since they correspond to the polarization condition
\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(1)}\right)=0
already identified in
[
CB69
]
and discussed in Section
3.1.3
, together with the stronger requirement
F^{(1)}_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}=0
. The fact that
F^{(1)}
satisfies these conditions will be proved in Section
.

The expansion of the Ricci tensor is based on (
3.1
), i.e its decomposition in generalised wave coordinates, and we first expand the wave part
\tilde{\Box}_{g}g_{\alpha\beta}
, then the quadratic non-linearity
P_{\alpha\beta}
and finally the gauge part
H^{\rho}
, in Propositions
4.1
,
4.2
and
4.3
respectively. Finally, Proposition
4.4
gives the expansion of the Ricci tensor of the oscillating metric
g
. The computations leading to these propositions are postponed to Appendix
A
.

4.2.1
The wave part

We start by expanding the wave part
\tilde{\Box}_{g}g_{\alpha\beta}
of the Ricci tensor of
g
.

Proposition 4.1
.

The wave part of the Ricci tensor of
g
admits the expansion

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}g_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=W^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda W^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda^{2}W^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta},

(4.10)

where

\displaystyle W^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}(g_{0})_{\alpha\beta}-\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-2L_{0}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+(\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}u_{0})F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}\right),

(4.11)

\displaystyle W^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-2L_{0}F^{(2,1)}_{\alpha\beta}+(\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}u_{0})F^{(2,1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\tilde{W}^{(1,1)}_{\alpha\beta}-2L_{0}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}+(\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}u_{0})\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}+\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}\right)

(4.12)

\displaystyle\quad-2\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-2L_{0}F^{(2,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+(\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}u_{0})F^{(2,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\tilde{W}^{(1,2)}_{\alpha\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathfrak{F}_{L_{0}L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(2,2)}_{L_{0}L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle W^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha\beta}+\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda\tilde{\Box}_{g}g^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta}+\tilde{W}^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta},

(4.13)

with lower order terms given schematically by

\displaystyle\tilde{W}^{(1,1)}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{-2}F^{(1)}\partial^{2}g_{0}+g_{0}^{-2}\partial^{2}F^{(1)}+g_{0}^{-2}(F^{(1)})^{2}\partial^{2}u_{0},

(4.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{W}^{(1,2)}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{-2}F^{(1)}\partial^{2}g_{0}+g_{0}^{-2}\partial^{2}F^{(1)}+g_{0}^{-2}(F^{(1)})^{2}\partial^{2}u_{0}+g_{0}^{-1}(\partial u_{0})^{2}F^{(2,1)}F^{(1)},

(4.15)

\displaystyle\tilde{W}^{(\geq 2)}

\displaystyle=(g^{-1})^{(\geq 3)}(\partial u_{0})^{2}F^{(1)}

(4.16)

\displaystyle\quad+(g^{-1})^{(\geq 2)}\left(\partial^{2}g_{0}+\partial^{2}u_{0}F^{(1)}+\partial u_{0}\left(\partial F^{(1)}+\partial F^{(2,1)}+\partial\mathfrak{F}\right)+(\partial u_{0})^{2}\left(F^{(2,i)}+\mathfrak{F}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+(g^{-1})^{(\geq 1)}\left(\partial^{2}F^{(1)}+\partial^{2}u_{0}\left(F^{(2,i)}+\mathfrak{F}\right)\right)+g^{-1}\partial^{2}F^{(2,i)}.

Remark 4.3
.

Let us briefly comment on these various expressions, recalling the formal discussion of Section
3.1.1
.

•

The absence of the quasi-linear forbidden harmonic
\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}
in
W^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}
coming from
F^{(1)}_{L_{0}L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
is due to (
4.9
).

•

The quasi-linear forbidden harmonic
\frac{3u_{0}}{\lambda}
in
W^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
is due to
F^{(2)}_{L_{0}L_{0}}
, and will eventually vanish thanks to polarization conditions.

4.2.2
The quadratic non-linearity

In this section we expand
P_{\alpha\beta}(g)(\partial g,\partial g)
(see (
3.2
) for an exact expression).

Proposition 4.2
.

The quadratic non-linearity in the Ricci tensor of
g
admits the expansion

P_{\alpha\beta}(g)(\partial g,\partial g)=P^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda P^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda^{2}P^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta},

(4.17)

where

\displaystyle P^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=P_{\alpha\beta}(g_{0})(\partial g_{0},\partial g_{0})-\frac{1}{4}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0}

(4.18)

\displaystyle\quad-\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-2L_{0}^{\rho}\Gamma(g_{0})^{\nu}_{(\alpha\rho}F^{(1)}_{\beta)\nu}+\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{\beta)}\right)+\frac{1}{4}\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0},

\displaystyle P^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-2L_{0}^{\rho}\Gamma(g_{0})^{\nu}_{(\alpha\rho}\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\nu\beta)}+F^{(2,1)}_{\nu\beta)}\right)+P^{(1,1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\hat{P}^{(1,1)}_{\beta)}\right)

(4.19)

\displaystyle\quad+\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(4L_{0}^{\rho}\Gamma(g_{0})^{\nu}_{(\alpha\rho}F^{(2,2)}_{\nu\beta)}+P^{(1,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\hat{P}^{(1,2)}_{\beta)}\right)+\cos\left(\frac{3u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\hat{P}_{\beta)}^{(1,3)},

with lower order terms given schematically by

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{(0,1)}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{-2}F^{(1)}\partial g_{0},

(4.20)

\displaystyle P^{(1,k)}

\displaystyle=\left(g_{0}^{-3}F^{(1)}\partial g_{0}+g_{0}^{-2}\partial F^{(1)}\right)\left(\partial g_{0}+\partial u_{0}F^{(1)}\right),

(4.21)

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{(1,i)}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{-2}\left(\partial g_{0}+\partial u_{0}F^{(1)}\right)\left(\mathfrak{F}+F^{(2,k)}\right)+\partial u_{0}g_{0}^{-3}(F^{(1)})^{3},

(4.22)

\displaystyle P^{(\geq 2)}

\displaystyle=(g^{-2}\partial g\partial g)^{(\geq 2)},

(4.23)

for
k=1,2
and
i=1,2,3
.

Remark 4.4
.

Let us briefly comment on these various expressions, recalling the formal discussion of Section
3.1.2
.

•

Thanks to
\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(1)}\right)=0
(which follows from (
4.9
)), the semi-linear forbidden harmonic
\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}
in
P^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}
is fully non-tangential. See (
3.20
), where
\mathcal{E}\left(F^{(1)},F^{(1)}\right)
is simplified thanks to
\mathrm{tr}_{g_{0}}F^{(1)}=0
from (
4.9
).

•

Again thanks to
\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(1)}\right)=0
, the semi-linear forbidden harmonic
\frac{3u_{0}}{\lambda}
in
P^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
is non-tangential, see (
3.21
).

4.2.3
The gauge part

In this section we expand the gauge term
H^{\rho}
. As explained in Sections
3.1.3
and
3.1.4
, it is responsible for polarization conditions for each wave in (
4.1
) and for a generalised wave gauge condition linked to the equation for the remainder
\mathfrak{h}
. The generalised wave gauge is equivalent to setting a group of problematic terms to 0, and these terms are grouped in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
below.

Proposition 4.3
.

The gauge term
H^{\rho}
admits the expansion

\displaystyle H^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\lambda(H^{(1)})^{\rho}+\lambda^{2}\left((H^{(2)})^{\rho}+\Upsilon^{\rho}\right)+\lambda^{3}(H^{(\geq 3)})^{\rho},.

(4.24)

where

\displaystyle(H^{(1)})^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}\left(F^{(2,1)}\right)+g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)+(\tilde{H}^{(1,1)})^{\rho}\right)

(4.25)

\displaystyle\quad+\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-2g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}\left(F^{(2,2)}\right)-\frac{1}{4}g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\right),

\displaystyle(H^{(2)})^{\rho}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}\left(\partial_{\theta}g^{(3)}\right)+(\tilde{H}^{(2)})^{\rho},

(4.26)

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\rho}

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\nu}g^{\rho\sigma}\left(\partial_{\mu}\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}\mathfrak{h}_{\mu\nu}+\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}\mathfrak{F}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}\mathfrak{F}_{\mu\nu}\right)+\lambda\left(\tilde{\partial}_{\mu}g^{(3)}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\partial}_{\sigma}g^{(3)}_{\mu\nu}\right)\right)

(4.27)

\displaystyle\quad-g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathfrak{h}^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}(g_{0})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}(g_{0})_{\mu\nu}-\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}\right)\right),

with lower order terms given schematically by

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{(1,1)}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{-2}\left(\partial F^{(1)}+g_{0}^{-1}F^{(1)}\partial g_{0}\right),

(4.28)

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{-3}\left((1+g_{0}^{-1})(F^{(1)})^{2}+F^{(2,i)}+\mathfrak{F}\right)(\partial g_{0}+\partial u_{0}F^{(1)})

(4.29)

\displaystyle\quad+g_{0}^{-3}F^{(1)}(\partial F^{(1)}+\partial u_{0}F^{(2,i)}+\partial u_{0}\mathfrak{F})+g_{0}^{-2}\partial F^{(2,i)},

\displaystyle H^{(\geq 3)}

\displaystyle=(g^{-2})^{(\geq 3)}\left(\partial g_{0}+\partial u_{0}F^{(1)}\right)

(4.30)

\displaystyle\quad+(g^{-2})^{(\geq 2)}\left(\partial F^{(1)}+\partial u_{0}F^{(2,i)}+\partial u_{0}\mathfrak{F}\right)+(g^{-2})^{(\geq 1)}\partial F^{(2,i)}

\displaystyle\quad+g^{-1}(g^{-1})^{(\geq 1)}\partial u_{0}\partial_{\theta}g^{(3)}.

Remark 4.5
.

Concretely,
\Upsilon^{\rho}
contains all derivatives of
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{F}
present in
H^{\rho}
. Derivatives of
\mathfrak{F}
are also problematic since the analysis of the equations will show that
\partial\mathfrak{F}
is at the level of
\partial\mathfrak{h}
. This also explains the presence of the
\tilde{\partial}g^{(3)}
terms since
g^{(3)}
will involve
\mathfrak{F}
. The second line in (
4.27
) is also linked to
g^{(3)}
and will be fully explained in Remark
5.2
.

For clarity, we denote by
\mathring{H}
the part of
H
containing only allowed terms from the wave equation perspective, that is

\displaystyle\mathring{H}^{\rho}=H^{\rho}-\lambda^{2}\Upsilon^{\rho}.

(4.31)

4.2.4
Expression of the Ricci tensor

We conclude this section by putting together Propositions
4.1
,
4.2
and
4.3
. Before that, we define the main transport operator

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}\vcentcolon=-2\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}+\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0},

(4.32)

where we recall that
\mathbf{D}
is the covariant derivative associated to
g_{0}
. It acts on tensor fields of all type, including scalar functions.

Proposition 4.4
.

Under the assumptions (
4.9
), the Ricci tensor of
g
admits the expansion

\displaystyle R_{\alpha\beta}=R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda^{2}R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}.

The term
R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}
is given by

\displaystyle 2R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}(g_{0})_{\alpha\beta}+P_{\alpha\beta}(g_{0})(\partial g_{0},\partial g_{0})-\frac{1}{4}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0}

(4.33)

\displaystyle\quad+\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\left(\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta)}\left(F^{(2,1)}\right)+\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta)}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)+\tilde{H}^{(1,1)}_{\beta)}+\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{\beta)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\left(4\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta)}\left(F^{(2,2)}\right)+\frac{3}{8}\partial_{\beta)}u_{0}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\right).

The term
R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
is given by

\displaystyle 2R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\bigg(-\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(2,1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\tilde{W}^{(1,1)}_{\alpha\beta}+P^{(1,1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\mathscr{L}_{0}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}-\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

(4.34)

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 113.81102pt+\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\left(\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(2,1)}\right)+\mathrm{Pol}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)+\tilde{H}^{(1,1)}\right)_{\beta)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\Bigg(-2\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(2,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+2\tilde{W}^{(1,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+P^{(1,2)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 85.35826pt+\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\left(-2\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(2,2)}\right)-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{d}u_{0}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\right)_{\beta)}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\hat{P}^{(1,1)}_{\beta)}+\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\hat{P}^{(1,2)}_{\beta)}+\cos\left(\frac{3u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\hat{P}_{\beta)}^{(1,3)}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathfrak{F}_{L_{0}L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(2,2)}_{L_{0}L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}\left(F^{(2,1)}\right)+g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)+(\tilde{H}^{(1,1)})^{\rho}\right)\partial_{\rho}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad-2\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(F^{(2,2)}\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad+\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\left(\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta)}\left(\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(3)}\right)+(g_{0})_{\rho\beta)}\partial_{\theta}(\tilde{H}^{(2)})^{\rho}+(g_{0})_{\rho\beta)}\partial_{\theta}(\Upsilon^{\rho})^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(1)}_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}u_{0}\left(g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}\left(F^{(2,1)}\right)+g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)+(\tilde{H}^{(1,1)})^{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-4\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)F^{(1)}_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}u_{0}g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}\left(F^{(2,2)}\right).

The term
R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}
is given by

\displaystyle 2R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha\beta}-\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}-\lambda\tilde{\Box}_{g}g^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta}-\tilde{W}^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}

(4.35)

\displaystyle\quad+P^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\left(H^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\beta}+g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}H^{\rho}\right)^{(\geq 2)}.

Remark 4.6
.

As discussed in Section
3.1.3
, the high-frequency expansion of the Ricci tensor a priori contains a term of order
\lambda^{-1}
. This term is zero thanks to
\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(1)}\right)=0
which follows again from (
4.9
).

4.3
Towards the hierarchy

In this section, we give a first version of the various partial differential equations and algebraic conditions we impose on each tensors in (
4.1
) so that
g
solves (
1.1
). We thus deduce them from the requirements that

\displaystyle R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}=0,\quad R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}=0\quad\text{and}\quad R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}=0.

The final version will be properly given in Section
5
, where the modification of the coupling between
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{F}
will be introduced (see Section
3.2.3
for where this modification is first discussed).

4.3.1
Ensuring
R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}=0

Canceling the non-oscillating term and the two oscillating terms in (
4.33
) is enough to ensure
R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}=0
. Set to zero, the non-oscillating term is a wave equation for the background metric
g_{0}
with
F^{(1)}
as source term

\displaystyle-\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}(g_{0})_{\alpha\beta}+P_{\alpha\beta}(g_{0})(\partial g_{0},\partial g_{0})-\frac{1}{4}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\partial_{\alpha}u_{0}\partial_{\beta}u_{0}=0.

(4.36)

Given the equation (
2.8
) satisfied by
g_{0}
, we see that (
4.36
) is equivalent to the following quadratic condition for
F^{(1)}

\displaystyle\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}

\displaystyle=8F_{0}^{2}.

(4.37)

Recall also that all the computations of Section
4
(and in particular Proposition
4.4
) are performed under the assumptions (
4.9
) on
F^{(1)}
. Canceling the
\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
term in (
4.33
) is done by imposing the following transport equation for
F^{(1)}

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(1)}

\displaystyle=0,

(4.38)

and the following polarization conditions on
F^{(2,1)}
and
\mathfrak{F}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(2,1)}\right)

\displaystyle=-\tilde{H}^{(1,1)}-\hat{P}^{(0,1)},

(4.39)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

(4.40)

Canceling the
\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
term in (
4.33
) is done by imposing the following polarization conditions on
F^{(2,2)}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(2,2)}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{32}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{d}u_{0}.

For convenience, we define the following tensors

\displaystyle V^{(2,1)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(2,1)}\right)+\tilde{H}^{(1,1)}+\hat{P}^{(0,1)},

(4.41)

\displaystyle V^{(2,2)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(2,2)}\right)+\frac{3}{32}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{d}u_{0},

(4.42)

so that the polarization conditions on
F^{(2,1)}
and
F^{(2,2)}
simply rewrite

\displaystyle V^{(2,1)}=0\quad\text{and}\quad V^{(2,2)}=0.

(4.43)

4.3.2
Ensuring
R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}=0

Before deriving equations and conditions from the requirement
R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}=0
, let us rewrite
R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
with the help of the tensors
V^{(2,1)}
and
V^{(2,2)}
. From (
4.34
), (
4.41
) and (
4.42
) we see that

\displaystyle 2R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(2,1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\tilde{W}^{(1,1)}_{\alpha\beta}+P^{(1,1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{\beta)}-\mathscr{L}_{0}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}-\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}\right)

(4.44)

\displaystyle\quad+\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\Bigg(-2\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(2,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+2\tilde{W}^{(1,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+P^{(1,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}F^{(1)}_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}u_{0}\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 142.26378pt-\frac{1}{2}\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{16}\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\left(\mathrm{d}u_{0}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\right)_{\beta)}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}\bigg(\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta)}\left(\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(3)}\right)+(g_{0})_{\rho\beta)}\partial_{\theta}(\tilde{H}^{(2)})^{\rho}+(g_{0})_{\rho\beta)}\partial_{\theta}(\Upsilon^{\rho})^{(0)}

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 85.35826pt+\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{P}^{(1,1)}_{\beta)}+\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{P}^{(1,2)}_{\beta)}+\cos\left(\frac{3u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{P}_{\beta)}^{(1,3)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\left(V^{(2,1)}+\mathrm{Pol}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)\right)_{\beta)}-2\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}V^{(2,2)}_{\beta)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\left(V^{(2,1)}_{L_{0}}+2\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}-g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\left(V^{(2,1)}_{\sigma}+\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)\right)F^{(1)}_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}u_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-4\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}F^{(1)}_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}u_{0}V^{(2,2)}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)-2\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\right)V^{(2,2)}_{L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta},

where we also used

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{L_{0}L_{0}}=-\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}(\mathfrak{F}),

and

\displaystyle F^{(2,2)}_{L_{0}L_{0}}=-\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}\left(F^{(2,2)}\right)=-V^{(2,2)}_{L_{0}},

which follows from (
4.42
). We see that the terms in (
4.44
) can be put in three categories that are canceled by different mechanisms.

•

The first two lines can be set to zero by imposing transport equations for
F^{(2,1)}
,
F^{(2,2)}
and
\mathfrak{F}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(2,1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{W}^{(1,1)}_{\alpha\beta}+P^{(1,1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{\beta)},

(4.45)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(2,2)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\tilde{W}^{(1,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}P^{(1,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{4}g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}F^{(1)}_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}u_{0}\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{\sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{32}\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\left(\mathrm{d}u_{0}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\right)_{\beta)},

(4.46)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}.

(4.47)

•

The third line is composed of non-tangential terms and can be set to zero by imposing the following polarization condition on
g^{(3)}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}\left(\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(3)}\right)=-(g_{0})_{\rho\beta}\partial_{\theta}(\tilde{H}^{(2)})^{\rho}-\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{P}^{(1,1)}_{\beta}-\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{P}^{(1,2)}_{\beta}-\cos\left(\frac{3u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{P}_{\beta}^{(1,3)}.

(4.48)

Note that we deliberately leave
(g_{0})_{\rho\beta)}\partial_{\theta}(\Upsilon^{\rho})^{(0)}
aside. Moreover, note that the RHS of (
4.48
) does not depend on
\mathfrak{h}
, thanks to the addition of the last line in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
(see Remark
5.2
for why we want
g^{(3)}
to be independent from
\mathfrak{h}
).

•

The last four lines are proportional to either
V^{(2,1)}
,
V^{(2,2)}
or
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathfrak{F})
, which will eventually vanish according to the previous discussion on ensuring
R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}=0
, see (
4.40
) and (
4.43
).

4.3.3
Ensuring
R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}=0

From (
4.35
), we see that
R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}=0
is ensured if we impose the following wave equation for the remainder

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}-\lambda\tilde{\Box}_{g}g^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta}-\tilde{W}^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}+P^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\left(\mathring{H}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\beta}+g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}\mathring{H}^{\rho}\right)^{(\geq 2)},

(4.49)

and the following generalised wave gauge condition

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\rho}=0,

where we recall that
H^{\rho}=\mathring{H}^{\rho}+\lambda^{2}\Upsilon^{\rho}
.

Remark 4.7
.

As explained in depth in Section
3.2.3
, (
4.47
) and (
4.49
) are not the final version of the equations that
\mathfrak{F}
and
\mathfrak{h}
will solve, see Section
5.2
below.

5
The hierarchy of equations

In this section, we give the final version of the equations that the different terms in (
4.1
) solve. We divide them into two groups of equations, the background system presented in Section
5.1
and the reduced system presented in Section
5.2
. Note that
g^{(3)}
is defined in Section
5.4
but doesn’t solve a differential equation.

5.1
The background system

The
background system
is the system satisfied by
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,1)}
and
F^{(2,2)}
. According to (
4.38
), (
4.45
) and (
4.46
) it writes

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=0,

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(2,1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{W}^{(1,1)}_{\alpha\beta}+P^{(1,1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{\beta)},

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(2,2)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\tilde{W}^{(1,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}P^{(1,2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{4}g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}F^{(1)}_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}u_{0}\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{\sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\hat{P}^{(0,1)}_{L_{0}}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{32}\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}\left(\mathrm{d}u_{0}\left|F^{(1)}\right|^{2}_{g_{0}}\right)_{\beta)}.

(5.3)

The background system admits a triangular structure. It will be solved in Section
6
and all the estimates and support properties of the background perturbations are given in Theorem
6.1
. Since (
5.1
), (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) are first order transport equation, the initial data for the background system consist in

\displaystyle F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}\quad\text{and}\quad F^{(2,i)}_{\alpha\beta}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}.

They are given in Section
5.5.1
.

5.2
The reduced system

We present now the system solved by the tensors
\mathfrak{F}
and
\mathfrak{h}
, called the
reduced system
. We already derived equations for
\mathfrak{F}
and
\mathfrak{h}
in Sections
4.3.2
and
4.3.3
, namely (
4.47
) and (
4.49
). However, as explained in Section
3.2.3
, the coupled system (
4.47
)-(
4.49
) is ill-posed and we need to modify it. We introduce the Fourier cut-offs
\Pi_{\leq}
and
\Pi_{\geq}
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\chi_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow[0,1]
be a smooth function supported in

\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}~\middle|~|\xi|\leq 2\right\},

and such that
\chi_{1}(\xi)=1
if
|\xi|\leq 1
. For
\lambda>0
we define
\chi_{\lambda}(\xi)=\chi_{1}(\lambda\xi)
, thus
\chi_{\lambda}
is supported in
\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}~\middle|~|\xi|\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\right\}
and
\chi_{\lambda}(\xi)=1
if
|\xi|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}
. We define
\Pi_{\leq}
by

\displaystyle\Pi_{\leq}(f)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\chi_{\lambda}\mathcal{F}(f)\right),

for
f\in L^{2}
. We also define

\displaystyle\Pi_{\geq}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}-\Pi_{\leq}.

The reduced system, i.e the equations for
\mathfrak{F}
and
\mathfrak{h}
, is then

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta},

(5.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}-\lambda\tilde{\Box}_{g}g^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{\lambda}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\Pi_{\geq}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{R}(g),

(5.5)

where we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(g)

\displaystyle=-\tilde{W}^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}+\left(\mathring{H}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\beta}+g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}\mathring{H}^{\rho}\right)^{(\geq 2)}+P^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}.

(5.6)

Since (
5.4
) is a first order transport equation and (
5.5
) is a second order wave equation the initial data for the reduced system consist in

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}},\quad\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha\beta}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha\beta}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}.

They are given in Section
5.5.2
.

5.3
Generalised wave gauge and polarization conditions

As it is usual when solving the Einstein equations, we impose gauge conditions. Since we solve a wave equation for
\mathfrak{h}
, we need a generalised wave gauge. Moreover, we also impose polarization conditions on
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,i)}
and
\mathfrak{F}
. These various algebraic conditions appeared naturally in the discussions of Sections
4.3.1
,
4.3.2
and
4.3.3
and we recall them for convenience.

Definition 5.2
.

The generalised wave gauge considered in Theorem
2.2
is defined by

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\rho}=0,

(5.7)

where
\Upsilon^{\rho}
is defined in (
4.27
).

The purpose of this generalised wave gauge is to remove from the RHS of the wave equation for
\mathfrak{h}
any second order derivatives of
\mathfrak{h}
, which would induce a loss of derivatives in the standard energy estimate. For later use, we define
\tilde{\Upsilon}^{\rho}
as the terms in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
independent from
\partial\mathfrak{h}
, i.e

\displaystyle\tilde{\Upsilon}^{\rho}

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\nu}g^{\rho\sigma}\left(\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}\mathfrak{F}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}\mathfrak{F}_{\mu\nu}\right)+\lambda\left(\tilde{\partial}_{\mu}g^{(3)}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\partial}_{\sigma}g^{(3)}_{\mu\nu}\right)\right)

(5.8)

\displaystyle\quad-g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathfrak{h}^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}(g_{0})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}(g_{0})_{\mu\nu}-\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}\right)\right).

The polarization conditions prescribe the polarization tensors of
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,i)}
and
\mathfrak{F}
.

Definition 5.3
.

The polarization conditions are

\displaystyle V^{(2,j)}_{\beta}=0,

(5.9)

where
V^{(2,j)}
are defined in (
4.41
) and (
4.42
), together with and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}\left(F^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

(5.10)

\displaystyle F^{(1)}_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}

\displaystyle=0,

(5.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}(\mathfrak{F})

\displaystyle=0.

(5.12)

Remark 5.1
.

Note that (
4.9
) is equivalent to (
5.10
)-(
5.11
).

5.4
Definition of
g^{(3)}

Unlike the other terms in (
4.1
), the tensor
g^{(3)}
doesn’t solve any differential equation but only a polarization condition, already given in (
4.48
), which we recall for convenience:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}\left(\partial_{\theta}^{2}g^{(3)}\right)=-(g_{0})_{\rho\beta}\partial_{\theta}(\tilde{H}^{(2)})^{\rho}-\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{P}^{(1,1)}_{\beta}-\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{P}^{(1,2)}_{\beta}-\cos\left(\frac{3u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{P}_{\beta}^{(1,3)}.

(5.13)

Since the RHS of (
5.13
) is clearly purely oscillating (note that any
\partial_{\theta}
-derivative is purely oscillating), it is possible to integrate twice (
5.13
) with respect to the oscillating variable and obtain
g^{(3)}
if and only if we know how to solve an equation of the form

\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}(T)=\Omega_{\beta},

(5.14)

with
\Omega_{\beta}
a 1-form and for
T
a symmetric 2-tensor. According to (
3.6
), (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) we just need to set

\displaystyle T_{L_{0}L_{0}}

\displaystyle=-\Omega_{L_{0}},

\displaystyle T_{L_{0}A}

\displaystyle=-\Omega_{A},

\displaystyle T_{11}

\displaystyle=-\Omega_{\underline{L}_{0}},

with all the other coefficients of
T
being zero. This implies that we can solve (
5.14
) for
T
satisfying
|T|\lesssim|\Omega|
with an implicit constant depending only on the background spacetime.

Thanks to (
4.29
) and (
4.22
), the RHS of (
5.13
) depends on the background quantities
g_{0}
,
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,i)}
and their first derivatives and on the non-background field
\mathfrak{F}
. However, it does not depend on
\mathfrak{h}
. We summarize this discussion in the next lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

Given
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,i)}
and
\mathfrak{F}
, there exists a symmetric 2-tensor
g^{(3)}
solution of (
5.13
) of the form

\displaystyle g^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathrm{T}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(g^{(3,\mathrm{T})}(\mathfrak{F})+g^{(3,\mathrm{T},\mathrm{BG})}\right),

where
\mathcal{A}
is a finite subset of trigonometric functions (see (
2.2
)),
g^{(3,\mathrm{T})}(\mathfrak{F})
satisfies schematically

\displaystyle g^{(3,\mathrm{T})}(\mathfrak{F})=g_{0}g_{0}^{-3}\left(\partial g_{0}+\partial u_{0}F^{(1)}\right)\mathfrak{F},

(5.15)

and where
g^{(3,\mathrm{T},\mathrm{BG})}
is a polynomial in the background quantities
g_{0}
,
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,i)}
and their first derivatives. In the rest of this article, we will use the notation

\displaystyle g^{(3)}(\mathfrak{F})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathrm{T}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)g^{(3,\mathrm{T})}(\mathfrak{F}),

\displaystyle g^{(3,\mathrm{BG})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathrm{T}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)g^{(3,\mathrm{T},\mathrm{BG})}.

Note that since
g^{(3)}
only absorbs oscillating terms, it has the same support as
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,i)}
or
\mathfrak{F}
, which will be proved to be compact in this article.

5.5
Initial data for the spacetime metric

In this section, we define the initial data on
\Sigma_{0}
for the different tensors appearing in the high-frequency ansatz (
4.1
). They are based on the solution of the constraint equations presented in Theorem
2.1
, which is the main result of
[
Tou23b
]
.

5.5.1
Initial data for the background system

We start with the tensors
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,1)}
and
F^{(2,2)}
. For the spatial components, we simply set

\displaystyle F^{(1)}_{ij}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=\bar{F}^{(1)}_{ij},\quad F^{(2,1)}_{ij}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=\bar{F}^{(2,1)}_{ij},\quad F^{(2,2)}_{ij}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=\bar{F}^{(2,2)}_{ij}.

(5.16)

For the time components of
F^{(1)}
, we set

\displaystyle F^{(1)}_{0\alpha}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0,

(5.17)

which is consistent with (
4.9
). For the time components of
F^{(2,i)}
, we need to ensure that
F^{(2,i)}
satisfy on
\Sigma_{0}
the polarization conditions (
5.9
). For clarity, we define the 1-forms
\Omega^{(i)}
for
i=1,2
so that

\displaystyle V^{(2,i)}_{\beta}=\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}\left(F^{(2,i)}\right)-\Omega^{(i)}_{\beta}.

(5.18)

Note from (
4.41
), (
4.42
), (
4.28
) and (
4.20
) that
\Omega^{(i)}
involve only
g_{0}
and its derivatives or
F^{(1)}
and its first derivatives
\partial F^{(1)}
. For
\nabla F^{(1)}
, the initial data on
\Sigma_{0}
are obtained by differentiating directly
F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
defined in (
5.16
) and (
5.17
) and for
\partial_{t}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
they are obtained by rewriting the equation we want
F^{(1)}
to satisfy, that is (
5.1
) which rewrites as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-N_{0}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+(\partial_{t}+N_{0})^{\rho}\Gamma(g_{0})^{\mu}_{\rho(\alpha}F^{(1)}_{\mu\beta)}+\frac{1}{2|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}}(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0})F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta},

(5.19)

where we used the fact that on
\Sigma_{0}
we have
L_{0}=|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}\left(\partial_{t}+N_{0}\right)
. At this stage, the RHS of (
5.19
) is fully defined on
\Sigma_{0}
, which implies that this is also the case for
\Omega^{(i)}
. It remains to define the initial data for
F^{(2,i)}_{0\alpha}
for
i=1,2
by

\displaystyle F^{(2,i)}_{0A}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=-\bar{F}^{(2,i)}_{AN_{0}}-\frac{1}{|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}}\Omega^{(i)}_{A}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}},

(5.20)

\displaystyle F^{(2,i)}_{0N_{0}}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}^{(2,i)}_{N_{0}N_{0}}-\frac{1}{2|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}^{2}}\Omega^{(i)}_{L_{0}}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}},

(5.21)

\displaystyle F^{(2,i)}_{00}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=0.

(5.22)

Note that these definitions only ensure

\displaystyle V_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}^{(2,i)}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0,

(5.23)

thanks to (
3.6
) and (
3.7
). The fact that
V^{(2,i)}_{\underline{L}_{0}}
also vanishes on
\Sigma_{0}
is the content of Lemma
8.4
.

5.5.2
Initial data for the reduced system

We define here the initial data for the solution of the reduced system. For the tensor
\mathfrak{F}
we simply set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=0.

(5.24)

Before we look at
\mathfrak{h}
, note that with (
5.24
) we can apply Lemma
5.1
and fully compute
g^{(3)}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}
. Indeed it is independent from
\mathfrak{h}
and depends only on
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,i)}
and their derivatives which can be computed on
\Sigma_{0}
by looking at the transport equations we want them to satisfy, that is (
5.1
), (
5.2
) and (
5.3
).

Since
\mathfrak{h}
solves a second order equation we need to prescribe
\mathfrak{h}
and
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}
on
\Sigma_{0}
. We start with the spatial components of
\mathfrak{h}
, defined so that the induced metric on
\Sigma_{0}
of the spacetime metric
g
defined by (
4.1
) is precisely the Riemannian metric
\bar{g}
obtained in Theorem
2.1
. This imposes

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{ij}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}-\lambda g^{(3)}_{ij}.

(5.25)

where
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}
is given in (
2.17
).

Remark 5.2
.

The definition (
5.25
) is the reason why we need
g^{(3)}
to be independent of
\mathfrak{h}
. This requirement led to the presence of the term

\displaystyle-g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\mathfrak{h}^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}(g_{0})_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}(g_{0})_{\mu\nu}-\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}\right)\right),

(5.26)

in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
(see (
4.27
)). Otherwise, this term would have been present in the RHS of (
5.13
), leading to
g^{(3)}
being a function of
\mathfrak{h}
. Defining
\mathfrak{h}_{ij}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}
would have thus required to invert a system of equations of the form

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}+\lambda g^{(3)}(\mathfrak{h})=\bar{\mathfrak{h}}.

If
\lambda
is small enough, one can easily solve this system of equations but we prefered to avoid this and to include (
5.26
) in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
.

For the time components of
\mathfrak{h}
, we choose them so that they compensate the one of
g^{(3)}
, that is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{0\alpha}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=-\lambda g^{(3)}_{0\alpha}.

(5.27)

Note that we can now obtain the initial data for the derivatives of
\mathfrak{F}
. Indeed, (
5.24
) obviously gives us that
\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0
and since we want
\mathfrak{F}
to solve (
5.4
), the time derivative of
\mathfrak{F}
is initially given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

(5.28)

where we also used (
5.24
) to simplify (
5.4
).

We now to define the initial data for
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha\beta}
. The spatial components, i.e
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}
, are chosen so that the second fundamental form of
\Sigma_{0}
as an hypersurface in
(\mathcal{M},g)
is given by
K
obtained in Theorem
2.1
. The second fundamental form is defined by
-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g
where
T
is the unit normal to
\Sigma_{0}
for
g
and
\mathcal{L}
is the Lie derivative.
A priori
, the unit normal
T
depends on
\lambda
. This shows that in order to obtain the dependence of
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}
on
\lambda
, we need to know more about
T
.

Lemma 5.2
.

The future unit normal
T
to
\Sigma_{0}
for the metric
g
given by (
4.1
) satisfies

\displaystyle T=\partial_{t}+Z,

(5.29)

with the vector field
Z=Z^{t}\partial_{t}+\bar{Z}^{i}\partial_{i}
given by

\displaystyle Z^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\lambda^{4}\bar{g}^{ij}g^{(2)}_{0i}g^{(2)}_{0j}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-1,

(5.30)

\displaystyle\bar{Z}^{i}

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{2}\bar{g}^{ij}g^{(2)}_{0j}\left(1+\lambda^{4}\bar{g}^{ij}g^{(2)}_{0i}g^{(2)}_{0j}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(5.31)

Proof.

Since the unit normal to a spacelike hypersurface for a given Lorentzian metric is unique, we just need to prove that we can construct the vector field
Z
such that
T
defined by (
5.29
) satisfies

\displaystyle g(T,T)=-1\quad\text{and}\quad g(T,\partial_{i})=0.

Note that thanks to (
5.17
), (
5.22
), (
5.24
) and (
5.27
) and the fact that
\partial_{t}
is the unit normal to
\Sigma_{0}
for
g_{0}
we have
g_{00}=-1
and
g_{0i}=\lambda^{2}g^{(2)}_{0i}
. This gives

\displaystyle g(T,\partial_{i})

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}g^{(2)}_{0i}\left(1+Z^{t}\right)+\bar{g}_{ij}\bar{Z}^{j},

so we impose

\displaystyle\bar{Z}^{i}

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{2}\bar{g}^{ij}g^{(2)}_{0j}\left(1+Z^{t}\right).

(5.32)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle g(T,T)

\displaystyle=-1+2g_{0\alpha}Z^{\alpha}+g_{\alpha\beta}Z^{\alpha}Z^{\beta},

so we impose
2g_{0\alpha}Z^{\alpha}+g_{\alpha\beta}Z^{\alpha}Z^{\beta}=0
. After expanding the quadratic term and inserting (
5.32
) this condition rewrites as a quadratic equation for
Z^{t}

\displaystyle\left(1+\lambda^{4}\bar{g}^{ij}g^{(2)}_{0i}g^{(2)}_{0j}\right)\left(Z^{t}\right)^{2}+2\left(1+\lambda^{4}\bar{g}^{ij}g^{(2)}_{0i}g^{(2)}_{0j}\right)Z^{t}+\lambda^{4}\bar{g}^{ij}g^{(2)}_{0i}g^{(2)}_{0j}=0.

The two roots of this equation are

\displaystyle\pm\left(1+\lambda^{4}\bar{g}^{ij}g^{(2)}_{0i}g^{(2)}_{0j}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-1.

Since we want
T
to be future directed, we choose the root where
\pm=+
, i.e (
5.30
). Inserting this into (
5.32
) gives (
5.31
).
∎

We now compute the expression of the second fundamental form of
\Sigma_{0}
as an hypersurface in
(\mathcal{M},g)
with
g
being given by (
4.1
).

Lemma 5.3
.

If
g
is given by (
4.1
) and if
\partial_{t}F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
is given by (
5.19
) then on
\Sigma_{0}
the following holds

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g_{ij}

\displaystyle=K^{(0)}_{\lambda}+\lambda K^{(1)}_{\lambda}+\lambda^{2}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}\mathfrak{h}+\tilde{K}_{evol}^{(\geq 2)}\right),

where
K^{(0)}_{\lambda}
and
K^{(1)}_{\lambda}
are given by (
2.19
) and (
2.20
). Moreover, we have schematically

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\tilde{K}_{evol}^{(\geq 2)}

\displaystyle=Z^{\alpha}\partial g_{0}+\lambda Z^{\alpha}\partial g^{(1)}+\lambda^{2}\left(\partial\mathfrak{F}+\partial F^{(2,i)}+Z^{\alpha}\partial g^{(2)}\right)+\lambda^{3}Tg^{(3)}.

(5.33)

Proof.

Recalling that
g
is defined by (
4.1
) we have

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g_{0}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g^{(1)}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g^{(2)}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}\mathfrak{h}-\frac{\lambda^{3}}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g^{(3)}.

We compute or schematically estimate each term in this expression, using the expansion of
T
given in Lemma
5.2
. The latter gives

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g_{0}=K_{0}+O\left(Z^{\alpha}\partial g_{0}\right),

where
Z^{\alpha}
denotes either
Z^{t}
or
\bar{Z}^{i}
and where we used the fact that
\partial_{t}
is the unit normal to
\Sigma_{0}
for
g_{0}
. Similarly, using
\partial_{t}u_{0}=|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}
on
\Sigma_{0}
we have

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g^{(1)}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}\bar{F}^{(1)}_{ij}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{t}F^{(1)}_{ij}+O\left(\lambda Z^{\alpha}\partial g^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}\bar{F}^{(1)}_{ij}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\lambda}{2}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-N_{0}\bar{F}^{(1)}_{ij}+(\partial_{t}+N_{0})^{\rho}\Gamma(g_{0})^{k}_{\rho(i}\bar{F}^{(1)}_{kj)}+\frac{1}{2|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}}(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0})\bar{F}^{(1)}_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+O\left(\lambda Z^{\alpha}\partial g^{(1)}\right),

where we used (
5.19
) and (
5.17
) to compute
\partial_{t}F^{(1)}_{ij}
and (
5.16
) to compute
F^{(1)}_{ij}
. Using in addition (
5.24
) we now obtain

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g^{(2)}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda}{2}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}\bar{F}^{(2,1)}_{ij}+\lambda\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}\bar{F}^{(2,2)}_{ij}

\displaystyle\quad+O\left(\lambda^{2}\left(\partial\mathfrak{F}+\partial F^{(2,i)}+Z^{\alpha}\partial g^{(2)}\right)\right).

This concludes the proof of the lemma, by recalling the expression of
K^{(i)}_{\lambda}
for
i=1,2
given in (
2.19
) and (
2.20
).
∎

As explained above, we want the initial data for
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}
to ensure that the second fundamental form of
\Sigma_{0}
for
g
, i.e
-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}g_{ij}
, matches the tensor
K_{\lambda}
given by Theorem
2.1
. According to the previous lemma, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{T}\mathfrak{h}+\tilde{K}^{(\geq 2)}_{evol}=K^{(\geq 2)}_{\lambda},

(5.34)

where
K_{\lambda}^{(\geq 2)}
is given in Theorem
2.1
. Using the expansion of
T
, this defines the initial data for
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}
on
\Sigma_{0}
by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{1}{1+Z^{t}}\left(-\bar{Z}^{k}\partial_{k}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}+\mathfrak{h}\left([Z,\partial_{(i}],\partial_{j)}\right)-2(K_{\lambda}^{(\geq 2)})_{ij}+2(\tilde{K}_{evol}^{(\geq 2)})_{ij}\right).

(5.35)

It remains to define the initial value for
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{0\alpha}
. They are chosen so that the generalised wave condition (
5.7
) holds on
\Sigma_{0}
. This condition rewrites as

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}\mathfrak{h}_{\mu\nu}\right)=-g_{\sigma\rho}\tilde{\Upsilon}^{\rho}.

(5.36)

Note that the initial data for
\tilde{\Upsilon}^{\rho}
are fully defined. Indeed the last line in (
5.8
) depends on background quantities and on
g\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}
. For the first line, we use (
5.24
) for the tangential derivatives or (
5.28
) for the initial value of
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{F}
. A similar argument using Lemma
5.1
allows us to deal with
\partial g^{(3)}
in
\tilde{\Upsilon}^{\rho}
.

Thanks to the decomposition of
g^{\mu\nu}
on
\Sigma_{0}
, (
5.36
) rewrites as

\displaystyle-T^{\mu}T^{\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}\mathfrak{h}_{\mu\nu}\right)=-g_{\sigma\rho}\tilde{\Upsilon}^{\rho}-\bar{g}^{k\ell}\left(\partial_{k}\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma\ell}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}\mathfrak{h}_{k\ell}\right),

where the RHS is fully defined thanks to (
5.25
), (
5.27
) and (
5.35
). We project this expression onto the initial frame
(T,\partial_{i})
and rearrange terms to obtain the initial value of
T\mathfrak{h}_{TT}
and
T\mathfrak{h}_{iT}
:

\displaystyle T\mathfrak{h}_{TT}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=2\mathfrak{h}_{\mu T}TT^{\mu}+2g_{T\rho}\tilde{\Upsilon}^{\rho}+2T^{\sigma}\bar{g}^{k\ell}\left(\partial_{k}\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma\ell}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}\mathfrak{h}_{k\ell}\right),

(5.37)

\displaystyle T\mathfrak{h}_{iT}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}_{i\nu}TT^{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}T^{\mu}T^{\nu}\partial_{i}\mathfrak{h}_{\mu\nu}+g_{i\rho}\tilde{\Upsilon}^{\rho}+\bar{g}^{k\ell}\left(\partial_{k}\mathfrak{h}_{i\ell}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\mathfrak{h}_{k\ell}\right).

(5.38)

Using the decomposition of
T
given by Lemma
5.2
we can deduce from (
5.37
) and (
5.38
) the initial value of
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{0\alpha}
(similarly to how we obtain (
5.35
) from (
5.34
)).

5.5.3
Initial estimates and properties

In the following corollary, we summarize the formal properties satisfied by the initial data defined in the previous sections and give the estimates they satisfy. It shows in particular that the initial data defined above from the solution of the constraint equations obtained in
[
Tou23b
]
are compatible with the generalised wave gauge condition and the polarization conditions (with the exception of
V^{(2,i)}_{\underline{L}_{0}}=0
).

Corollary 5.1
.

The initial data for the background and reduced systems defined in Sections
5.5.1
and
5.5.2
are such that

(i)

the first and second fundamental forms of the hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
are given by
(\bar{g}_{\lambda},K_{\lambda})
from Theorem
2.1
and therefore solve the constraint equations,

(ii)

the tensors
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,1)}
and
F^{(2,2)}
are initially supported in
\{|x|\leq R\}
and there exists a constant
C_{\mathrm{in}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|F^{(1)}\right\|_{H^{N}(\Sigma_{0})}+\left\|F^{(2,1)}\right\|_{H^{N-1}(\Sigma_{0})}+\left\|F^{(2,2)}\right\|_{H^{N-1}(\Sigma_{0})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{in}}\varepsilon,

(5.39)

\displaystyle\max_{k\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\partial\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}(\Sigma_{0})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{in}}\varepsilon,

(5.40)

(iii)

the following initial polarization conditions hold

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(1)}\right)\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=0,

(5.41)

\displaystyle V^{(2,i)}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=0,

(5.42)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=0,

(5.43)

(iv)

the initial backreaction condition holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\left(F^{(1)},F^{(1)}\right)\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=-4F_{0}^{2},

(5.44)

where
\mathcal{E}
is defined in (
3.19
),

(v)

the initial generalised wave gauge condition holds

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\rho}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=0.

(5.45)

Proof.

The first item of the corollary holds thanks to (
5.16
), (
5.24
), (
5.25
) and (
5.34
). We now turn to the second item the corollary. The support property of
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,1)}
and
F^{(2,2)}
follows directly from the support property of
\bar{F}^{(1)}
,
\bar{F}^{(2,1)}
and
\bar{F}^{(2,2)}
stated in Theorem
2.1
and (
5.16
), (
5.17
), (
5.20
), (
5.21
) and (
5.22
). Similarly, we deduce (
5.39
) from (
2.21
).

Proving (
5.40
) amounts to bound the Sobolev norm of the initial data for
\mathfrak{h}
and
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}
. First, from (
5.25
) and (
5.27
) we can schematically write
\mathfrak{h}=\bar{\mathfrak{h}}+\lambda g^{(3)}
. On
\Sigma_{0}
,
g^{(3)}
is compactly supported and depends only on background quantities (see the discussion following (
5.24
)). The estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r+1}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}(\Sigma_{0})}\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}},

(5.46)

for
r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
thus follows from (
2.23
). We now look at
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}
, defined in (
5.35
). Since
Z=O\left(\lambda^{2}\right)
and only contains background quantities (see (
5.31
) and (
5.30
)) the worse terms in (
5.35
) are the last two (the first two can be estimated with (
5.46
)). For them we use (
2.24
) and (
5.33
) (note that
\tilde{K}^{(\geq 2)}_{evol}
involves the non-background quantities
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{F}
for which we need to use (
5.28
)). This proves that

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}(\Sigma_{0})}\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}},

for
r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
. We proceed similarly for
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}_{0\alpha}
using (
5.37
) and (
5.38
). This concludes the proof of (
5.40
).

The initial polarization conditions (
5.42
) and (
5.43
) are consequences of (
5.20
)-(
5.21
)-(
5.22
) and (
5.24
), while (
5.41
) holds since
F^{(1)}
is initially
P_{0,u}
-tangent and
g_{0}
-traceless (see the corresponding properties of
\bar{F}^{(1)}
in Theorem
2.1
). Samewise, (
5.44
) follows from (
2.22
). The initial generalised wave gauge condition (
5.45
) follows from (
5.37
) and (
5.38
).
∎

5.6
Reformulation of Theorem
2.2

The conclusion of Sections
4
and
5
is the following: proving Theorem
2.2
is equivalent to constructing solutions to both the background and reduced system ((
5.1
)-(
5.3
) and (
5.4
)-(
5.5
) respectively) on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
with initial data given in Section
5.5
, and which satisfies moreover the generalised wave gauge condition and polarization conditions (see Definition
5.2
and
5.3
respectively). The rest of this article is devoted to this task, which we divide in three parts:

•

in Section
6
, we solve the background system (see Theorem
6.1
),

•

in Section
7
, we solve the reduced system (see Theorem
7.1
),

•

in Section
8
, we show that the generalised wave gauge condition and polarization conditions are satisfied (see Propositions
8.2
and
8.3
).

5.7
Basic transport and energy estimates

Before proceeding with this program, we collect here the basic transport estimate for the operator
L_{0}
and the energy estimate for the operator
\tilde{\Box}_{g}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
h
a scalar function on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
for some
T\leq 1
, compactly supported. The following holds for all
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(t)\leq C(C_{0})\left(\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(s)+\left\|L_{0}h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s\right).

(5.47)

Proof.

We start by decomposing
L_{0}
in the
(\partial_{t},\partial_{i})
basis:

\displaystyle L_{0}=L_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}+L_{0}^{i}\partial_{i}.

Recalling (
2.6
) we have

\displaystyle L_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle=-g_{0}^{tt}\partial_{t}u_{0}-g_{0}^{it}\partial_{i}u_{0},

\displaystyle L_{0}^{i}

\displaystyle=-g_{0}^{ti}\partial_{t}u_{0}-g_{0}^{ji}\partial_{j}u_{0}.

Since
u_{0}
satisfies the eikonal equation (
2.4
) we have

\displaystyle g_{0}^{tt}(\partial_{t}u_{0})^{2}+2g_{0}^{ti}\partial_{t}u_{0}\partial_{i}u_{0}+|\nabla u_{0}|^{2}_{\bar{g}_{0}}=0.

Taking
\varepsilon
small enough in (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) then ensures that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{L_{0}^{t}}\right|+\left|L_{0}^{\mu}\right|+\left|\partial L_{0}^{\mu}\right|\leq C(C_{0}),

(5.48)

on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We now prove the energy estimate. After multiplication by
h
, the equation
L_{0}h=f
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}h^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{fh}{L_{0}^{t}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{L_{0}^{i}}{L_{0}^{t}}\partial_{i}h^{2}.

(5.49)

For
s\in[0,T]
we integrate (
5.49
) on
\Sigma_{s}
with respect to the usual Lebesgue measure and integrate by part in the last integral to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma_{s}}\partial_{t}h^{2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{s}}\frac{fh}{L_{0}^{t}}\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma_{s}}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{L_{0}^{i}}{L_{0}^{t}}\right)h^{2}\mathrm{d}x.

(5.50)

In the integral of the LHS, we recognize the time derivative of an energy for
h
. Therefore (
5.50
) leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(s)

\displaystyle=2\int_{\Sigma_{s}}\frac{fh}{L_{0}^{t}}\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Sigma_{s}}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{L_{0}^{i}}{L_{0}^{t}}\right)h^{2}\mathrm{d}x.

Using now the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the first integral in the RHS and (
5.48
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(s)\leq C(C_{0})\left(\left\|h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(s)+\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(s)\right).

Integrating this inequality on
[0,t]
for
t\leq T
concludes the proof.
∎

For clarity we define

\displaystyle E_{\sigma}(h)

\displaystyle=\left\|\partial_{t}h\right\|_{L^{2}_{\sigma}}^{2}+\left\|\nabla h\right\|_{L^{2}_{\sigma}}^{2}

for
h
a scalar function and
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
h
be a scalar function on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, for all
t\in[0,T]
we have

\displaystyle E_{\sigma}(h)(t)\lesssim E_{\sigma}(h)(0)+\left(1+\left\|\partial g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\int_{0}^{t}E_{\sigma}(h)(s)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\tilde{\Box}_{g}h\right\|_{L^{2}_{\sigma}}^{2}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Proof.

Let
w(x)=\left(1+|x|^{2}\right)^{\sigma}
. We set
f=\tilde{\Box}_{g}h
and multiply this identity by
w\partial_{t}h
and integrate over
\Sigma_{t}
for
t\in[0,T]
with the usual Lebesgue measure:

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{t}}wg^{tt}\partial_{t}h\partial^{2}_{t}h\mathrm{d}x+2\int_{\Sigma_{t}}wg^{ti}\partial_{t}h\partial_{i}\partial_{t}h\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Sigma_{t}}wg^{ij}\partial_{t}h\partial_{i}\partial_{j}h\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}w\partial_{t}hf\mathrm{d}x.

We rewrite the first integral and integrate by parts in the second and third integrals to obtain:

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}E}{\mathrm{d}t}-\int_{\Sigma_{t}}w\left(\partial_{t}g^{tt}\right)(\partial_{t}h)^{2}\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\partial_{i}\left(wg^{0i}\right)(\partial_{t}h)^{2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\partial_{i}\left(wg^{ij}\right)\partial_{t}h\partial_{j}h\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}w\left(\partial_{t}g^{ij}\right)\partial_{i}h\partial_{j}h\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}w\partial_{t}hf\mathrm{d}x,

(5.51)

where

\displaystyle E(t)=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}w\left(-g^{tt}(\partial_{t}h)^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}h\partial_{j}h\right).

Taking
\varepsilon
small enough in (
2.9
) we obtain the existence of a universal constant
C^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C^{\prime}}E_{\sigma}(h)\leq E\leq C^{\prime}E_{\sigma}(h).

(5.52)

Using
|\nabla w|\lesssim w
, we obtain
|\partial(wg)|\lesssim\left(1+\left\|\partial g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)w
. Going back to (
5.51
), this implies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}E}{\mathrm{d}t}(t)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+\left\|\partial g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)E_{\sigma}(h)(t)+\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\sigma}}^{2}(t),

where we also used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Integrating this inequality from 0 to
t\in[0,T]
we obtain

\displaystyle E(t)\leq E(0)+\left(1+\left\|\partial g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\int_{0}^{t}E_{\sigma}(h)(s)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\sigma}}^{2}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Using (
5.52
) concludes the proof.
∎

6
Solving the background system

In this section we solve the background system (
5.1
)-(
5.3
) defined in Section
5.1
. The following theorem summarizes the estimates and the support properties of the background perturbations.

Theorem 6.1
.

Given initial data as in Corollary
5.1
and if
\varepsilon
is small enough, there exists a unique solution

\displaystyle\left(F^{(1)},F^{(2,1)},F^{(2,2)}\right),

(6.1)

of the background system (
5.1
)-(
5.3
) on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Moreover:

(i)

the tensors in (
6.1
) are supported in
\{|x|\leq C_{\mathrm{supp}}R\}
,

(ii)

there exists
C_{1}=C_{1}(C_{0})>0
such that the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\left\|F^{(1)}\right\|_{H^{N}}+\left\|F^{(2,1)}\right\|_{H^{N-2}}+\left\|F^{(2,2)}\right\|_{H^{N-2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\varepsilon,

(iii)

F^{(1)}
satisfies (
2.27
), (
2.28
) and (
2.29
).

We want the background perturbations to solve the equations (
5.1
)-(
5.2
)-(
5.3
). The equations solved by their coefficients are of the form

\displaystyle\left(L_{0}+\eta\right)h=f,

(6.2)

for
f
and
\eta
given scalar functions on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
(recall (
4.32
)). Moreover, thanks to Corollary
5.1
the initial data on
\Sigma_{0}
for the background perturbations are compactly supported. For the model equation (
6.2
) this allows us to consider
f
supported in
[0,1]\times K
for
K
a compact of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
h\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}
compactly supported. Since
L_{0}
is the spacetime gradient of the background phase
u_{0}
and thanks to (
2.4
) and (
2.10
) we can define characteristics for the equation (
6.2
) on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The existence of a solution
h
to this equation can thus be proved using the method of characteristics, we don’t give the details.

A solution
h
of (
6.2
) can be proved to be as regular as
f
using its expression given by the characteristics method. However, since the proof of Theorem
2.2
is based on Sobolev spaces, we have derived an estimate for the transport operator
L_{0}
in Lemma
5.4
. As a byproduct, this standard estimate proves the uniqueness of the solution to (
6.2
) and therefore of the solutions to (
5.1
)-(
5.2
)-(
5.3
). These equations have a triangular structure:

•

(
5.1
) only depends on the background quantities through the coefficients of the operator
\mathscr{L}_{0}
,

•

the RHS of (
5.2
) depends in addition on
\partial^{\leq 2}F^{(1)}
,

•

the RHS of (
5.3
) depends on the background quantities,
\partial^{\leq 2}F^{(1)}
and
F^{(2,1)}
.

Moreover, thanks to the support properties stated in Corollary
5.1
, we obtain that the background perturbations are supported in
\{|x|\leq C_{\mathrm{supp}}R\}
. The estimates stated in Theorem
6.1
follow directly from Lemma
5.4
, the estimates of the RHS of (
5.2
)-(
5.3
) we prove in the following lemma and the initial regularity stated in Corollary
5.1
, see (
5.39
).

Lemma 6.1
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\text{RHS of \eqref{eq F21}}\right\|_{H^{N-2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|F^{(1)}\right\|_{H^{N}},

(6.3)

\displaystyle\left\|\text{RHS of \eqref{eq F22}}\right\|_{H^{N-2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\left(\left\|F^{(1)}\right\|_{H^{N}}+\left\|F^{(2,1)}\right\|_{H^{N-2}}\right).

(6.4)

Proof.

The estimates (
6.3
) and (
6.4
) follow from (
4.14
), (
4.15
), (
4.21
) and (
4.20
) and the background regularity stated in Section
2.2
.
∎

7
Solving the reduced system

In this section we solve the reduced system (
5.4
)-(
5.5
). This is done by a bootstrap argument, i.e by showing that we can improve
a priori
estimates using the equations.

7.1
Bootstrap assumptions

As explained in Section
3.2.2
, the reduced system loses one derivative. The addition of the Fourier cut-off
\Pi_{\leq}
in the equation for
\mathfrak{F}
implies that two derivatives of
\mathfrak{F}
are actually at the level of one derivative of
\mathfrak{h}
, which is consistent with what we would obtain from an energy estimate on the wave equation for
\mathfrak{h}
. Indeed, by commuting (
5.4
) with
\nabla^{2}
(the case of time derivatives is similar) we obtain broadly from (
C.12
)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\nabla^{2}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\nabla\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

Putting this in the energy estimate for
\mathfrak{h}
would give
\left\|\nabla\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}T
with
T
the time of existence. Compensating the largeness of
\frac{1}{\lambda}
by the smallness of
T
would prove well-posedness for the system (
5.4
)-(
5.5
) on
[0,T_{\lambda}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
T_{\lambda}\to 0
when
\lambda
tends to 0. This is far from what we want since it doesn’t allow us to consider the high-frequency limit
\lambda\to 0
of
g_{\lambda}
. For this we need a time of existence independent of
\lambda
. In other words, the addition of
\Pi_{\leq}
removes the loss of derivative but does not remove the ”loss of
\lambda
”. This discussion explains our choice of bootstrap assumptions on
\mathfrak{F}
and
\mathfrak{h}
:

•

\mathfrak{F}
is supported in
J_{0}^{+}(\{|x|\leq R\})
and satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\max_{r\in\llbracket 1,6\rrbracket}\lambda^{r-1}\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,5\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\partial_{t}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r+1}\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{1}\varepsilon,

(
BA1
)

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{2}\varepsilon,

(
BA2
)

•

\mathfrak{h}
satisfies

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left(\left\|\partial_{t}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}+\left\|\nabla\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}\right)+\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,3\rrbracket}\lambda^{r+1}\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+2+r}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{3}\varepsilon.

(
BA3
)

These bootstrap assumptions solve the ”loss of
\lambda
” since (
BA2
) implies that
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}
is bounded by
\lambda^{0}
in
L^{2}
while (
BA1
) implies boundedness by
\frac{1}{\lambda}
. Recovering (
BA2
) is the main challenge we face. Note that the support property of
\mathfrak{F}
just follows from the transport equation it satisfies (
5.4
), the fact that its RHS is supported in
J_{0}^{+}(\{|x|\leq R\})
and that
\mathfrak{F}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0
.

As explained above, a local in time solution to (
5.4
)-(
5.5
) exists
a priori
and by taking the constants
A_{i}
large enough (compared to
C_{\mathrm{in}}
of Corollary
5.1
) we can assume that the set of times
t
such that (
BA1
)-(
BA2
)-(
BA3
) are satisfied on
[0,t]
is non-empty. We define
T
to be the supremum of these times. We have
0<T\leq 1
but
a priori

T
depends on
\lambda
. In the next sections, we prove that actually (
BA1
)-(
BA2
)-(
BA3
) hold with better constants on
[0,T]
. This will contradict the definition of
T
and prove that a solution to (
5.4
)-(
5.5
) exists and satisfy (
BA1
)-(
BA2
)-(
BA3
) on
[0,1]
, that is the interval of existence of
g_{0}
,
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,1)}
and
F^{(2,2)}
.

In the sequel, we will always precise the dependence on
\lambda
of the estimates. For
X
a numerical quantity, the notation
C(X)
will denote any function of
X
. The symbol
\lesssim
will denote
\leq C
for
C
depending only on
\delta
,
R
and other irrelevant numerical constants. We already assume that
A_{1}\ll A_{2}\ll A_{3}
and that
A_{i}\gg C(C_{0})
for all the
C(C_{0})
that will appear in the proofs.

Remark 7.1
.

Since
\lambda
can be chosen as small as we want, the bootstrap assumptions (
BA1
) and (
BA3
) imply that
\left\|X\right\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{s}X\right\|_{L^{2}}
for
X\in\{\mathfrak{F},\mathfrak{h}\}
(for
\mathfrak{h}
this holds with the appropriate weights at spacelike infinity), i.e that the main term in each
H^{s}
norm is the norm of the top derivatives. Therefore in the sequel, when using (weighted) Sobolev embeddings we will only write down the norm of the top derivatives.

7.2
First consequences

We give here the first consequences of the bootstrap assumptions (
BA1
)-(
BA2
)-(
BA3
). Note first that the inequality

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1}}(t)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1}}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1}}\mathrm{d}s

together with (
BA3
) implies
\left\|\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1}}\lesssim C_{\mathrm{in}}\varepsilon+TA_{3}\varepsilon
. Since
T\leq 1
and
A_{3}
is assumed to be larger than
C_{\mathrm{in}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1}}\lesssim A_{3}\varepsilon.

(7.1)

We now deduce from the bootstrap assumptions some estimates for the metric
g
. We introduce the following split of the metric
g
:

\displaystyle g=g_{\text{BG}}+\tilde{g},

(7.2)

where

\displaystyle g_{\text{BG}}

\displaystyle=g_{0}+\lambda g^{(1)}+\lambda^{2}\left(F^{(2,1)}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)+F^{(2,2)}\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\right)+\lambda^{3}g^{(3,\mathrm{BG})},

(7.3)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\left(\mathfrak{F}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)+\mathfrak{h}+\lambda g^{(3)}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)\right).

(7.4)

Remark 7.2
.

Note that the term
g^{(3)}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)
in
\tilde{g}
will be omitted in the sequel (with the exception of Lemma
7.5
) since it is basically a linear term in
\mathfrak{F}
(see Lemma
5.1
) with an extra
\lambda
, and thus behaves at least better than
\mathfrak{F}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
.

On the one hand, the metric
g_{\text{BG}}
contains the background metric and all the background perturbations. Thanks to Theorem
6.1
it is very regular but because of
g^{(1)}
its behaviour with respect to
\lambda
is bad. On the other hand, the metric
\tilde{g}
is less regular than
g_{\text{BG}}
but satisfies better estimates in terms of
\lambda
. The following lemma gathers the required properties of
g
,
g_{\mathrm{BG}}
and
\tilde{g}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

The following estimates hold

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,3\rrbracket}\lambda^{r-1}\left\|\nabla^{r}(g-g_{0})\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim A_{3}\varepsilon,

(7.5)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{4}(g_{\mathrm{BG}}-g_{0})\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda^{3}}.

(7.6)

Proof.

Let
r\leq 3
. We have
g-g_{0}=g_{\mathrm{BG}}-g_{0}+\tilde{g}
. For
g_{\mathrm{BG}}-g_{0}
we simply use (
7.3
), the estimates of Theorem
6.1
and Lemma
5.1
to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}(g_{\mathrm{BG}}-g_{0})\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r-1}}.

The proof of (
7.6
) is similar. For
\tilde{g}
we use the weighted Sobolev embedding
H^{2}_{\delta+r}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
of Proposition
2.1
:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{g}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{2}\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\frac{\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\lambda^{r_{2}-2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{2}\left\|\nabla^{r+2}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+2}}+\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\frac{\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}+2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}}{\lambda^{r_{2}-2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{2}\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r+1}}+\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\frac{1}{\lambda^{r_{2}-2}}\times\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{1}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r-1}},

where we used (
BA3
), (
BA1
) and
r\leq 3
.
∎

Also note that the estimates in Lemma
7.1
also hold for the inverse of
g
, which we can also decompose as in (
7.2
). In what follows we won’t make any difference between a coefficient of
g
or a coefficient of its inverse.

We also deduce from the bootstrap assumptions an estimate for
\mathcal{R}(g)
, i.e the term in (
5.5
) containing only non-problematic terms.

Lemma 7.2
.

The following estimate holds

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathcal{R}(g)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}\lesssim C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}.

(7.7)

Proof.

In terms of expansions in powers of
\lambda
, the definition of
\mathcal{R}(g)
(see (
5.6
)) ensures the correct behaviour, that is
\mathcal{R}(g)=O\left(\lambda^{0}\right)
but since it contains oscillating terms one loses a power of
\lambda
for each derivatives. This explains why

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathcal{R}(g)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda^{r}}.

(7.8)

In terms of its dependency on the metric coefficients, we don’t need to be very precise about the exact expression of
\mathcal{R}(g)
, its only important property is that it does not contain second order derivatives of either
\mathfrak{F}
,
g^{(3)}
or
\mathfrak{h}
. Indeed:

•

the quadratic non-linearity term
P^{(\geq 2)}
is of the form
g^{-2}\partial g\partial g
,

•

the
\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}
,
\tilde{\partial}^{2}g^{(3)}
and
\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}
coming from the wave part of the Ricci tensor are already in (
5.5
) and are thus absent from
\tilde{W}^{(\geq 2)}
,

•

the
\partial\mathfrak{F}
,
\tilde{\partial}g^{(3)}
and
\partial\mathfrak{h}
coming from the
H^{\rho}
term in the Ricci tensor are already put into
\Upsilon^{\rho}
and therefore absent from
\mathring{H}^{\rho}
, which implies that the
\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}
,
\tilde{\partial}^{2}g^{(3)}
and
\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}
coming from the gauge part are absent from
\mathcal{R}(g)
.

Therefore, the terms in
\mathcal{R}(g)
can be put into two categories: first the purely background terms, i.e depends only on
g_{0}
,
F^{(1)}
or
F^{(2,i)}
and their first and second derivatives, and second the non-background terms, depending quadratically on zeroth or first order derivatives of
\mathfrak{F}
or
\mathfrak{h}
with background coefficients.

We can bound all of terms from the first category in
L^{\infty}
thanks to the background regularity, Theorem
6.1
and the fact that
N\geq 10
(see Remark
3.4
). Since these terms are all background, the constant appearing in the estimate is of the form
C(C_{0})
.

The terms from the second category can all be estimated using the bootstrap assumptions (
BA1
) and (
BA3
) and by bounding the background coefficients in
L^{\infty}
. We only give the details for the worse term in terms of
\lambda
behaviour and spatial support, i.e
\lambda^{2}g_{0}^{-2}\left(\partial\mathfrak{h}\right)^{2}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda^{2}\nabla^{r}\left(g_{0}^{-2}\left(\partial\mathfrak{h}\right)^{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}}
