\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\lambda^{2}\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\left\|\partial\nabla^{r_{1}}\mathfrak{h}\partial\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}}.

If
r_{1}\leq 2
, we put
\partial\nabla^{r_{1}}\mathfrak{h}
in
L^{\infty}
, i.e we use the product law of weighted Sobolev spaces from Proposition
2.1
:

\displaystyle\left\|\partial\nabla^{r_{1}}\mathfrak{h}\partial\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\partial\nabla^{r_{1}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+r_{1}+1}}\left\|\partial\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{1}+2}}\times\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{2}}}.

If
r_{1}\geq 3
, then
r_{2}\leq 2
(since
r\leq 4
) and we proceed similarly. Finally we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda^{2}\nabla^{r}\left(g_{0}^{-2}\left(\partial\mathfrak{h}\right)^{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}}.

∎

7.3
Estimates for
\mathfrak{F}

We start by studying
\mathfrak{F}
, which solves the transport equation (
5.4
). For clarity, we simply write
\mathfrak{h}
instead of
\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}
appearing in this equation.

Proposition 7.1
.

The following estimates hold

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim A_{3}\varepsilon^{2},

(7.9)

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 1,6\rrbracket}\lambda^{r-1}\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\lambda A_{1}\varepsilon+A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}\right),

(7.10)

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,5\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\partial_{t}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r+1}\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\lambda A_{1}\varepsilon+A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}\right).

(7.11)

Proof.

We want to apply Lemma
5.4
, so we need to estimate the
L^{2}
norm of the RHS of (
5.4
), which we rewrite

\displaystyle L_{0}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0})\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}+L_{0}^{\mu}\Gamma(g_{0})^{\nu}_{\mu(\alpha}\mathfrak{F}_{\nu\beta)}.

(7.12)

The RHS of (
7.12
) is supported in
\{|x|\leq C_{\mathrm{supp}}R\}
thanks to the support property of
\mathfrak{F}
and
F^{(1)}
. We estimate all the background quantities (see Theorem
6.1
) in
L^{\infty}
and obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\text{RHS of \eqref{eq F true}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\left(\varepsilon\left\|\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}+C(C_{0})\left\|\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}},

where we forgot about the projector
\Pi_{\leq}
and used (
7.1
). Therefore,
\mathfrak{F}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0
and Lemma
5.4
imply that for all
t\in[0,T]
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}(t)\lesssim C(C_{0})A^{2}_{3}\varepsilon^{4}+C(C_{0})\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

which gives (
7.9
) after applying Gronwall’s inequality.

We now turn to the proof of (
7.10
), for which we need to commute (
7.12
) with spatial derivatives. For
r\in\llbracket 1,6\rrbracket
we schematically obtain

\displaystyle L_{0}\left(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[L_{0},\nabla^{r}\right]\mathfrak{F}+\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\left(\nabla^{r_{1}}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)\nabla^{r_{2}}F^{(1)}+\nabla^{r_{1}}\left(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0}+L_{0}\Gamma(g_{0})\right)\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{F}\right).

(7.13)

From Lemma
5.4
, (
7.13
) and
\mathfrak{F}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0
we have for
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}(t)\lesssim C(C_{0})\int_{0}^{t}\left(\left\|\left[L_{0},\nabla^{r}\right]\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\sum_{r^{\prime}\leq r}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\left\|\nabla^{r^{\prime}}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla^{r^{\prime}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}s,

(7.14)

where we again estimate all the background quantities in
L^{\infty}
.

We estimate the commutator
\left[L_{0},\nabla^{r}\right]\mathfrak{F}
using the following formula, true for any linear operators:

\displaystyle[A,B^{n}]=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}B^{n-1-k}[A,B]B^{k}.

(7.15)

With the usual Leibniz rule this gives

\displaystyle\left|\left[L_{0},\nabla^{r}\right]\mathfrak{F}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left|\nabla^{r-1-k}\left((\nabla L_{0}^{\alpha})\partial_{\alpha}\nabla^{k}\mathfrak{F}\right)\right|\lesssim C(C_{0})\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left|\partial\nabla^{k}\mathfrak{F}\right|

(7.16)

where we used the background regularity.

We first treat the case
r=1
in (
7.14
). In order to treat the case of a time derivative in (
7.16
), we rewrite the equation satisfied by
\mathfrak{F}

\displaystyle L_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{F}=L_{0}^{i}\nabla\mathfrak{F}+\Pi_{\leq}(\mathfrak{h})F^{(1)}+(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0}+L_{0}\Gamma(g_{0}))\mathfrak{F}

(7.17)

which together with (
7.1
) gives
\left\|\partial\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+C(C_{0})A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}
. Therefore, using (
7.9
) and (
7.1
) we obtain from (
7.14
)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}(t)

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})A_{3}^{2}\varepsilon^{4}+C(C_{0})\int_{0}^{t}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\left\|\nabla\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})A_{3}^{2}\varepsilon^{4}+C(C_{0})\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

where we also forgot about
\Pi_{\leq}
and used (
BA3
). Using Gronwall’s lemma, this proves (
7.10
) in the case
r=1
. If now
r\geq 2
in (
7.14
), we have from (
BA1
)

\displaystyle\left\|\left[L_{0},\nabla^{r}\right]\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\sum_{k=0}^{r-2}\left\|\partial\nabla^{k}\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+C(C_{0})\left\|\partial\nabla^{r-1}\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim C(C_{0})\frac{A_{1}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{2(r-2)}}+C(C_{0})\left\|\partial\nabla^{r-1}\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

For the second term in this last expression we rewrite the equation for
\nabla^{r-1}
:

\displaystyle L_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}\nabla^{r-1}\mathfrak{F}

\displaystyle=\left[L_{0},\nabla^{r-1}\right]\mathfrak{F}+L_{0}^{i}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}

(7.18)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r-1}\left(\nabla^{r_{1}}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)\nabla^{r_{2}}F^{(1)}+\nabla^{r_{1}}\left(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0}+L_{0}\Gamma(g_{0})\right)\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{F}\right).

which thanks to (
7.16
), (
BA1
), (
BA3
) and (
C.12
) gives

\displaystyle\left\|\partial\nabla^{r-1}\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C(C_{0})\frac{A_{3}^{2}\varepsilon^{4}+A_{1}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{2(r-2)}}.

(7.19)

Therefore, we have estimated the commutator in (
7.14
):

\displaystyle\left\|\left[L_{0},\nabla^{r}\right]\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C(C_{0})\frac{\lambda^{2}A_{3}^{2}\varepsilon^{4}+\lambda^{2}A_{1}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}.

For the first sum in the time integral in (
7.14
), we distinguish between
r^{\prime}=r
and
r^{\prime}\leq r-1
and use (
C.12
) and (
BA3
) (and
r-1\leq 5
):

\displaystyle\sum_{r^{\prime}\leq r}\varepsilon^{2}\left\|\nabla^{r^{\prime}}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\right)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\nabla^{r-1}\mathfrak{h}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\sum_{r^{\prime}\leq r-1}\varepsilon^{2}\left\|\nabla^{r^{\prime}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{A_{3}^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}.

For the second sum in the time integral in (
7.14
), we simply use (
BA1
) if
r^{\prime}\leq r-1
. Putting everything together gives

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}(t)\lesssim C(C_{0})\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}A_{1}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}+\frac{A_{3}^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}{\lambda^{2(r-1)}}\right)+C(C_{0})\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

which gives (
7.10
) after applying Gronwall’s lemma. Now that (
7.10
) is proved, we notice that (
7.19
) implies the first part of (
7.11
). For the second part, we apply
\partial_{t}
to (
7.18
) and estimate the result with (
7.10
).
∎

7.4
Estimates for
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}

In this section, we derive the estimates for
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}
. This is the most crucial and intricate part of the proof, where we deal with the loss of derivatives exhibited in Section
3.2.2
. As explained in Section
3.2.3
, we start by using the high-frequency character of
g
and decompose
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}
as in

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}+\left(g^{\mu\nu}-g_{0}^{\mu\nu}\right)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}.

(7.20)

We are going to estimate separately the two terms in (
7.20
) in order to improve the bootstrap assumption (
BA2
).

•

The term
\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}
is estimated in Section
7.4.1
thanks to the transport equation it satisfies. The heart of the proof is the use of various commutator estimates in Lemmas
7.3
and
7.4
. We also benefit from
g-g_{0}=O\left(\lambda\right)
in Lemma
7.6
. The final result of this section is the content of Proposition
7.2
.

•

The term
\left(g^{\mu\nu}-g_{0}^{\mu\nu}\right)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}
is estimated in Section
7.4.2
. The heart of the proof is the
\lambda
gain from
g-g_{0}=O\left(\lambda\right)
, used in Proposition
7.3
. Section
7.4.2
is concluded by Proposition
7.4
with the final estimate on
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}
.

7.4.1
Estimates for
\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}

In this section, we prove by a finite strong induction argument on the value
r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

(7.21)

The proof of the induction step or the base case are similar. Therefore, we will prove (
7.21
) for a fixed value of
r
and assume that it holds for all value strictly less than
r
in
\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
. This induction assumption will only be used in the proof of Lemma
7.8
.

In order to estimate
\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}
, we start by deriving a transport equation from (
5.4
):

\displaystyle L_{0}(\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F})=\nabla^{r}[L_{0},\Box_{g_{0}}]\mathfrak{F}+[L_{0},\nabla^{r}]\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}+\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}L_{0}\mathfrak{F}.

(7.22)

We give names to the three terms in (
7.22
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{r}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\nabla^{r}[L_{0},\Box_{g_{0}}]\mathfrak{F},

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{r}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=[L_{0},\nabla^{r}]\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F},

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{r}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}L_{0}\mathfrak{F}.

These notations and the induction argument presented above will allow us to use the triangular structure of the equations

\displaystyle L_{0}(\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{0}+\mathbf{B}_{0}+\mathbf{C}_{0},

\displaystyle L_{0}(\nabla\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{1}+\mathbf{B}_{1}+\mathbf{C}_{1},

\displaystyle\;\,\vdots

\displaystyle L_{0}(\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{r}+\mathbf{B}_{r}+\mathbf{C}_{r}.

Indeed, during the proof of Lemma
7.8
, we will estimate
\mathbf{B}_{r}
in terms of the
\mathbf{A}_{k}
,
\mathbf{B}_{k}
and
\mathbf{C}_{k}
for
0\leq k\leq r-1
(see (
7.36
)).

We start by estimating
\mathbf{A}_{r}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{A}_{r}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon+A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}}+\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

We start with the case
r\geq 1
. We apply (
3.33
) to
\mathfrak{F}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}[L_{0},\Box_{g_{0}}]\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{r}\partial L_{0}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{H^{r}}+\left\|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{H^{r-1}}.

Thanks to (
BA1
) and
r\leq 4
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\partial\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{H^{r}}+\left\|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{H^{r-1}}\lesssim\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

Since the equation (
5.4
) schematically reads
L_{0}\mathfrak{F}=\mathfrak{F}+\mathfrak{h}F^{(1)}
we also have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\partial L_{0}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{r}\left(\left\|\partial\nabla^{k}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon\left\|\partial\nabla^{k}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon+A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}},

where we used (
BA1
), (
BA3
) and
r\leq 4
. This concludes the proof of the lemma in the case
r\geq 1
. The case
r=0
is treated similarly with (
3.32
) instead of (
3.33
).
∎

We now estimate
\mathbf{C}_{r}
. Using (
5.4
) we see that

\displaystyle|\mathbf{C}_{r}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)F^{(1)}\right|+\left|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right|+|\mathcal{Q}_{r}|,

(7.23)

where
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
contains the terms where at most
r+1
derivatives (out of the
r+2
involved in
\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}
) hit either
\mathfrak{F}
or
\mathfrak{h}
. In this case, the last derivative must therefore hit some background quantity. This implies that
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
contains only lower order terms and using the background regularity we easily obtain
\left\|\mathcal{Q}_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim A_{1}\varepsilon+A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}
and if
1\leq r\leq 4

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{Q}_{r}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r^{\prime}\leq r-1}\left(\left\|\partial^{2}\nabla^{r^{\prime}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon\left\|\partial^{2}\nabla^{r^{\prime}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon+A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}},

(7.24)

where the
\varepsilon
in front of
\left\|\partial^{2}\nabla^{r^{\prime}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}
comes from
F^{(1)}
and where we used (
BA1
), (
BA3
) and
r-1\leq 3
.

We now turn to the estimates of the first term in (
7.23
), i.e
\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)
. Forgetting about
\Pi_{\leq}
and
g_{0}
, this term is of the form
\Box\mathfrak{h}
, for which we would like to use (
5.5
). In order to do this, we need first to commute
\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}
and
\Pi_{\leq}
and second to replace
\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}
by
\tilde{\Box}_{g}
. This give the decomposition

\displaystyle\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{r}\left[\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}},\Pi_{\leq}\right]\mathfrak{h}+\nabla^{r}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}\right)-\nabla^{r}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\tilde{\Box}_{g-g_{0}}\mathfrak{h}\right).

(7.25)

The lemmas
7.4
,
7.5
and
7.6
below estimate the three different terms in the decomposition (
7.25
). Since
\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)
is multiplied by
F^{(1)}
in (
7.23
), giving an extra
\varepsilon
, we don’t need to be very precise in terms of the bootstrap constants
A_{i}
. For the same reason, the support property of
F^{(1)}
implies that we can forget about the weight at spacelike infinity for
\mathfrak{h}
or
g
(except in Lemma
7.5
below).

Lemma 7.4
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\left[\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}},\Pi_{\leq}\right]\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

Proof.

Thanks to
\left[\nabla^{r},\Pi_{\leq}\right]=0
and to the Leibniz rule we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\left[\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}},\Pi_{\leq}\right]\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\left\|\left[\nabla^{r_{1}}g_{0}\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}},\Pi_{\leq}\right]\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

We first study the case where
r_{2}\leq 3
. We use
\left[\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}},\Pi_{\leq}\right]=0
and (
C.14
)

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\nabla^{r_{1}}g_{0}\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}},\Pi_{\leq}\right]\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\left[\nabla^{r_{1}}g_{0},\Pi_{\leq}\right]\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}+1}g_{0}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\lambda\times\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}},

where we used the background regularity and (
BA3
) (with
r_{2}\leq 3
). If now
r_{2}=4
, then
r_{1}=0
and
r=4
. Since we can’t estimate
\partial^{2}\nabla^{4}\mathfrak{h}
, we need to gain one spatial derivative, and for this purpose we use (
3.37
) instead of (
C.14
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\left[g_{0}\partial^{2}\nabla^{4},\Pi_{\leq}\right]\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\left[g_{0},\Pi_{\leq}\right]\partial^{2}\nabla^{4}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial^{2}\nabla^{3}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{4}},

where we also used the background regularity and (
BA3
).
∎

In the next lemma, we estimate the term
\nabla^{r}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}\right)
in (
7.25
) and therefore we need to estimate the
L^{2}
norm of

\displaystyle\nabla^{r}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\text{RHS of \eqref{eq h}}\right).

(7.26)

In the forthcoming Section
7.5
we will improve (
BA3
) with the energy estimate for the wave operator. Therefore, we will apply
\nabla^{r}
to (
5.5
) and will need an estimate for the
weighted

L^{2}
norm of (
7.26
)
without
the projector
\Pi_{\leq}
(in addition to a commutator estimate, see Proposition
7.5
). This explains why in the next lemma we project ourself and first prove (
7.27
) and then get (
7.28
) as a consequence.

Lemma 7.5
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}}\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{2}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}},

(7.27)

which in particular implies

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(A_{i})\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

(7.28)

Proof.

We only need to prove (
7.27
). As explained above, we rely on the equation satisfied by
\mathfrak{h}
, that is (
5.5
). We have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\frac{\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}}{\lambda^{r_{2}}}+\lambda\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}g^{(3)}\right\|_{L^{2}}

(7.29)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda}\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\frac{\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}\Pi_{\geq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}}{\lambda^{r_{2}}}+\left\|\nabla^{r}\mathcal{R}(g)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}

\displaystyle=\vcentcolon I+II+III+IV,

where the
\varepsilon
in front of
\Pi_{\geq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)
comes from
F^{(1)}
. For
I
we simply use (
BA2
) to get

\displaystyle I\lesssim\frac{A_{2}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

(7.30)

For
II
, we first note that
g^{(3)}
is oscillating and therefore one looses potentially two powers of
\lambda
in
\tilde{\Box}_{g}g^{(3)}
. However this is not the case since if
\mathrm{T}
is a trigonometric function and
f
a scalar function then we have schematically

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\left(\lambda\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)f\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}g^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})f+g\partial f+\lambda\tilde{\Box}_{g}f

\displaystyle=g\partial f+\lambda\left(\tilde{\Box}_{g}f+gf\right),

(7.31)

where we used
g^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})=O\left(\lambda^{2}\right)
(see (
A.2
)) and did not write the trigonometric functions (see (
3.9
) for an exact computation). Therefore, we use the decomposition of Lemma
5.1
and obtain from (
7.31
) on the one hand

\displaystyle\lambda\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}g^{(3,\mathrm{BG})}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}},

and on the other hand

\displaystyle\lambda\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}g^{(3)}(\mathfrak{F})\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}g\partial\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\lambda\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}},

where for the sake of clarity we replaced each
g^{(3,\mathrm{T})}(\mathfrak{F})
by
\mathfrak{F}
(see (
5.15
)). We study the sum. If
r_{1}\leq 3
, then we use (
7.5
) and
\left\|g_{0}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
and (
BA1
) to write

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}g\partial\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{2}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r-1}}.

If
r_{1}=4
, then we use the decomposition (
7.2
) and write

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{4}g\partial\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{4}g_{\mathrm{BG}}\partial\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla^{4}\tilde{g}\partial\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{4}g_{\mathrm{BG}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|\partial\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla^{4}\tilde{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\partial\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{3}}+\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{4}},

where we used (
BA1
). Using in addition (
BA2
) we obtain

\displaystyle\lambda\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}g^{(3)}(\mathfrak{F})\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{2}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

We have proved that

\displaystyle II\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{2}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

(7.32)

For
III
, we use (
C.13
) to obtain

\displaystyle III

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\frac{\left\|\nabla\nabla^{r_{1}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}}{\lambda^{r_{2}}}\lesssim\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}},

(7.33)

where we used (
BA3
) and
r_{1}\leq 4
. For
IV
, we simply use (
7.7
). Together with (
7.30
), (
7.32
) and (
7.33
) this concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\Box_{g-g_{0}}\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(A_{i})\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}}.

Proof.

We first treat the case
r=0
. We forget about the projector
\Pi_{\leq}
and use (
7.5
) and (
BA3
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\Pi_{\leq}\left(\left(g-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|g-g_{0}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda A_{3}\varepsilon\times\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}.

If
r\geq 1
we use (
C.12
) to get rid of one derivative and the Leibniz rule to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\left(g-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\|\nabla^{r-1}\left(\left(g-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r-1}\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}\left(g-g_{0}\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\times\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{1}-1}}\times\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(A_{i})\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}},

where we again use (
7.5
) in addition to (
BA3
) and the fact that
r_{i}\leq 3
.
∎

Putting together Lemmas
7.4
,
7.5
and
7.6
and the estimate (
7.24
) we obtain

Lemma 7.7
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{C}_{r}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon+A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}}+\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

We now estimate
\mathbf{B}_{r}
.

Lemma 7.8
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{B}_{r}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon+C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}}+\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbf{B}_{0}=0
so we can assume
r\geq 1
. We first use (
7.16
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{B}_{r}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left\|\nabla^{k+1}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left\|\partial_{t}\nabla^{k}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}},

(7.34)

where we distinguished between time and spatial derivatives. For the first sum in (
7.34
), we note that if
k\leq r-2
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{k+1}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{k+1}\left\|\partial\nabla^{\ell}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}},

where we used (
BA1
). This gives

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left\|\nabla^{k+1}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

(7.35)

For the second sum in (
7.34
) we need to proceed differently since we didn’t include time derivative of
\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}
in the bootstrap assumptions. We use the equation satisfied by
\nabla^{k}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}
which schematically reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\nabla^{k}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}=\nabla^{k+1}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}+\mathbf{A}_{k}+\mathbf{B}_{k}+\mathbf{C}_{k}.

Therefore, if we sum these equalities from
0
to
r-1
and use (
7.35
) again we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{B}_{r}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}+\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left(\left\|\mathbf{A}_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\mathbf{B}_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\mathbf{C}_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right).

(7.36)

We can now use Lemmas
7.3
and
7.7
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left(\left\|\mathbf{A}_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\mathbf{C}_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left(\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon+A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{k}}+\left\|\nabla^{k}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon+C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r-1}},

where we used the induction assumption (
7.21
) for
\left\|\nabla^{k}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}
with
k\leq r-1
. Since we can assume
\lambda\leq\varepsilon
this shows that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left(\left\|\mathbf{A}_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\mathbf{C}_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}}.

Going back to (
7.36
) this gives

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{B}_{r}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon+C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}}+\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left\|\mathbf{B}_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

Thanks to an iterative argument, this shows that

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{B}_{r}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon+C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}},

where we again used the induction assumption (
7.21
) for
\left\|\nabla^{k}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}
with
k\leq r-1
and the fact that
\mathbf{B}_{0}=0
.
∎

Putting everything together, we finally obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 7.2
.

The following estimate holds

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{1}\varepsilon.

(7.37)

Proof.

We want to apply Lemma
5.4
to the equation (
7.22
). We estimate its RHS, by putting together Lemmas
7.3
,
7.8
and
7.7
:

\displaystyle\left\|\text{RHS of \eqref{eq box F}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

Lemma
5.4
then implies

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}(t)\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}(0)+\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s.

(7.38)

We estimate the initial data term in (
7.38
). Since
\mathfrak{F}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0
, the only cases we need to consider are
\partial_{t}\nabla^{r+1}\mathfrak{F}
and
\partial^{2}_{t}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}
. The initial data for such terms are obtained by differentiating (
5.28
). Using the background regularity this gives

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\nabla^{r+1}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}(0)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{r^{\prime}\leq r+1}\left\|\nabla^{r^{\prime}}\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}(0),

where the
\varepsilon
comes from
F^{(1)}
in front of
\Pi_{\leq}\left(\mathfrak{h}\right)
in (
5.28
). Using
r\leq 4
and (
BA3
) we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\nabla^{r+1}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}(0)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}}.

The proof is similar for
\partial^{2}_{t}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}
and we omit the details. Going back to (
7.38
) we have proved that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s.

Gronwall’s inequality then implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

This concludes the induction proving that (
7.21
) holds for all values of
r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
. Since
\Box_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}
and
\tilde{\Box}_{g_{0}}\mathfrak{F}
only differs by lower order terms, this also concludes the proof of (
7.37
).
∎

7.4.2
Estimates for
(g-g_{0})\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}

We now estimate the second term in (
7.20
).

Proposition 7.3
.

For
r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
we have

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\nabla^{r}\left(\left(g-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}.

(7.39)

Proof.

We start by noticing that (
BA1
) imply

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r+1}\left\|\partial^{2}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim A_{1}\varepsilon,

(7.40)

which in turn implies

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,2\rrbracket}\lambda^{r+3}\left\|\partial^{2}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim A_{1}\varepsilon,

(7.41)

with the usual
H^{2}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
embedding. To prove the proposition, we start with the Leibniz rule:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\left(\left(g-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}\left(g-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

If
r_{1}\leq 3
, then we use (
7.5
) and (
7.40
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}\left(g-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}\left(g-g_{0}\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{1}-1}}\times\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}},

where we also used the fact that
r_{2}\leq 4
.

If now
r_{1}=4
, we have
r=4
ans
r_{2}=0
. In this case we use the decomposition (
7.2
) to write

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{4}\left(g-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{4}\left(g_{\mathrm{BG}}-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla^{4}\tilde{g}\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(7.42)

For the first term in (
7.42
) we put the background quantities in
L^{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{4}\left(g_{\mathrm{BG}}-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{4}\left(g_{\mathrm{BG}}-g_{0}\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

(7.43)

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda^{3}}\times\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{4}},

where we used (
7.6
) and (
7.40
). For the second term in (
7.42
) we use (
7.4
) to decompose it further:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{4}\tilde{g}\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{2}\left\|\nabla^{4}\left(\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathfrak{F}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\lambda^{2}\left\|\nabla^{4}\mathfrak{h}\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\lambda^{3}\left\|\nabla^{4}g^{(3)}(\mathfrak{F})\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(7.44)

Using (
BA3
) and (
7.41
) we first obtain

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\left\|\nabla^{4}\mathfrak{h}\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{2}\left\|\nabla^{4}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

(7.45)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{2}\times\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{3}}\times\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{1}A_{3}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{4}}.

Now, by using the Leibniz rule on the product
\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathfrak{F}
and putting aside the case where 4 derivatives hit
\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
we obtain

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\left\|\nabla^{4}\left(\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathfrak{F}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\mathfrak{F}\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a+b=4\\
a\leq 3\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{a-2}}\left\|\nabla^{b}\mathfrak{F}\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

(7.46)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a+b=4\\
a\leq 3\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{a-2}}\left\|\nabla^{b}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\times\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda}\times\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a+b=4\\
a\leq 3\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{a-2}}\times\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{b-1}}\times\frac{A_{1}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{1}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{4}},

where we used (
7.40
) and (
7.41
). We treat the better term
\lambda^{3}\left\|\nabla^{4}g^{(3)}(\mathfrak{F})\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}
in (
7.44
) similarly. Putting (
7.42
), (
7.43
), (
7.45
) and (
7.46
) together gives

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{4}\left(g-g_{0}\right)\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(A_{i})\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{4}}.

This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Together with the decomposition (
7.20
), the results of Propositions
7.2
and
7.3
finally imply

Proposition 7.4
.

The following estimate holds

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{r}\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{1}\varepsilon.

7.5
Estimates for
\mathfrak{h}

Proposition 7.5
.

We have

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket}\lambda^{2r}E_{\delta+1+r}\left(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{1+C(A_{i})\varepsilon}\left(C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{4}+\left(C^{2}_{\mathrm{in}}+A_{2}^{2}\right)\varepsilon^{2}\right),

(7.47)

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\llbracket 0,3\rrbracket}\lambda^{r+1}\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{1+C(A_{i})\varepsilon}\left(C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+\left(C_{\mathrm{in}}+A_{2}\right)\varepsilon\right).

(7.48)

Proof.

We start with the proof of (
7.47
). We commute
\nabla^{r}
and
\tilde{\Box}_{g}
(with
r\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
) to obtain the equation satisfied by
\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}
from (
5.5
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=\left[\tilde{\Box}_{g},\nabla^{r}\right]\mathfrak{h}+\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}.

(7.49)

From the energy estimate in Lemma
5.5
we need to estimate the
L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}
norm of the RHS of (
7.49
). We estimate the commutator first. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\tilde{\Box}_{g},\nabla^{r}\right]\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r-1}\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}+1}g\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}.

We have
r_{1}\leq 3
. If
r_{1}\leq 2
, then mimicking the proof of Lemma
7.1
gives
\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}+1}g\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{r_{1}}}\lesssim\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{1}}}
which in turn implies

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}+1}g\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}+1}g\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{r_{1}}}\left\|\partial^{2}\nabla^{r_{2}}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+2+r_{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{1}}}\times\frac{A_{3}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(A_{i})\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}},

where we used (
BA3
). If
r_{1}=3
, then
r=4
and
r_{2}=0
we use the decomposition (
7.2
):

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{4}g\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+5}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{4}g_{\mathrm{BG}}\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+5}}+\left\|\nabla^{4}\tilde{g}\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+5}}.

For the first term, we put
\nabla^{4}g_{\mathrm{BG}}
in
L^{\infty}_{3}
as in the proof of Lemma
7.1
and use (
BA3
). For the second term, we do as in the proof of Proposition
7.3
, see (
7.45
) and (
7.46
). This proves that the commutator in (
7.49
) satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\tilde{\Box}_{g},\nabla^{r}\right]\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+1+r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(A_{i})\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{r}}.

(7.50)

We now estimate the second term in (
7.49
). This has already been done in Lemma
7.5
, we recall (
7.27
):

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{r}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}}\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{2}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

Together with (
7.50
) this implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\Box}_{g}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}}\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{2}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

(7.51)

We now apply Lemma
5.5
to (
7.49
) with
\sigma=\delta+r+1
. Thanks to (
7.5
), we have
\left\|\partial g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C(A_{i})\varepsilon
and Lemma
5.5
gives for
t\in[0,T]
(after multiplication by
\lambda^{2r}
)

\displaystyle\lambda^{2r}

\displaystyle E_{\delta+r+1}(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h})(t)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{2r}E_{\delta+r+1}(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h})(0)+(1+C(A_{i})\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{t}\lambda^{2r}E_{\delta+r+1}(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h})(s)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\lambda^{2r}\left\|\tilde{\Box}_{g}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+1}}(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{2r}E_{\delta+r+1}(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h})(0)+C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{4}+A_{2}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+(1+C(A_{i})\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{t}\lambda^{2r}E_{\delta+r+1}(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h})(s)\mathrm{d}s.

We apply Gronwall’s lemma to obtain

\displaystyle\lambda^{2r}E_{\delta+r+1}(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h})(t)\lesssim e^{1+C(A_{i})\varepsilon}\left(\lambda^{2r}E_{\delta+r+1}(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h})(0)+C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{4}+A_{2}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\right).

In order to estimate
E_{\delta+r+1}(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h})(0)
, we use (
5.40
):

\displaystyle\lambda^{2r}E_{\delta+r+1}(\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h})(0)\leq C_{\mathrm{in}}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}.

This concludes the proof of (
7.47
).

We now turn to the proof of (
7.48
). For this we write the operator
\tilde{\Box}_{g}
in the following schematic way:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}

\displaystyle=g^{tt}\partial_{t}^{2}+g\partial\nabla.

Thanks to the background regularity we can divide by
g^{tt}
and therefore obtain for
r\leq 3
:

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla^{r}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}g\nabla^{r_{2}}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+2}}+\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3}=r}\left\|\nabla^{r_{1}}g\nabla^{r_{2}}g\partial\nabla^{r_{3}+1}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+r+2}}

\displaystyle=\vcentcolon I+II.

For
I
, we use (
7.5
), the background regularity and (
7.27
):

\displaystyle I\lesssim\frac{C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+A_{2}\varepsilon}{\lambda^{r}}.

For
II
we proceed similarly but use (
7.47
) instead:

\displaystyle II\lesssim\frac{e^{1+C(A_{i})\varepsilon}\left(C(A_{i})\varepsilon^{2}+\left(C_{\mathrm{in}}+A_{2}\right)\varepsilon\right)}{\lambda^{r+1}}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

7.6
Conclusion of the bootstrap argument

Looking at Propositions
7.1
-
7.4
-
7.5
, we see that a choice of constants
A_{i}
such that
A_{1}\ll A_{2}\ll A_{3}
together with
\varepsilon
and
\lambda
small enough compared to 1 (but with
\varepsilon
still independent from
\lambda
) allows us to improve the bootstrap assumptions (
BA1
)-(
BA2
)-(
BA3
), as long as the time of existence of
(\mathfrak{F},\mathfrak{h})
is less than 1. This shows that the time of existence of the solution
(\mathfrak{F},\mathfrak{h})
is actually equal to 1. We have proved the following theorem.

Theorem 7.1
.

Given initial data as in Corollary
5.1
and if
\varepsilon
and
\lambda
are small enough (with
\varepsilon
independent from
\lambda
), there exists a solution
(\mathfrak{F},\mathfrak{h})
to the reduced system (
5.4
)-(
5.5
) on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Moreover there exists a numerical constant
C>0
such that (
BA1
)-(
BA2
)-(
BA3
) hold with the
A_{i}
replaced by
C
.

The estimates that
(\mathfrak{F},\mathfrak{h})
satisfied, along with the ones satisfied by
F^{(1)}
,
F^{(2,1)}
and
F^{(2,2)}
(see Theorem
6.1
) prove the main estimate (
2.26
) in Theorem
2.2
if one defines

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}=\mathfrak{h}+\lambda g^{(3)}.

It only remains to prove that
g
given by (
4.1
) solves the Einstein vacuum equations.

8
Propagation of polarization and gauge conditions

By proving Theorems
6.1
and
7.1
, we construct a metric
g
given by (
4.1
) solving both the background and the reduced systems on the manifold
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In this section, we conclude the proof of Theorem
2.2
by proving that
g
is actually a solution to the Einstein vacuum equations, i.e that
R_{\mu\nu}(g)=0
on the same manifold. We will show in Section
8.2
that
R_{\mu\nu}(g)
contains only polarization condition tensors
V^{(2,i)}
or the generalised wave gauge term
\Upsilon^{\rho}
. The fact that these terms vanish will be proved in Sections
8.3
using the contracted Bianchi identities, i.e the fact that the Einstein tensor of any Lorentzian metric is divergence free.

8.1
Algebraic properties of
F^{(1)}
and
\mathfrak{F}

In this section we prove that
F^{(1)}
satisfies (
2.27
) and (
2.28
). To this purpose, we define

\displaystyle\underline{\zeta}_{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}g(\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}\underline{L}_{0},e_{A}).

(8.1)

Thanks to the properties of the background null frame, we obtain the following decomposition of
\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}X
for
X
a vector field of the null frame:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}L_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

(8.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}\underline{L}_{0}

\displaystyle=2\underline{\zeta}_{A}e_{A},

(8.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}e_{A}

\displaystyle=\underline{\zeta}_{A}L_{0}+\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}e_{A},

(8.4)

where
\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}X
is the projection of
\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}X
onto
TP_{t,u}
. Note that the regularity of the background stated in Section
2.2
is enough to ensure that
\underline{\zeta}_{A}
as well as
g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}e_{A},e_{B})
are bounded on
\mathcal{M}
.

Remark 8.1
.

The notation
\underline{\zeta}
in (
8.1
) is standard when working with null frames and goes back to
[
CK93
]
. The equations (
8.2
), (
8.3
) and (
8.4
) are a subset of the structure equations of the null frame.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
T
be a symmetric 2-tensor.

(i)

If
\mathrm{Pol}\left(\mathscr{L}_{0}T\right)=0
, then
\mathrm{Pol}(T)
satisfies the following system

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}(T)

\displaystyle=0,

(8.5)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{A}(T)

\displaystyle=-2\underline{\zeta}_{A}\mathrm{Pol}_{L_{0}}(T)-2\delta^{BC}g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}e_{A},e_{B})\mathrm{Pol}_{C}(T),

(8.6)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}\mathrm{Pol}_{\underline{L}_{0}}(T)

\displaystyle=-4\delta^{AB}\underline{\zeta}_{A}\mathrm{Pol}_{B}(T).

(8.7)

(ii)

If
(\mathscr{L}_{0}T)_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}=0
, then
T_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}T_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}

\displaystyle=4\delta^{AB}\underline{\zeta}_{A}\mathrm{Pol}(T)_{B}.

(8.8)

(iii)

If
\mathscr{L}_{0}T=0
,
\mathrm{Pol}(T)=0
and
T_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}=0
, then
\mathcal{E}(T,T)
satisfies

\displaystyle(-L_{0}+\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0})\mathcal{E}(T,T)=0.

(8.9)

Proof.

Using (
8.2
) we have

\displaystyle L_{0}T_{L_{0}L_{0}}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}T)_{L_{0}L_{0}}+2T_{L_{0}\alpha}\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}L_{0}^{\alpha}=(\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}T)_{L_{0}L_{0}}.

Using in addition (
8.4
) we obtain

\displaystyle L_{0}T_{L_{0}A}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}T)_{L_{0}A}+T_{L_{0}\alpha}\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}e_{A}^{\alpha}+T_{A\alpha}\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}L_{0}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}T)_{L_{0}A}+\underline{\zeta}_{A}T_{L_{0}L_{0}}+\delta^{BC}g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}e_{A},e_{B})T_{L_{0}C}.

Similarly, we obtain

\displaystyle L_{0}\left(\delta^{AB}T_{AB}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta^{AB}(\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}T)_{AB}+\delta^{AB}T_{A\alpha}\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}e_{B}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\delta^{AB}(\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}T)_{AB}+2\delta^{AB}\underline{\zeta}_{A}T_{L_{0}B},

where we also use

\delta^{AB}\delta^{CD}T_{AC}g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}e_{B},e_{D})=0,

which holds because
T
is symmetric and
g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}\cdot,\cdot)
is antisymmetric. Recalling (
4.32
), this gives the following identities

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}T_{L_{0}L_{0}}

\displaystyle=(\mathscr{L}_{0}T)_{L_{0}L_{0}},

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}T_{L_{0}A}

\displaystyle=(\mathscr{L}_{0}T)_{L_{0}A}-2\underline{\zeta}_{A}T_{L_{0}L_{0}}-2\delta^{BC}g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}e_{A},e_{B})T_{L_{0}C},

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}\left(\delta^{AB}T_{AB}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta^{AB}(\mathscr{L}_{0}T)_{AB}-4\delta^{AB}\underline{\zeta}_{A}T_{BL_{0}}.

Now, thanks to (
3.6
), (
3.7
) and (
3.8
), the assumption
\mathrm{Pol}\left(\mathscr{L}_{0}T\right)=0
can be used to rewrite the previous system as (
8.5
), (
8.6
) and (
8.7
). Similarly, we can show that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}T_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}

\displaystyle=(\mathscr{L}_{0}T)_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}-4\delta^{AB}\underline{\zeta}_{A}T_{BL_{0}},

which becomes (
8.8
) after using the assumption
(\mathscr{L}_{0}T)_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}=0
. We now prove (
8.9
). First, note that the assumptions
\mathrm{Pol}(T)=0
and
T_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}=0
imply that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\left(T,T\right)

\displaystyle=-\left(\left(T_{e_{1}e_{1}}\right)^{2}+\left(T_{e_{1}e_{2}}\right)^{2}\right),

(8.10)

where we recall that
\mathcal{E}\left(T,T\right)
is defined in (
3.19
). We now use the additional assumption
\mathscr{L}_{0}T=0
, the null structure equation (
8.4
) and the fact that
g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}\cdot,\cdot)
is antisymmetric to derive a transport equation for the quantities involved in (
8.10
):

\displaystyle-L_{0}\left(T_{e_{1}e_{1}}\right)^{2}+(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0})\left(T_{e_{1}e_{1}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=-4T_{e_{1}e_{1}}T_{e_{1}e_{2}}g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}e_{1},e_{2}),

\displaystyle-L_{0}\left(T_{e_{1}e_{2}}\right)^{2}+(\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0})\left(T_{e_{1}e_{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=2T_{e_{1}e_{2}}T_{e_{1}e_{1}}g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}e_{1},e_{2})-2T_{e_{1}e_{2}}T_{e_{2}e_{2}}g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}e_{1},e_{2}).

Thanks to
T_{e_{2}e_{2}}=-T_{e_{1}e_{1}}
(consequence of
\mathrm{Pol}_{\underline{L}_{0}}(T)=0
) the RHS of the second equation becomes
4T_{e_{1}e_{2}}T_{e_{1}e_{1}}g_{0}(\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\,}_{L_{0}}e_{1},e_{2})
and we obtain (
8.9
)
∎

When applied to
F^{(1)}
and
\mathfrak{F}
, this lemma gives the following.

Corollary 8.1
.

The tensor
F^{(1)}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

(8.11)

\displaystyle F^{(1)}_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}

\displaystyle=0,

(8.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\left(F^{(1)},F^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle=-4F_{0}^{2}.

(8.13)

The tensor
\mathfrak{F}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pol}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)=0.

(8.14)

Proof.

Since
F^{(1)}
satisfies the transport equation (
5.1
),
\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(1)}\right)
and
F^{(1)}_{L_{0}\underline{L}_{0}}
satisfy the linear and homogeneous system (
8.5
)-(
8.6
)-(
8.7
)-(
8.8
). Since these quantities vanish on
\Sigma_{0}
(see Section
5.5.1
), we obtain (
8.11
) and (
8.12
). We can then use (
2.5
) and (
8.9
) and obtain

\displaystyle\left(-L_{0}+\Box_{g_{0}}u_{0}\right)\left(\mathcal{E}\left(F^{(1)},F^{(1)}\right)+4F_{0}^{2}\right)=0.

(8.15)

Moreover, (
5.44
) implies that
\mathcal{E}\left(F^{(1)},F^{(1)}\right)+4F_{0}^{2}
vanishes on
\Sigma_{0}
, equation (
8.15
) then implies (
8.13
). The proof of (
8.14
) is similar. Thanks to (
8.11
) and (
5.4
), we have
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathscr{L}_{0}\mathfrak{F})=0
. Therefore
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathfrak{F})
satisfies the linear and homogeneous system (
8.5
)-(
8.6
)-(
8.7
), while vanishing initially (since
\mathfrak{F}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0
), we thus obtain (
8.14
).
∎

Remark 8.2
.

This corollary shows the consistency of the computations of Section
4.2
, which were made under the assumptions that (
8.11
) and (
8.12
) hold, since (
8.11
) and (
8.12
) are equivalent to (
4.9
).

The transport equations for
F^{(2,i)}
, i.e (
5.2
) and (
5.3
), are not as simple as (
5.1
) and we can’t hope to compute
\mathrm{Pol}\left(\mathscr{L}_{0}F^{(2,i)}\right)
easily. Moreover, the polarization conditions for
F^{(2,i)}
are not simply
\mathrm{Pol}\left(F^{(2,i)}\right)=0
but rather involve non-linear expressions depending on
F^{(1)}
(see (
4.41
) and (
4.42
)). For these two reasons, the propagation of the second polarization, i.e the propagation of the polarization conditions satisfied by
F^{(2,i)}
, can’t be proved with Lemma
8.1
and is postponed to Section
8.3
.

8.2
The Einstein tensor and the contracted Bianchi identities

We compute the Ricci tensor of the solution
g
to the background and reduced systems. This is based on Proposition
4.4
, which provides the expression of the terms in the following expansion

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}+\lambda R^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}+\lambda^{2}R^{(\geq 2)}_{\mu\nu}.

(8.16)

Remark 8.3
.

In the sequel, we will encounter a lot of terms proportional to the polarization terms
V^{(2,j)}
for which a precise expression is useless. Therefore, we introduce the following practical notation: we denote by
O\left(V^{(2)}\right)
any combination of the tensors
V^{(2,j)}
or its derivatives for
j=1,2
. This notation extends to tensors, in the sense that
O^{\alpha}\left(V^{(2)}\right)
will denote any 1-tensor with coefficients of the form
O\left(V^{(2)}\right)
.

Proposition 8.1
.

The three terms in the expansion (
8.16
) of the Ricci tensor of the metric constructed in Theorems
6.1
and
7.1
are given by

\displaystyle R^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,1)}_{\beta)}-2\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,2)}_{\beta)},

\displaystyle R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}V^{(2,1)}_{\beta)}-\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}V^{(2,2)}_{\beta)}

\displaystyle\quad+O^{\rho}\left(V^{(2)}\right)F^{(1)}_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}u_{0}+O\left(V^{(2)}\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\Pi_{\geq}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}(g_{0})_{\rho\beta)}\partial_{\theta}(\Upsilon^{\rho})^{(0)},

\displaystyle R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\lambda}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\Pi_{\geq}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\Upsilon^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\beta}+g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}\Upsilon^{\rho}\right)^{(\geq 0)}.

We omit the proof of this proposition since it follows the same lines as the recipe presented in Section
4.3
. Note that the term proportional to
\frac{1}{\lambda}\Pi_{\geq}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\right)
in
R^{(\geq 2)}
cancels out with the same term present in
R^{(1)}
so that it disappears from the final Einstein tensor computed below. Also note that

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{2}\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}(g_{0})_{\rho\beta)}\partial_{\theta}(\Upsilon^{\rho})^{(0)}+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(\Upsilon^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\beta}+g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}\Upsilon^{\rho}\right)^{(\geq 0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(\Upsilon^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\beta}+g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}\Upsilon^{\rho}\right).

(8.17)

The first term on the LHS of (
8.17
) corresponds to the
\Upsilon
terms in
R^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
, while the second one corresponds to the
\Upsilon
terms in
R^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}
. Therefore the RHS of (
8.17
) completely describes the
\Upsilon
terms in the Ricci tensor.

As Proposition
8.1
shows, the Ricci tensor of
g
contains only gauge terms or the polarization conditions, i.e terms depending only on
\Upsilon
and
V^{(2,i)}
. In order to show that
g
is solution of the Einstein vacuum equations, it thus remains to show that they vanish. This is the content of Section
8.3
and it is proved using the contracted Bianchi identities, i.e the fact that the Einstein tensor of any metric is divergence free, which will give us extra equations satisfied by
V^{(2,i)}
and
\Upsilon
. Even though this is standard when working with the (generalised or not) wave coordinates, the high-frequency character of
g
(which manifests itself at this point by the presence of the polarization conditions tensors
V^{(2,i)}
) changes the situation. Schematically, we will show that the divergence of
G
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{g}G

\displaystyle=\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,i)}+\lambda^{2}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\Upsilon,

with
\mathrm{T}
a trigonometric function. The contracted Bianchi identities precisely state that for all
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{0}]
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,i)}+\lambda^{2}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\Upsilon=0,

(8.18)

with
\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,i)}
independent of
\lambda
and
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\Upsilon
depending on
\lambda
through
g
and
\Upsilon
. Our goal is to extract from (
8.18
) the two equations
\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,i)}=0
and
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\Upsilon=0
. However, because of the oscillating term in (
8.18
) and the fact that
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\Upsilon
depends on
\lambda
(since
\Upsilon
depends on
\mathfrak{h}
or
\mathfrak{F}
), we can’t simply consider the expression in (
8.18
) as a polynomial in
\lambda
vanishing on the interval
(0,\lambda_{0}]
. Instead we multiply (
8.18
) by
\mathrm{T}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
and use the fact that its weak limit in
L^{2}(K)
when
\lambda
tends to 0 is 0, where
K\vcentcolon=\{|x|\leq C_{\mathrm{supp}}R\}
is the maximal support of all the background perturbations, and in particular the support of
V^{(2,i)}
(this fact was justified in Section
2.2
). In order to consider the limit
\lambda
tends to 0 for
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\Upsilon
we need precise estimates obtained in Lemma
8.2
and which follow from our bootstrap assumptions for
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{F}
.

We define the Einstein tensor of
g
as

\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=R_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\alpha\beta},

(8.19)

where
R=g^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}
is the scalar curvature of
g
. The terms
V^{(2,i)}
and
\Upsilon
will be treated differently so it is useful to decompose
G
in the following way:

\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=G\left(V\right)_{\alpha\beta}+G\left(\Upsilon\right)_{\alpha\beta},

(8.20)

with

\displaystyle G\left(\Upsilon\right)_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}\Upsilon^{\rho}-g_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\rho}\Upsilon^{\rho}+\Upsilon^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\Upsilon^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\mu\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\right).

(8.21)

and
G\left(V\right)
depending only on
V^{(2,i)}
. Thanks to Proposition
8.1
, the tensor
G\left(V\right)
admits a high-frequency expansion

\displaystyle G\left(V\right)_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=G^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda G^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+O\left(\lambda^{2}\right),

where

\displaystyle G^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,1)}_{\beta)}+V^{(2,1)}_{L_{0}}(g_{0})_{\alpha\beta}\right)

(8.22)

\displaystyle\quad-2\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,2)}_{\beta)}+V^{(2,2)}_{L_{0}}(g_{0})_{\alpha\beta}\right),

\displaystyle G^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}V^{(2,1)}_{\beta)}-\mathrm{div}_{g_{0}}V^{(2,1)}(g_{0})_{\alpha\beta}\right)

(8.23)

\displaystyle\quad-\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}V^{(2,2)}_{\beta)}-\mathrm{div}_{g_{0}}V^{(2,2)}(g_{0})_{\alpha\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+O^{\rho}\left(V^{(2)}\right)F^{(1)}_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}u_{0}+O\left(V^{(2)}\right)F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}.

The (contracted) Bianchi identities precisely reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{g}G

\displaystyle=0.

(8.24)

Thanks to (
8.20
), (
8.24
) reads
\mathrm{div}_{g}G(\Upsilon)+\mathrm{div}_{g}G(V)=0
. These two terms are studied in Lemmas
8.2
and
8.3
respectively. In the proof of Lemma
8.2
, we only consider the
\partial\mathfrak{h}
and
\partial\mathfrak{F}
terms in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
(recall (
4.27
)) since they satisfy the worst estimates. Moreover, since we only care about
L^{2}(K)
norms with
K
compact we can neglect the weights for
\mathfrak{h}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{div}_{g}G\left(\Upsilon\right)_{\alpha}\right\|_{L^{2}(K)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda.

Proof.

The expression in coordinates

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{g}G\left(\Upsilon\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}G\left(\Upsilon\right)_{\nu\alpha}-g^{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu(\alpha}G\left(\Upsilon\right)_{\rho\nu)},

and a standard computation first give

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{g}G\left(\Upsilon\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(g_{\rho\alpha}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\Upsilon^{\rho}+\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}\right),

(8.25)

with

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{(\alpha}\Upsilon^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}g_{\rho\nu)}-g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}\Upsilon^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}g_{\nu\alpha}+g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\left(\Upsilon^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\rho\sigma}\Upsilon^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}g_{\rho\sigma}g_{\alpha\nu}\right)-g^{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu(\alpha}G\left(\Upsilon\right)_{\rho\nu)}.

We use the notation
L^{2}=L^{2}(K)
for clarity. We start with
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}
. Recall that
g
and
\partial g
are bounded in
L^{\infty}
irrespective of
\lambda
. Moreover,
\partial\mathfrak{h}
and
\partial\mathfrak{F}
are bounded in
L^{2}
irrespective of
\lambda
thanks to (
BA1
) and (
BA3
). Therefore, the only terms potentially losing one power of
\lambda
in
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}
are
\partial\Upsilon
and
\partial^{2}g
. Indeed thanks to (
4.1
), (
BA1
) and (
BA3
) these terms are bounded by
\frac{1}{\lambda}
in
L^{2}
, which shows that

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}.

(8.26)

Let us now look at
\tilde{\Box}_{g}\Upsilon^{\rho}
. We only focus on the main terms in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
, that is
\partial\mathfrak{h}+\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial\mathfrak{F}
. For
\partial\mathfrak{h}
, we use the equation (
5.5
) and commute it with one derivative to obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\partial\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=\partial\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}+[\tilde{\Box}_{g},\partial]\mathfrak{h}.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|[\tilde{\Box}_{g},\partial]\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\partial g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda},

where we used (
BA3
). We now use the equation (
5.5
) (again, we only write down the terms depending on
\mathfrak{F}
with the highest derivative):

\displaystyle\left\|\partial\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\|\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}+\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}},

where we used (
BA2
). This estimate can’t directly be used to bound
\partial_{t}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}
, instead we use (
7.17
):

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|[\partial_{t},\tilde{\Box}_{g}]\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\tilde{\Box}_{g}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\tilde{\Box}_{g}\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}.

We proved that

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\Box}_{g}\partial\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}.

(8.27)

For the
\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial\mathfrak{F}
term in
\Upsilon^{\rho}
we use (
3.9
) to deduce that we need to estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathscr{L}_{0}\partial\mathfrak{F}+\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(\tilde{\Box}_{g}\partial\mathfrak{F}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}g^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})\partial\mathfrak{F}\right).

First, using (
A.2
), (
BA1
) and (
BA2
) we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\Box}_{g}\partial\mathfrak{F}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}g^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})\partial\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}.

Moreover, using (
5.4
) commuted with one derivative we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathscr{L}_{0}\partial\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|[\mathscr{L}_{0},\partial]\mathfrak{F}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial\mathfrak{h}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1,

where we used (
BA1
) and (
BA3
). We proved that

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\Box}_{g}\left(\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial\mathfrak{F}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}.

(8.28)

Together with (
8.27
), (
8.28
) proves that
\left\|\tilde{\Box}_{g}\Upsilon^{\rho}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{g}G\left(V\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathcal{B}(V)^{(-1)}_{\alpha}+\mathcal{B}(V)^{(0)}_{\alpha}+O\left(\lambda\right),

(8.29)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(V)^{(-1)}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0,

(8.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(V)^{(0)}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,1)}_{\alpha}+g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,1)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{[\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha]}\right)

(8.31)

\displaystyle\quad+2\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,2)}_{\alpha}+g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,2)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{[\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha]}\right).

Proof.

The
\frac{1}{\lambda}
terms in
\mathrm{div}_{g}G\left(V\right)_{\alpha}
comes from the differentiation of the oscillating parts of
G^{(0)}_{\nu\alpha}
, i.e

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(V)^{(-1)}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial^{\nu}u_{0}\left(\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,1)}_{\nu)}+V^{(2,1)}_{L_{0}}(g_{0})_{\alpha\nu}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+4\sin\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial^{\nu}u_{0}\left(\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,2)}_{\nu)}+V^{(2,2)}_{L_{0}}(g_{0})_{\alpha\nu}\right),

where we recall (
8.22
). If
i=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle\partial^{\nu}u_{0}\left(\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,i)}_{\nu)}+V^{(2,i)}_{L_{0}}(g_{0})_{\alpha\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\nu}u_{0}V^{(2,i)}_{L_{0}}+V^{(2,i)}_{L_{0}}\partial_{\nu}u_{0}=0,

which concludes the proof of (
8.30
). Now for (
8.31
), from the definition (
8.29
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{g}G\left(V\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}G(V)_{\nu\alpha}-g^{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}G(V)_{\rho\alpha}-g^{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\alpha}G(V)_{\rho\nu},

(8.32)

where
\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}
denotes the Christoffel symbols associated to
g
computed in (
4.7
). Note that
F^{(1)}_{L_{0}\alpha}=0
and
\mathrm{tr}_{g_{0}}F^{(1)}=0
imply
g_{0}^{\mu\nu}(\tilde{\Gamma}^{(0)})^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}=0
(see (
4.8
)). This gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(V)_{\alpha}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}u_{0}\partial_{\theta}G_{\nu\alpha}^{(1)}+g_{0}^{\mu\nu}\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{\mu}G^{(0)}_{\nu\alpha}-g_{0}^{\mu\nu}(\tilde{\Gamma}^{(0)})^{\rho}_{\mu\alpha}G^{(0)}_{\rho\nu},

(8.33)

where in
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{\mu}G^{(0)}
we don’t differentiate the oscillating functions, as for the corresponding notation
\tilde{\partial}
. We start by looking at the term depending on
G^{(1)}
, whose expression is given by (
8.23
). Thanks to
F^{(1)}_{L_{0}\alpha}=0
the terms proportional to
O\left(V^{(2)}\right)
or
O^{\rho}\left(V^{(2)}\right)
in (
8.23
) don’t contribute to
\mathcal{B}(V)_{\alpha}^{(0)}
. We are left with

\displaystyle g_{0}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}u_{0}\partial_{\theta}G_{\nu\alpha}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(L_{0}^{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}V^{(2,1)}_{\nu)}-\mathrm{div}_{g_{0}}V^{(2,1)}(L_{0})_{\alpha}\right)

(8.34)

\displaystyle\quad+2\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(L_{0}^{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{(\alpha}V^{(2,2)}_{\nu)}-\mathrm{div}_{g_{0}}V^{(2,2)}(L_{0})_{\alpha}\right).

Let us now look at the third term in (
8.33
). Let
i=1,2
, we expand using (
4.8
):

\displaystyle(g_{0})^{\mu\nu}(\tilde{\Gamma}^{(0)})^{\rho}_{\mu\alpha}

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{(\rho}u_{0}V^{(2,i)}_{\nu)}+V^{(2,i)}_{L_{0}}(g_{0})_{\rho\nu}\right)

(8.35)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)g_{0}^{\mu\nu}g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\left(\partial_{(\mu}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\sigma\alpha)}-\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\mu\alpha}\right)\partial_{(\rho}u_{0}V^{(2,i)}_{\nu)}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)g_{0}^{\mu\nu}g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\left(\partial_{(\mu}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\sigma\alpha)}-\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\mu\alpha}\right)V^{(2,i)}_{L_{0}}(g_{0})_{\rho\nu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)g_{0}^{\mu\nu}g_{0}^{\rho\sigma}\left(\partial_{(\mu}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\sigma\alpha)}-\partial_{\sigma}u_{0}F^{(1)}_{\mu\alpha}\right)\partial_{\nu}u_{0}V^{(2,i)}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=0.

Therefore the third term in (
8.33
) vanishes. Finally, let us look at the second term in this expression. From
\mathbf{D}g_{0}=0
we obtain

\displaystyle g_{0}^{\mu\nu}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}G^{(0)}_{\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,1)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha}-V^{(2,1)}_{\alpha}\mathrm{div}_{g_{0}}L_{0}\right.

(8.36)

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-(L_{0})_{\alpha}\mathrm{div}_{g_{0}}V^{(2,1)}-\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}V^{(2,1)}_{\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}V^{(2,1)}_{L_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-2\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,2)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha}-V^{(2,2)}_{\alpha}\mathrm{div}_{g_{0}}L_{0}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-(L_{0})_{\alpha}\mathrm{div}_{g_{0}}V^{(2,2)}-\mathbf{D}_{L_{0}}V^{(2,1)}_{\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}V^{(2,2)}_{L_{0}}\right).

Adding this to (
8.34
) concludes the proof.
∎

8.3
Conclusion

Before proving the propagation of the second polarization, we first prove that
V_{\underline{L}_{0}}^{(2,i)}=0
on
\Sigma_{0}
. Recall that the tangential components of
V^{(2,i)}
already vanish on
\Sigma_{0}
thanks to our choice of initial data for
F^{(2,i)}
, see (
5.42
). Since
V_{\underline{L}_{0}}^{(2,i)}
only involves the projection of the metric on
\Sigma_{0}
(see (
3.8
)),
V_{\underline{L}_{0}}^{(2,i)}=0
can’t be ensured by well-chosen initial data, it has to be directly satisfied by the solution of the constraint equations. This means that we could have performed this computation in
[
Tou23b
]
. However, it seems more consistent with the splitting between
[
Tou23b
]
and the present article to study
V_{\underline{L}_{0}}^{(2,i)}
here. Moreover, we will benefit from the reduced form of the Einstein tensor obtained above, and the fact that the constraint equations are solved on the initial hypersurface.

Lemma 8.4
.

We have

\displaystyle V^{(2,i)}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0.

Proof.

Thanks to (
5.23
), it remains to show that
V^{(2,i)}_{\underline{L}_{0}}=0
on
\Sigma_{0}
. In fact, it is enough to show that

\displaystyle V^{(2,i)}_{0}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0.

(8.37)

Indeed, if (
8.37
) holds then
V^{(2,i)}_{\underline{L}_{0}}=-V^{(2,i)}_{L_{0}}
(where we used that
L_{0}=|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}(\partial_{t}+N_{0})
and
\underline{L}_{0}=|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}(\partial_{t}-N_{0})
on
\Sigma_{0}
) and (
5.23
) then gives the result. Therefore, let us prove (
8.37
). First, (
8.22
) and (
5.23
) imply that on
\Sigma_{0}

\displaystyle G(V)_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,1)}_{\beta)}-2\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,2)}_{\beta)}+O\left(\lambda\right).

Moreover,
\Upsilon^{\rho}
contains oscillating terms (see (
4.27
)) and also terms depending on
\lambda
like
\mathfrak{h}
or
\mathfrak{F}
. More precisely, if we only write the top derivatives of the non-background terms we have

\displaystyle|G(\Upsilon)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda|\partial\mathfrak{F}|+\lambda^{2}\left(|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{F}|+|\partial^{2}\mathfrak{h}|\right).

Estimates (
BA1
) and (
BA3
) then imply that
G(\Upsilon)
is
O\left(\lambda\right)
in
L^{2}(K)
. Conclusion, we have

\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,1)}_{\beta)}-2\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\partial_{(\alpha}u_{0}V^{(2,2)}_{\beta)}+O\left(\lambda\right),

(8.38)

where the
O\left(\lambda\right)
has to be understood in
L^{2}(K)
. Now, Corollary
5.1
ensures that the constraint equations are satisfied on
\Sigma_{0}
, which in particular gives
G_{TT}=0
on
\Sigma_{0}
, where
T
is the unit normal to
\Sigma_{0}
for
g
. Thanks to Lemma
5.2
, we have
T=\partial_{t}+O\left(\lambda^{2}\right)
so the previous identity implies that
G_{00}=O\left(\lambda^{2}\right)
on
\Sigma_{0}
. Thanks to (
8.38
) and (
2.12
) this rewrites as

\displaystyle-\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}V^{(2,1)}_{0}-4\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}V^{(2,2)}_{0}=O\left(\lambda\right).

(8.39)

We multiply this identity by
\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
and take the weak limit in
L^{2}(K)
when
\lambda
tends to 0. Moreover, the weak limit of
\sin^{2}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
and
\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
are respectively
\frac{1}{2}
and 0, and
V^{(2,1)}_{0}
does not depend on
\lambda
so we obtain
V^{(2,1)}_{0}=0
. Then (
8.39
) becomes

\displaystyle\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)|\nabla u_{0}|_{\bar{g}_{0}}V^{(2,2)}_{0}=O\left(\lambda\right).

(8.40)

Multiplying this identity by
\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
and taking the weak limit in
L^{2}(K)
when
\lambda
tends to 0 gives similarly that
V^{(2,2)}_{0}=0
. This proves (
8.37
) and concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We can now prove the propagation of the second polarization.

Proposition 8.2
.

The following holds on the whole spacetime

\displaystyle V^{(2,i)}=0,

(8.41)

for
i=1,2
.

Proof.

We look at the quantity
\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{div}_{g}G_{\alpha}
, which thanks to Lemma
8.3
rewrites

\displaystyle\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{div}_{g}G_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,1)}_{\alpha}+g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,1)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{[\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha]}\right)

(8.42)

\displaystyle\quad+2\sin\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,2)}_{\alpha}+g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,2)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{[\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+O\left(\lambda\right),

where we use the Bianchi identities (
8.24
), Lemma
8.2
and where the
O\left(\lambda\right)
has to be understood in
L^{2}(K)
. The quantity

\displaystyle-\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,1)}_{\alpha}+g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,1)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{[\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha]},

only involves the background perturbations so it doesn’t depend on
\lambda
. Therefore the weak limit when
\lambda
tends to 0 of the first two lines in (
8.42
) is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\left(-\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,1)}_{\alpha}+g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,1)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{[\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha]}\right).

(8.43)

Thanks to (
8.24
), the weak limit of (
8.42
) is zero and we obtain the following equation for
V^{(2,1)}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,1)}_{\alpha}=g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,1)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{[\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha]}.

(8.44)

If we now multiply
\mathrm{div}_{g}G_{\alpha}
by
\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
, Lemma
8.3
and (
8.44
) imply

\displaystyle\cos\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{div}_{g}G_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=2\cos^{2}\left(\frac{2u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\left(-\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,2)}_{\alpha}+g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,2)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{[\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha]}\right)+O\left(\lambda\right).

We again take the weak limit in
L^{2}
when
\lambda
tends to 0 and thanks to (
8.24
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}V^{(2,2)}_{\alpha}=g_{0}^{\mu\nu}V^{(2,2)}_{\nu}\mathbf{D}_{[\mu}(L_{0})_{\alpha]}.

(8.45)

Equations (
8.44
) and (
8.45
) are transport equations for the quantities
V^{(2,i)}
,
i=1,2
. Thanks to Lemma
8.4
we know that

\displaystyle V^{(2,i)}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0,

(8.46)

for
i=1,2
. Together with the energy estimate for the transport operator
L_{0}
(see Lemma
5.4
) and Gronwall’s inequality, (
8.45
) and (
8.46
) lead to (
8.41
).

∎

It remains to prove that
\Upsilon^{\rho}=0
. The Einstein tensor of
g
now reduces to
G\left(\Upsilon\right)
, i.e

\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}\Upsilon^{\rho}-g_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\rho}\Upsilon^{\rho}+\Upsilon^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\Upsilon^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{\mu\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\right).

(8.47)

At this point, it is standard to deduce from the Bianchi identities (
8.24
) the fact that
\Upsilon^{\rho}
vanishes if it vanishes initially, we redo the proof for completeness.

Proposition 8.3
.

The following holds on the whole spacetime

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\rho}=0.

Proof.

The main ingredient is already contained in (
8.25
). Since
V^{(2,i)}=0
, we have
G_{\alpha\beta}=G\left(\Upsilon\right)_{\alpha\beta}
and (
8.25
) together with (
8.24
) then implies

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}\Upsilon^{\rho}+A^{\mu\rho}_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Upsilon^{\nu}+B^{\rho}_{\alpha}\Upsilon^{\alpha}=0.

(8.48)

Therefore,
\Upsilon
solves (
8.48
), i.e a system of linear wave equations on the spacetime. If
\Upsilon^{\rho}
and
T\Upsilon^{\rho}
vanish on
\Sigma_{0}
, then
\Upsilon^{\rho}
vanishes on the whole spacetime (recall that
T
is the unit normal to
\Sigma_{0}
for
g
, see Lemma
5.2
). From Corollary
5.1
and our choice of initial data for
g
, we deduce what we need.

•

From Corollary
5.1
we have
\Upsilon^{\rho}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0
. Thanks to (
8.47
) this also implies that on
\Sigma_{0}
we have

\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(g_{\rho(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}\Upsilon^{\rho}-g_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{t}\Upsilon^{0}\right).

(8.49)

•

Corollary
5.1
also states that the constraint equations are solved on
\Sigma_{0}
, which reads
G_{TT}=0
and
G_{Ti}=0
. Since
T=(1+Z^{t})\partial_{t}+\bar{Z}^{i}\partial_{i}
and
g(T,\partial_{i})=0
the previous components of the Einstein tensor rewrite

\displaystyle G_{TT}

\displaystyle=2T^{\alpha}g_{\rho\alpha}T\Upsilon^{\rho}+\partial_{t}\Upsilon^{0}=-\frac{T\Upsilon^{0}}{1+Z^{t}},

and

\displaystyle G_{Ti}

\displaystyle=g_{0i}T\Upsilon^{0}+\bar{g}_{ij}T\Upsilon^{j},

where we used (
8.49
) and
\Upsilon^{\rho}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0
to cancel any spatial derivative of
\Upsilon^{\rho}
. Since
Z^{t}=O\left(\lambda^{4}\right)
(see (
5.30
)) the constraint equations indeed imply that
T\Upsilon^{\rho}\restriction{\Sigma_{0}}=0
.

∎

Propositions
8.2
and
8.3
concludes the proof of Theorem
2.2
, by showing that the metric
g
solves the Einstein vacuum equations.

Appendix A
Computations of Section
4

A.1
Proof of Proposition
4.1

In this section, we derive the expressions of the coefficients in the expansion of the wave part of the Ricci tensor:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Box}_{g}g_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=W^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda W^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda^{2}W^{(\geq 2)}_{\alpha\beta}.

(A.1)

The main ingredient is the computation (
3.9
), applied to each oscillating term in (
4.1
). We also need to expand the eikonal term
g_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})
,
g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}u_{0}\partial_{\nu}f
and
\tilde{\Box}_{g}f
(for various
f
), all appearing in (
3.9
). For that, we use the expansion of the inverse given in (
4.4
)-(
4.6
) and (
4.9
). This gives

\displaystyle g^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}u_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{0})-\lambda F^{(1)}_{L_{0}L_{0}}\cos\left(\frac{u_{0}}{\lambda}\right)
